## IX A Hostel Frolic / 第九章 宿舍联欢


===Original===
“The Foursome League,” which Verity had instituted with her roommates at the hostel, was kept by them as a solemn compact. They stuck to one another nobly, though often in the teeth of great inconvenience. It generally took three of them to urge Fil through her toilet in the mornings and drag her down to breakfast in time. She was always so terribly sleepy at seven o’clock, and so positive that she could whisk through her dressing in ten minutes, and that it was quite unnecessary to get up so soon: even when the others mercilessly pulled the bedclothes from her, and pointed to their watches, she would dawdle instead of “whisking,” and spend much superfluous time over manicure or dabbing on cucumber cream to improve her complexion. She was so innocent about her little vanities, and conducted them with such childlike complacency, that the girls tolerated them quite good humoredly, and even assisted sometimes. One of them generally volunteered to brush her long flaxen hair, and tie her ribbon, and half out of habit the others would tidy her cubicle, which was apt to be chaotic, and put her things away in her drawers. They did it almost automatically, for they had come to look upon Fil somewhat in the light of a big doll, the exclusive property of “The Foursome League,” and to be treated as the mascot of the dormitory.
Mrs. Best, the hostel matron, was what the girls called “rather an old dear.” Her gray hair was picturesque, and the knowledge that she had lost her husband and a son in the war added an element of pathetic interest to her personality. She was experienced in the ways of girls, and contrived to keep order without seeming to be constantly obtruding rules. Among her various sane practices she instituted the plan of awarding marks for good conduct and order to each dormitory, and allowing the one which scored the highest to give an entertainment to the others during the last hour before bedtime on Thursday night. Naturally this was a privilege to be desired. It was fun to act variety artistes before the rest of the hostel, and well worth being in time for meals, preserving silence during prep., or getting up a little earlier so as to leave cubicles in apple-pie order. The Foursome League had not yet earned distinction, chiefly owing to lapses on the part of Fil, and Nora’s incorrigible love of talking in season and out of season. One week, however, after a really heroic series of efforts, they succeeded in establishing a record, and sat perking themselves at dinnertime when Mrs. Best read out the score.
“We’ve not had you on the boards before,” said Susie Wakefield, one of the Sixth, as the girls filed from the room when the meal was over; “we’re all expecting something extra tiptop and thrillsome, so play up!”
“Hope we shan’t let you down!” replied Ingred. “Please don’t expect too much, or you mayn’t get it!”
===Chinese===
薇丽蒂（Verity）和室友们在宿舍里创立的“四人同盟”（The Foursome League），被她们当作一桩庄严盟约恪守着。几个姑娘彼此扶持，义气十足，哪怕常常要为此吃天大的苦头。每天早上，总得三个人合力，才能催着菲尔（Fil）完成晨间梳洗，及时把她拽下楼吃早饭。七点钟的她总是困得死去活来，还一口咬定自己十分钟能穿戴利索，压根儿犯不着起这么早：哪怕别人毫不留情地掀掉她的被褥、把手表举到她眼前，她也照样磨磨蹭蹭，全无半点“利索”的影子，反倒把大把多余的工夫花在修指甲、轻拍黄瓜霜润肤养颜上。好在她对这些小小虚荣全然天真，做起来又一脸孩子气的自得，姑娘们便好脾气地由她去，有时甚至还搭把手。通常会有人自告奋勇替她梳那头亚麻色的长发、系好发带，其余的人则半出于习惯，替她收拾那个动不动就乱作一团的小隔间，把东西归置进抽屉。她们做来几乎是全凭本能，因为大伙儿早把菲尔看成一个大娃娃——“四人同盟”的专属财产，一个要当寝室吉祥物供着宠着的宝贝。
舍监贝斯特太太（Mrs. Best），用姑娘们的话说，是位“顶可亲的老宝贝”。她那头灰发别有风韵，而她在战争中失去了丈夫和一个儿子，这又给她的为人平添了几分惹人怜惜的意味。她深谙女孩子家的心性，把宿舍管得井井有条，却从不显得条条框框压人。在她种种明智的章程之中，有一项是给各寝室的品行与内务记分，得分最高的寝室，可以在星期四晚上就寝前的最后一个钟头，给其余寝室办一场余兴节目。这自然是人人心向往之的殊荣。能在全宿舍面前扮一回杂耍艺人，实在有趣得很，为此吃饭准时、自习保持安静、或者早起片刻好把小隔间收拾得井井有条，都大值得。只可惜“四人同盟”至今没挣到过这份荣耀，一来怪菲尔屡屡失分，二来怪诺拉（Nora）那张嘴，不分场合想聊就聊，无可救药。不过有一个星期，经过一番真正称得上悲壮的努力，她们终于创下了纪录；午饭时贝斯特太太宣读分数，四个人坐在桌边，个个神气活现。
“以前可从没见你们登过台，”饭毕姑娘们鱼贯走出饭厅时，六年级的苏西·韦克菲尔德（Susie Wakefield）说道，“我们都巴望着一台顶呱呱、够刺激的好戏呢，加油吧！”
“但愿别让你们失望！”英格丽德（Ingred）应道，“可千万别期望太高，不然怕是要落空！”

===Original===
Dormitory 2 held a hurried conclave before afternoon school.
“It’s a great stunt!” rejoiced Nora.
“What *are* we to act?” fluttered Fil.
“Especially when we’ve to play up!” twittered Verity.
“What silly idiots we were not to plan it all out beforehand! But I really never dreamt we’d ever get the chance!”
===Chinese===
下午上课前，二号寝室匆匆开了个秘密会议。
“这可是桩了不得的大事！”诺拉喜不自胜。
“我们*到底*要演什么呢？”菲尔慌慌张张地问。
“还偏偏非得演好不可！”薇丽蒂尖声附和。
“咱们真是傻透了，居然没提前把节目全盘筹划好！我可压根儿没想到还能有这机会！”

===Original===
“No more did I,” said Ingred, sitting with her head in her hands, considering. “On the whole, it doesn’t matter. Sometimes a quite impromptu thing goes off best. It’s largely a question of what costumes we can rake up out of nothing.
“The cleverer those are, the more we’ll get applauded. I’ve one or two ideas simmering. Thank goodness it’s drawing this afternoon, and I shall have time to think them over.”
“We’ll all think!” agreed Verity. “Then we’ll compare notes at four o’clock, and fix on what we’re going to do. Great Minerva! It’ll be a hectic evening! I’m shivering in my shoes!”
“And I’m absolutely green with stage-fright! What a life!” proclaimed Fil.
===Chinese===
“我也没想到，”英格丽德双手托着头坐着，思忖道，“不过总的来说，不要紧。有时候全然即兴的玩意儿反而收效最佳。关键就看咱们能凭空凑出些什么行头。
“行头越巧妙，掌声就越热烈。我心里已经有一两个点子在冒泡了。谢天谢地，下午是绘画课，我正好有工夫细细琢磨。”
“大家一起想！”薇丽蒂赞成，“四点钟碰头交换意见，定下演什么。伟大的密涅瓦（Minerva）呀！今晚准要热闹翻天！我连鞋子里都在打颤！”
“我都快吓绿了脸，怯场怯到家了！真是要命！”菲尔嚷嚷道。

===Original===
If Miss Godwin, the drawing-mistress, noticed a slacking off in accuracy on the part of four of her pupils, that afternoon, she perhaps set it down to want of artistic feeling. It is difficult to copy with absolute exactness when only your fingers are busy, and your brain is far away. Ingred planned enough entertainments to supply a Pierrot troupe for a month, but abandoned most of them as being quite impossible to act with the very limited resources that were available at the hostel. At a select Foursome Committee after school, however, she presented the pick of the performances, and as nobody else had thought of anything better, or indeed quite so good, her suggestions, with a few amendments and alterations, were carried unanimously.
At eight o’clock that evening, when preparation was finished, the boarders’ room was rapidly transformed into an amateur theater. The trestle tables were carried to one end to form the gallery, rows of chairs represented the dress circle, and cushions in front either the pit or the stalls, according to individual taste, or, as Mrs. Best said, the behavior of the occupants.
There was no curtain, but, as the scenery preserved Shakespearian methods of simplicity, that did not matter. Part of the charm of these Thursday night entertainments was their absolutely spontaneous character, and the fact that many details had to be left to the imagination of the spectators only made things more amusing.
When the audience, after a slight struggle for gallery seats, had settled itself, and Mrs. Best and Nurse Warner had taken possession of the armchairs specially reserved for them, Dollie Ransome, who had been requisitioned by the performers to act as Greek chorus, placed some stools by the fireplace, and announced importantly:
===Chinese===
那天下午，绘画老师戈德温小姐（Miss Godwin）倘若察觉到四个学生画功欠精准，多半会归咎于她们缺乏艺术感觉。手指忙着，脑子却早飞到九霄云外，这时候要把画临摹得分毫不差，实在难为人。英格丽德盘算出的节目，足够一个皮埃罗（Pierrot）剧团演上一个月，但其中大半都因宿舍里那点可怜的家底实在没法上演，只得忍痛舍弃。不过放学后，在四人同盟核心委员会的会议上，她端出了节目中的精华；旁人也没想出更好的主意——说实话，连不相上下的都没有——于是她的方案稍加修改增删，便获一致通过。
当晚八点，自习一结束，寄宿生活动室便迅速改造成一座业余剧场。支架长桌搬到屋子一头，权当顶层楼座；一排排椅子算作花楼包厢；再往前的坐垫，算是正厅后座还是前排正座，全凭各人喜好——或者照贝斯特太太的说法，全看坐的人表现如何。
舞台没有幕布也不要紧，反正布景秉承莎士比亚式的极简之风。这些星期四晚间余兴节目的魅力，一半正在于那份十足的即兴本色，许多细节只好留给观众自行想象，这反倒更添趣味。
观众为争楼座小小混战一场、各就各位之后，贝斯特太太和沃纳护士（Nurse Warner）也在特意为她们留出的扶手椅上落了座。这时，被演员们抓来充当古希腊歌队式报幕人的多莉·兰瑟姆（Dollie Ransome）在壁炉边摆好几张凳子，郑重其事地宣布：

===Original===
“King Alfred and the Cakes. A Historical Drama.”
The little old woman who entered, carrying some sticks and a basin, was difficult to identify as Fil. Her fair hair had been powdered, wrinkles were painted on her smooth forehead, a handkerchief was knotted on her head for a cap, and she wore an apron borrowed from the cook, and a check table-cover arranged as a shawl. She bestowed the sticks in the fender to represent a fire on the hearth, and taking some biscuits from her basin, placed them amongst the supposed embers, indulging meanwhile in a soliloquy about the hardness of the times for poor folk, and the danger from the Danes.
A violent knocking on the door was followed by the entrance of such a magnificent object that the spectators immediately applauded his advent. Nora, with her large build, short-cut hair, and generally boyish appearance, was the very one to act King Alfred. She had folded a plaid traveling rug into a kilt which reached just to her bare knees, borrowed a velvet coatee and a leather belt from Mrs. Best, and, by the aid of bandages from the ambulance cupboard, had made quite a good imitation of Saxon leg-gear. Armed with a bow and arrows, hastily constructed from twigs cut in the garden, she advanced with a manly stride, begged for hospitality, and was accommodated with a stool by the hearth, where she sat whittling arrows in an abstracted fashion, and heaving gusty sighs.
===Chinese===
“阿尔弗雷德国王与糕饼。一部历史剧。”
进门来的小个子老妇人，抱着几根柴火和一只盆，很难认出就是菲尔。她那头金发扑了粉，光洁的额头上画满皱纹，手帕在头顶打个结权充便帽，身上系着向厨娘借来的围裙，一块格纹桌布披作披肩。她把柴火码进炉挡里，算作炉膛生的火，又从盆里取出几块饼干，摆在这想象中的余烬上，一边还大段独白，慨叹穷苦人世道艰难，丹麦人又是何等凶险。
一阵急促的敲门声后，进来的那位人物好不威风，观众当即为他的登场鼓起掌来。诺拉身量大、头发剪得短，通身一副假小子做派，演阿尔弗雷德国王（King Alfred）再合适不过。她把一条格子花呢旅行毯折成一条刚及裸膝的短褶裙，向贝斯特太太借来一件天鹅绒短上衣和一根皮带，又借急救柜里的绷带一裹，扮出了一副像模像样的萨克森绑腿。她手执弓箭——都是现砍园中树枝匆匆扎成——迈着男子汉的大步上前，请求借宿，得了炉边一张凳子安身，坐在那儿心不在焉地削箭，不时深深叹上一口气。

===Original===
The audience had hardly recovered from its astonishment when it was thrilled again by the entrance of an ancient and elderly peasant man, so disguised that it was almost impossible to recognize Ingred. A waterproof with a broad leather belt served as coat, and, being padded inside with a pillow, gave the effect of bent and bowed shoulders. Some tow, supplied by Mrs. Best, was fastened as a long straggling beard, and bushy eyebrows of the same material were fixed on with soap. Leaning heavily upon a stick, he came limping in, complaining in a tremulous voice of his rheumatism, started with amazement at the sight of the handsome stranger seated by his hearth, and drew his wife aside for explanations. The old couple, after conversing in audible whispers, decided to go out for more firewood, and as a last charge the dame commended her cakes to the care of their guest. King Alfred, on being left alone by the hearth, whittled away at his arrows with more energy than discrimination, and showed indeed a sad lack of practical skill for so well seasoned a warrior. Perhaps, however, he was not accustomed to have to make them for himself, and missed his chief archer. Throwing them down at last, he sank his head in his hands in an absolute cinema pose of despondency, and sighed to an extent which must have been painful to his lungs. The dame returned to sniff burning cakes and fly to the rescue of her cookery. Fil was quite a good little actress, and produced what she considered her pièce de résistance. She had spent her summer holidays in Somerset, and had there picked up a local ballad which dealt with the legend in dialect. She brought out a verse of it now with great effect:

> “Cusn’t ee zee the ca-akes, man?
 

 And cusn’t ee zee ’em burrn?
 

 I’se warrant ee eat ’em fast enough,
 

 Zoon as it be ee turn!”

And catching up a biscuit, carefully blackened beforehand by toasting it over the gas, she flaunted it in the face of the embarrassed monarch.
===Chinese===
观众尚未从惊愕中回过神，又一位年迈的老农登场，再度叫全场屏息——那副扮相，几乎认不出是英格丽德。一件雨衣腰束宽皮带权充外袍，袍内塞了只枕头，便有了弯腰伛背的模样；一部又长又乱的胡子是用麻絮粘成的——麻絮也是贝斯特太太供给——同料的两撮浓眉则拿肥皂粘上。他重重拄着手杖，一瘸一拐蹭进来，嗓音发颤地抱怨着风湿，猛然瞧见自家炉边坐着位仪表堂堂的生客，惊得直打突，忙把老婆拉到一旁问个端详。老两口旁若无人地咬了一阵耳朵，决定出门再拾些柴火；临行前老太太千叮万嘱，将糕饼托付客人照看。阿尔弗雷德国王独自留在炉边，削起箭来干劲有余、章法全无，身为久经沙场的武士，手上功夫如此不济，着实令人扼腕。不过话说回来，他大约向来不必亲手制箭，如今少了首席弓箭手伺候，才这般失魂落魄。末了他把箭一撂，双手抱头，摆出一个十足电影腔的颓丧姿势，长吁短叹得怕不把肺都伤了。老太太回得屋来，一嗅糕饼烤糊，慌忙飞身抢救锅灶。菲尔不愧是个挺出色的小演员，此刻亮出了她自命的压轴绝活。她的暑假是在萨默塞特（Somerset）过的，在那里学来一支方言歌谣，唱的正是这段传说。这会儿她施展开来，效果煞是惊人：

> “汉子，你咋就瞅不见那糕饼？
 

 咋就瞅不见饼儿烤焦？
 

 俺敢保你吃得飞快——
 

 只消轮到你那一遭！”

说着她抓起一块饼干——这块饼干活计之前特意在煤气炉上烤得乌黑——直举到那位窘态毕露的君王脸前晃悠。

===Original===
The dramatic situation was slightly spoilt by the delay in the entrance of the courtier, who ought to have come in at that psychological moment, and didn’t. The fact was that Verity, finding it dull waiting in the passage, had run upstairs to make some additions to her costume, and had miscalculated the length, or rather shortness, of the act. It is difficult for the most accomplished actor to go on looking embarrassed for any length of time, and as Fil’s eloquence in the scolding line suddenly failed her, there was an awful pause while the peasant husband, with wonderful agility considering his rheumatism, hopped to the door and called agitatedly for the missing performer. The courtier flew downstairs like a whirlwind, tripped into the room, and fell upon his red-stockinged knees to do homage to his sovereign, who rose majestically and extended a hand of pardon to the now grovelling peasant.
The audience, particularly that portion seated in the gallery, clapped and cheered to such an extent that one of the trestles, which had been carelessly fixed, collapsed, and sent a whole row of girls sliding on to the floor, whence they were rescued speechless with laughter, but uninjured. They came crowding round the performers to admire the costumes.
“They’re topping!”
“How *did* you think of them?”
“I like King Alfred’s legs!”
“Ingred, you look about a hundred!”
===Chinese===
只可惜这戏剧性的一刻，被廷臣误了场，稍稍败了兴——他本该恰在这个节骨眼上登台，却迟迟不见踪影。原来是薇丽蒂在过道里候场候得无聊，跑上楼去给行头再添几样，却把这一幕的长度——不如说短度——估摸岔了。再有本事的演员，也很难把一脸窘相长久挂下去；偏偏菲尔骂人的词儿又突然告罄，于是一阵要命的冷场，那位老农丈夫顾不得风湿，以惊人的敏捷一蹦蹦到门口，急赤白脸地呼唤那位误场的演员。廷臣这才旋风似的冲下楼，踉跄抢进屋，扑通跪倒在红长袜裹着的双膝上，向君王行礼；君王庄严起身，向这会儿伏地不起的农夫伸出了赦免之手。
观众——尤其是楼座上那一拨——拍手叫好，闹得一张没搭稳的支架桌轰然塌了，整排姑娘顺势出溜到地板上，被搀起来时个个笑得说不出话，好在毫发无伤。她们又围上演员，对行头赞叹不已。
“妙极了！”
“这些*到底*是怎么想出来的？”
“我喜欢阿尔弗雷德国王的腿！”
“英格丽德，你瞧着足有一百岁！”

===Original===
“Fil *could* scold!”
“Verity, what was a courtier doing rambling about a forest in a blue dressing-gown? It would get torn on the bushes!”
“I know. We told her so, but she *would* wear it!” declared Ingred. “She was just pigheaded over that dressing-gown!”
“Well, go and look at the Saxon pictures for yourself, in the history book!” retorted Verity, sticking to her point. “You’ll see the courtiers in long flowing garments very like dressing-gowns. I think it was a capital idea, and the best I could do. There wasn’t another rug for the kilt anyhow, and when other people have taken the best parts and the nicest costumes, you’ve just got to put up with anything you can find that’s left.”
“You did it so well,” Ingred assured her hastily, for Verity had gone very pink, and her voice sounded distinctly offended. “I thought the way you dropped on one knee and cried: ‘My liege lord! I am your humble socman!’ was most impressive. What made you think of ‘socman’?”
“Got it out of the history book,” said Verity, slightly mollified. “It means a man who owned land, but wasn’t quite as high up as a thane. I meant to bring in some more Saxon words, but I hadn’t time.”
===Chinese===
“菲尔*真会*骂人！”
“薇丽蒂，哪有廷臣穿着一身蓝晨袍在林子里晃荡的？非叫灌木丛挂破不可！”
“谁说不是呢。我们劝过她，可她*偏*要穿！”英格丽德声称，“为那件晨袍，她犟得十头牛也拉不回！”
“哼，有本事自己上历史书里瞧萨克森人的画像去！”薇丽蒂反唇相讥，寸步不让，“你们自会看见那些廷臣身着长袍、衣袂飘飘，同晨袍一般无二。我自认这主意妙得很，也是我力所能及的最好法子。做短褶裙的毯子反正统共就一条，等旁人把好角色、好行头挑了个净，你就只能捡着剩下的将就了。”
“你演得实在好，”英格丽德忙不迭地宽慰她，因为薇丽蒂已经涨红了脸，话音里分明带着恼意，“你单膝跪地，高呼‘我的君王！我是您卑微的索克曼（socman）！’——我觉得那一幕气派极了。你怎么想起‘索克曼’这个词的？”
“历史书里捡来的，”薇丽蒂说，气略略平了，“就是有地产的人，只是地位没有塞恩（thane）那样高。我原想再多用几个萨克森词，可惜来不及。”

===Original===
“You must win the dormitory score again, and give us another performance,” urged Mrs. Best. “I’m afraid it’s too late for any more tonight, though we’re all sorry to stop. Those juniors ought to be in bed. Janie and Doreen, if you’d like a quiet half-hour to finish your prep. you may go into my room. Somebody put the tables back, please, and be sure the trestles are in their right places this time, we don’t want another collapse! Phyllis, your cough’s worse. Nurse shall rub your chest with camphorated oil, and you mustn’t kiss anybody. Betty too? I’ll give you a lozenge, but don’t suck it lying down in bed, in case you choke.”
So saying, Mrs. Best, who generally mothered the hostel, dismissed her large family and bustled away with Nurse to superintend the putting to bed of the juniors and the due care of those who might be regarded as even ever so slightly on the sick list. It was perhaps owing to the excitement of their spirited performance that the members of No. 2 Dormitory could not get to sleep that night. They all lay wide awake in bed, and told each other tales about burglars, in whispers. Verity’s stories were bloodcurdling in the extreme; she was a great reader, and had got them from magazines. Her three roommates listened with cold shivers running down their spines. According to Verity’s accounts it was a common and every day occurrence for a housebreaker to force an entrance, murder the occupants, and depart, leaving a case to baffle the police until some amateur detective turned up and solved the mystery.
“Has it ever struck you that the hostel would be a very easy place to burgle?” asked Fil. “Those French windows have no shutters, and the glass could be cut with a diamond.”
===Chinese===
“你们务必再把寝室的分挣回来，给我们另演一场，”贝斯特太太怂恿道，“只可惜今晚太迟，演不成第二个节目了，尽管大家都舍不得停。低年级的孩子们早该睡了。珍妮（Janie）、多琳（Doreen），你们要想安安静静半小时补完作业，就到我屋里去。请哪位把桌子搬回原处，这回务必把支架桌摆对位置，可别再塌一回！菲莉丝（Phyllis），你的咳嗽重了。让护士给你胸口擦些樟脑油，还不许你再亲任何人。贝蒂（Betty）也咳嗽吗？我给你一颗含片，可不许躺着含，仔细噎着。”
说罢，平日把宿舍当一大家子照拂的贝斯特太太便打发了她这群儿女，同护士一道匆匆去了，督着低年级的上床安寝，也照看那些哪怕只算沾着一星半点病气的人。这天夜里，二号寝室的众位或许正是叫这场火热的演出闹得兴奋过了头，翻来覆去睡不着。大家躺在床上毫无睡意，交头接耳地讲起盗贼的故事。薇丽蒂的故事惊悚到了极点；她是个大书迷，这些都是打杂志上看来的。三个室友听得脊梁骨一阵阵发凉。照薇丽蒂的说法，撬门入户、戕害住客、扬长而去，简直是家常便饭；那案子悬得警察一筹莫展，直到半路杀出个业余侦探，才把谜团解开。
“你们想过没有，咱们这宿舍特别容易遭贼？”菲尔问，“那些落地窗没有护窗板，玻璃用钻石一划就开。”

===Original===
“Or the doors could be opened with a skeleton key!” quavered Nora.
“I suppose they generally wear goloshes, so as to tread softly,” ventured Ingred.
“Wouldn’t it be dreadful,” continued Verity, whose mind still ran on magazine stories, “to marry a fascinating man whom you’d met by chance, and then find out that he was a gentleman-burglar? What would you do?”
“It often happens on the cinema,” said Nora. “The girl wavers about in an agony whether to tell or not, and wrings her hands and rolls her eyes, like they always *do* roll them on the films, and then, just when things are at the very last gasp, the husband tumbles over a precipice, or is wrecked at sea, or smashed in a railway accident, and she marries the other, who’s as good as gold, and loved her first.”
“Is the man who loves you first always as good as gold?” asked Fil.
“Well, generally on the Pictures. He’s loved you as a child, you see. You come on the film hand in hand, in socks, and he gives you his apple.”
===Chinese===
“不然就是门叫万能钥匙捅开！”诺拉声音发颤。
“我猜贼人一般都穿胶套鞋，好放轻脚步，”英格丽德大胆推测。
“要是一个不小心，嫁了个萍水相逢、偏偏又迷人的男人，”薇丽蒂接着说，脑子还转着杂志上的故事，“婚后才发现他是个绅士大盗，那可怎么好？你们会怎么办？”
“电影里常见这路子，”诺拉说，“姑娘左右为难，痛苦万分，不知说不说破，又是绞手，又是转眼珠——就跟他们在银幕上*照例*转的那样；然后呢，眼看事情到了山穷水尽的关头，丈夫一头栽下悬崖，要么海上遇难，要么火车事故里粉身碎骨，她便嫁了另一位——那位好得没挑，还是头一个爱上她的。”
“头一个爱你的人，就一定好得没挑吗？”菲尔问。
“呃，银幕上一般如此。你想啊，人家从小就爱你。你们穿着短袜手拉手一块儿上镜，他把苹果让给你。”

===Original===
“But suppose they don’t love you from a child?” said Fil plaintively. “I’ve only known a lot of horrid little boys whom I didn’t care for in the least. None of them ever gave me his apple, though I remember one taking mine. Is the first fascinating man I meet the true lover or the burglar? How am I to know which is which?”
“You’d better let me be there to decide for you, child, or you’ll be snapped up by the first adventurer that comes along,” declared Nora. “Don’t trust him if he has a mustache. ‘Daring Dick of the Black Gang’ had a little twisted mustache like Mephistopheles in *Faust*.”
“Oh dear! And the last piece I saw on the Pictures, the villain was clean shaven! That’s no guide at all!”
“Girls, you’re breaking the silence rule!” said Mrs. Best, opening the door of Dormitory 2, where the conversation, which had begun in whispers, had risen to a pitch audible on the landing outside. “This doesn’t look like scoring again next week, and giving another performance. Why, Nora, the rain’s driving through that open window straight on to your bed! You’ll be getting rheumatism! I shall shut it, and leave the door wide open for air instead. Now be good girls and go to sleep at once. Don’t let me hear any more talking.”
===Chinese===
“可要是没人从小爱你呢？”菲尔委屈巴巴地说，“我认识的净是些讨厌的小子，一个也入不了眼。谁也不曾让苹果给我，倒记得有一个抢过我的。那我头一回碰上的迷人男人，到底是真情人，还是大盗？叫我怎么分清谁是谁？”
“小丫头，你顶好让我在旁边替你拿主意，不然头一个过路的冒险家就能把你拐跑，”诺拉断言，“他要是留胡子，千万别信。《黑帮大胆迪克》（Daring Dick of the Black Gang）里那位，就蓄着两撇拧起的小胡子，活像《浮士德》（Faust）里的梅菲斯特（Mephistopheles）。”
“哎呀糟了！可我上回在电影里瞧见的那个坏蛋，脸刮得光光的！这压根儿没法子判断嘛！”
“姑娘们，你们犯静默规矩了！”贝斯特太太说着，推开二号寝室的门——这场起初还咬着耳朵的谈话，这会儿已经响到外面楼梯间都听得见了。“照这样子，下星期还想挣分、还想再演一场？我说，诺拉，雨正从那扇敞开的窗子直灌到你床上！你想得风湿吗！我把窗关上，房门敞开来通风就是。都做乖姑娘，立刻睡觉。别再让我听见一句说话。”

===Original===
The Foursomes, in common with most of the hostel, were fond of Mrs. Best, so they turned over obediently, and composed themselves to slumber. They were really tired by this time, and dropped off into the land of Nod before the clock on the stairs had chimed another quarter. How long she slept, Ingred did not know. She dreamt quite a long and circumstantial dream of wandering on the cliffs near the sea with a gentleman-burglar, who was telling her his intention of raiding Buckingham Palace and taking away the Crown Jewels, and she heard his daring designs (as we always do in dreams) without the slightest surprise or any suggestion that the Crown Jewels are kept at the Tower instead of at Buckingham Palace. She woke suddenly, and laughed at the absurdity of the idea. She felt hot, and threw back her eiderdown. The other girls were sleeping quietly, and the rain was still beating against the window in heavy showers, for it was a stormy night. The door of the bedroom stood wide open. What was that sound coming up the stairs from the hall below? It was certainly not the ticking of the clock. It seemed more like muffled and stealthy footsteps. In an instant Ingred was very wide awake indeed, and listening intently. There it came again! She could not lie still and ignore it. She got out of bed, and with rather shaking knees walked on to the landing and peeped over the banisters. There was a tiny oil-lamp hanging on the wall; it faintly illuminated the stairs. Was that somebody moving about in the darkness of the hall? If it was a burglar, he certainly must not come upstairs, or she would die of fright. An idea occurred to her, and acting on a sudden impulse she dashed into Dormitory 2, roused the others, and told them to snatch what missiles they could, and hurry to her aid.
“We’ll fling things at him if he tries to come up!” she gasped, groping for her boots.
It was a horrible experience: four nervous, quaking girls stood in the dim light on the landing gazing down into the haunted blackness of the shadowy hall. The sounds had ceased temporarily, but now they began again﻿—a distinct shuffling as of footsteps, and even a subdued sniff, then the outline of a dark figure made its appearance, bearing straight for the stairs.
===Chinese===
与宿舍里大多数人一样，四人同盟的几位也喜爱贝斯特太太，便乖乖翻过身，安顿入睡。这时她们实在也乏了，楼梯上的钟还未敲完下一个一刻钟，便都已沉入梦乡。睡了多久，英格丽德不知道。她做了一个又长又细密的梦，梦见同一位绅士大盗在海边悬崖上漫步，大盗正向她吐露雄图，要去洗劫白金汉宫（Buckingham Palace），把御宝（Crown Jewels）席卷而走；她听着那些胆大包天的谋划（我们在梦里向来如此），没有半分诧异，也没想起御宝原是收在伦敦塔（Tower），并不在白金汉宫。她蓦地醒来，想到这念头的荒唐，不由失笑。身上发热，她掀开鸭绒被。别的姑娘睡得正稳，外面是个风雨之夜，大雨仍旧一阵阵扑打着窗子。卧室的门敞得大开。从楼下大厅沿楼梯传上来的是什么响动？那决计不是钟的滴答。倒更像是压得极低的、蹑手蹑脚的脚步声。刹那间，英格丽德睡意全消，凝神细听。又来了！她再不能躺着置之不理。她下了床，双膝微微打颤地走上楼梯间，从栏杆上探头下望。墙上挂着一盏小油灯，昏昏地照着楼梯。大厅的黑影里，可是有人在走动？倘若真是贼，万万不能放他上楼，不然她会活活吓死。她灵机一动，一时兴起，奔回二号寝室叫醒众人，吩咐她们抓起一切抓得到的投掷家什，速来援手。
“他敢上来，咱们就拿东西砸他！”她喘着气说，一面摸索自己的靴子。
这滋味可真够受的：四个战战兢兢的姑娘立在楼梯间昏暗的光里，俯瞰着楼下鬼影幢幢、黑沉沉的大厅。那声响停了一阵，此刻又起了——分明是蹭着地的脚步声，甚至夹着一声闷闷的抽鼻子，跟着，一个黑影显出轮廓，径直朝楼梯而来。

===Original===
With quite commendable bravery Ingred flung her boots at it, which missiles were instantly followed by Nora’s hairbrush, Fil’s dispatch case, and Verity’s pillow. It screamed in a most unburglar-like voice, and apparently with genuine fright.
“If you t-t-t-try to c-c-come nearer, I’ll sh-sh-shoot you dead!” quavered Ingred, wishing she had at least some semblance of a pistol to bluff with.
“What *are* you doing, girls?” replied the dark shadow, persisting in its movement towards the staircase, and, as it came into the faint circle of radiance spread by the lamp, resolving itself into the familiar form of Nurse Warner. “Have you suddenly gone mad?”
Here was a situation! The four girls flew back to their dormitory in great haste, especially as Mrs. Best, disturbed by the noise, had opened her door and come on to the scene in a pink-and-gray dressing-gown. They were followed, however, by both Matron and Nurse, and forced to give an explanation of their extraordinary conduct.
===Chinese===
英格丽德勇气着实可嘉，抡起靴子便掷了过去；这阵“弹雨”旋即引来了诺拉的发刷、菲尔的公文包和薇丽蒂的枕头。那黑影发出一声全然不似大盗的尖叫，听来是当真吓着了。
“你、你、你要是敢、敢、敢再靠近，我、我、我就开枪崩了你！”英格丽德声气发颤，心里只恨没有一把哪怕充样子的手枪好壮声势。
“姑娘们，你们*究竟*在干什么？”黑影答话，脚下仍不住往楼梯这边来；待走进油灯洒开的一圈微光里，显出了那张熟识的面孔——不是沃纳护士是谁。“你们是突然疯了不成？”
这下场面可妙了！四个姑娘飞也似的逃回寝室，何况贝斯特太太已被响动惊起，开了房门，穿着粉灰相间的晨袍赶到现场。然而舍监和护士双双跟了进来，她们只得对这番离奇行径从头交代。

===Original===
“I couldn’t sleep for the wind, so I put on my felt slippers and my cloak, and went downstairs for a biscuit,” declared Nurse Warner, whose voice sounded rather aggrieved. “I didn’t think I should disturb anybody.”
“You girls are the limit with your silly notions!” said Mrs. Best, really angry for once. “If you fill your heads with absurd ideas about burglars before you go to sleep, of course you can imagine anything. If I hear any more talking in No. 2 another night after the lights are out, I shall separate you, and send each of you to sleep in another dormitory. I’ll not have the house upset like this! So you know what to expect. Are you all in your beds? Then not another word!”
“It’s very uncomfy without my pillow!” whispered naughty Verity, in distinct disobedience to this mandate, as the door of Mrs. Best’s room closed. “Dare I go and fetch it?”
“Sh! Sh! No!”
“I know what we’ll give Nursie for a Christmas present,” murmured Fil softly. “A nice ornamental tin box of biscuits to keep in her bedroom. She shan’t get hungry in the night again, poor dear!”
“*Sh! Sh. Will* you go to sleep!” warned Ingred emphatically.
===Chinese===
“风吵得我睡不着，我就趿上毡拖鞋、披了斗篷，下楼找块饼干吃，”沃纳护士申明，话音里颇带着委屈，“我原想不至于搅了谁。”
“你们这群丫头，犯起傻来真是没治了！”贝斯特太太说，这一回是真动了气，“睡前满脑子塞满盗贼的荒唐念头，自然什么都能疑神出来。往后再有哪个夜里熄了灯，我听见二号寝室还有人讲话，就把你们拆开，一人发一个寝室各自去睡。这个家可容不得这般鸡飞狗跳！该当如何，你们自己掂量。都归床了？那就再不许出一个字！”
“没了枕头真难受！”淘气的薇丽蒂悄声道，明摆着没把这道禁令放在心上——贝斯特太太的房门恰在此时关上了。“我敢不敢去把枕头捡回来？”
“嘘！嘘！不行！”
“我知道圣诞节送护士小姐什么礼物了，”菲尔柔声嘀咕，“送一只体面的彩铁盒饼干，搁在她卧室里。可怜的人，夜里就再不会挨饿啦！”
“*嘘！嘘——你们*倒是睡觉呀！”英格丽德加重了语气警告。

## XVI An Easter Pilgrimage / XVI 复活节朝圣


===Original===
The thirteen weeks between Christmas and Easter dragged much more slowly than those of the autumn term. The weather was cold and variable. As fast as Spring stirred in the earth, Winter seemed to stretch forth chilly fingers to check her advent. Nature, like a careful mother, kept the buds tightly folded on the trees and the yellow daffodil blossoms securely hidden under their green casement curtains. Only the most foolhardy birds ventured to begin building operations. The rooks in the elm trees near the Abbey had begun to repair their nests during a mild spurt in January, then put off further alterations till late in March. Morning after morning the girls would wake to find the roofs covered with hoar frost. Ingred, who hated the cold, shivered as she crossed the windy quadrangle from the college to the hostel, and congratulated herself that she lived in the days of modern comforts.
“How the old monks and nuns managed to exist in those wretched chilly damp cloisters I can’t imagine,” she said, as she squatted by the stove warming her hands. “Were they allowed to take hot bricks to bed with them in their cells? Think of turning out for midnight services into an unwarmed church! It sounds absolutely miserable!”
“Perhaps they made themselves more comfortable than we think,” commented Verity. “One of them probably kept up the fire and doled out hot drinks after the services. It might even have been possible to take a hot-water bottle to church under the folds of those ample habits.”
“I don’t believe that would have been allowed. Surely the cold was part of the discipline.”
“I shouldn’t have been a nun if I’d lived in the Middle Ages,” said Fil. “I’d have wanted to go to the tournaments and to have seen my knight fighting with my ribbons in his helmet and bringing me the crown. Oh, wouldn’t it have been fun? Life’s not a scrap romantic nowadays. I do think men are slackers. Why don’t they wear their ladies’ colors at football, and let whoever gets a goal carry a wreath of flowers to the pavilion and crown his girl ‘Queen of Beauty’? There’d be some excitement in looking on then. As it is it’s nothing but a scrimmage; and I never care a button which side wins. You needn’t laugh. Why shouldn’t a footballer look gallant and present trophies? The world would jog on a great deal better if there were more chivalry in it.”
===Chinese===
从圣诞节到复活节之间的十三个星期，比秋季学期的日子拖沓得多。天气寒冷多变。春天刚在地底下有些动静，冬天似乎就伸出冰冷的手指，拦住她的来路。大自然像一位细心的母亲，把树上的芽苞攥得紧紧的，让黄色的水仙花朵稳稳地藏在绿色的窗帷后面。只有最不管不顾的鸟儿才敢动手筑巢。修道院（Abbey）旁榆树上的那群白嘴鸦，一月里趁着一段暖和天气动工修整旧巢，随后便把进一步的翻修工程推迟到了三月下旬。女孩子们一个早晨接一个早晨醒来，总发现屋顶上覆着一层白霜。英格丽德（Ingred）最怕冷，顶着风从教学楼穿过四方院子走向宿舍时，冻得直打哆嗦，一面暗自庆幸自己生活在享有现代舒适设施的年月。
“真想象不出，古时候的修士修女是怎么在那些又阴又冷又湿的回廊里活下来的。”她蹲在火炉边烤着手说，“他们的小屋里，让不让抱着热砖头上床？想想看，半夜爬起来做午夜礼拜，一头扎进一座不生火的教堂！听着简直惨透了！”
“说不定他们把自己弄得比咱们想的舒服呢。”薇丽蒂（Verity）评论道，“多半有个专人守着炉火，礼拜完给大伙儿分发热饮。没准儿还能把热水袋藏在那宽袍大褶里，偷偷带进教堂去。”
“我看那是不被允许的。受冻肯定就是苦修的一部分。”
“我要是生活在中世纪，才不当修女呢。”菲尔（Fil）说，“我要去看骑士比武大会，看我的骑士头盔上系着我的缎带，上阵厮杀，再把冠冕捧回来献给我。噢，那该多有意思！如今的生活一点儿浪漫味儿都没有。我真觉得如今的男人们个个懒洋洋、没劲头。踢足球的时候，他们干吗不佩戴心上人的颜色？谁进了球，就让他捧一顶花环到看台上去，把自己的姑娘加冕为‘美之女王’？那样观战才有几分刺激。可眼下呢，无非是乱哄哄抢作一团，哪边赢了我半个铜板都不在乎。你们别笑。足球运动员干吗就不能英姿飒爽一回，献上战利品？这世上要是多几分骑士精神，准会顺当得多。”

===Original===
“The girls want to play games themselves nowadays instead of looking on and receiving trophies,” giggled Verity.
“I don’t!” declared Fil emphatically. “I hate tearing about at hockey, or running at cricket. I’d far rather let my knight do the work for me.”
“Chilly work looking on in this weather. The games keep one warm,” said Ingred, who was still only half thawed.
In spite of boisterous March winds and late spring frosts the sun climbed steadily higher in the sky and the days lengthened. Ingred, who used to arrive home in the twilight at Wynchcote on Friday afternoons, could now dig in the garden after tea. She liked the scent of newly-turned earth, and was happy working away with a trowel transplanting roots of wallflowers and forget-me-nots to make a display in the bed near the dining-room window. At school the various forms vied with one another in shows of hyacinths grown in bowls, the best of which were lent to the studio on drawing days and figured as models for watercolor sketches, together with daffodils and hazel catkins. Lispeth, who did not relax the activities of The Rainbow League, revived her idea of a Posy Union, persuaded some of the girls to bring little pots of gay crocuses or blue squills to school, and after these had been duly exhibited on a table in the lecture-hall, sent them through the agency of a “Children’s Welfare Worker” to brighten the bedsides of various small invalids in the poorer quarters of the town and let them know that spring had arrived.
===Chinese===
“如今的女孩子都想自己上场打球，谁还肯站在边上看、光等着领奖杯呀。”薇丽蒂咯咯笑着说。
“我就肯！”菲尔斩钉截铁地宣称，“我最恨在曲棍球场上疯跑，或者在板球场上追着球跑。我宁可让我的骑士替我代劳。”
“这种天气站在边上看球可是个冷差事，打打球身上倒暖和。”英格丽德说，她到这会儿才暖和过来一半。
尽管三月的风刮得正猛，春寒的霜也迟迟不退，太阳仍旧一天天稳稳地爬得更高，白昼也一日日变长。英格丽德从前每逢星期五下午回家，抵达温奇科特（Wynchcote）时总是暮色苍茫；如今喝过下午茶，还能到花园里掘掘土。她喜欢新翻泥土的气息，拿着小铲子把桂竹香和勿忘我的根株移栽到饭厅窗前的花坛里，好让那里开出一片景致，干得满心欢喜。在学校里，各年级争相炫耀钵栽的风信子，其中最好的几盆，到上绘画课的日子便借去画室，同黄水仙、榛树柔荑一道，充当水彩写生的模特儿。丽丝佩丝（Lispeth）丝毫没有放松“彩虹联盟”（The Rainbow League）的活动，这回又重提她那个“花束联合会”（Posy Union）的构想，劝得几位女同学带了几小盆鲜亮的番红花或蓝色的蓝钟花到学校来。这些花先在讲堂的桌子上规规矩矩展出一场，然后经一位“儿童福利员”的手送出去，装点镇上穷苦街区一张张病榻边的小天地，好让那些生病的小家伙也知道：春天来了。

===Original===
Eastertide was very near now, and the school would break up for three weeks. Miss Burd was going away to allow her tired brains to lie fallow for a while, and most of the other teachers were looking forward to a well-earned rest apart from their forms. It came as a surprise to everybody when Miss Strong﻿—alone﻿—among the staff﻿—suggested the project of taking some of her pupils for a short walking tour. They were to start off, like pilgrims of old, carrying with them the barest necessaries, and have a four days’ tramp to visit a few of the beauty spots of the neighborhood, spending a couple of nights en route.
“It will be a real open-air holiday,” she assured them. “We shall be out of doors all day long and eat most of our meals by the roadside. I’ve planned it out carefully. A short railway journey to Carford, then walk by easy stages through Ryton-on-the-Heath to Dropwick and Pursborough, where we can get the train again back to Grovebury. I know of two extremely nice Temperance Hotels where we can be put up for the night. By going in this way we shall see the cream of the country. Any girl who is a good walker may join the party.”
It certainly sounded a fascinating program, and after due consideration at home eight girls put their names down for the excursion﻿—Ingred, Verity, Nora, Bess, Linda, Francie, Kitty, and Belle. They felt it would be quite a new experience to know Miss Strong out of school hours; the light in her eyes when she announced the scheme gave promise of hitherto hidden capacities for fun. It circulated round the form that she might prove quite a jolly companion. Those girls who could not join the tour were a trifle wistful and inclined towards envy. They took it out of the pilgrims in gloomy prognostications concerning the weather.
===Chinese===
复活节眼看就要到了，学校将放假三个星期。伯德小姐（Miss Burd）打算出门远行，让她疲惫的脑子休耕一阵子；其余大多数教师也盼着离开各自的班级，享受一个受之无愧的假期。不料，全体教员中竟有一位——而且是仅有的一位——斯特朗小姐（Miss Strong）提出了一项计划：带几名学生作一次短途徒步旅行，人人都为此吃了一惊。她们要像古时的朝圣者那样启程，随身只携带最起码的必需品，徒步四天，寻访邻近几处胜景，途中在外过两夜。
“这将是一个地道的户外假期，”她向大家保证，“我们成天都在露天里，大多数饭都在路边吃。行程我仔细盘算过了：先坐一小段火车到卡福德（Carford），然后分作轻松的几段，徒步经过荒原畔赖顿（Ryton-on-the-Heath），走到德罗普威克（Dropwick）和珀斯伯勒（Pursborough），在那里再搭火车回格罗夫伯里（Grovebury）。我知道两家极好的禁酒旅馆，可供我们投宿。这样走一遭，这一带乡野的精华便尽收眼底。谁脚力好，谁就可以加入这支队伍。”
这计划听上去着实诱人。经过家里的郑重商量，八个女孩子报了名——英格丽德、薇丽蒂、诺拉（Nora）、贝丝（Bess）、琳达（Linda）、弗朗茜（Francie）、凯蒂（Kitty）和贝尔（Belle）。她们觉得，在课堂之外结识斯特朗小姐，将是一番全新的体验；她宣布这项计划时眼里闪动的光彩，预示着她身上还潜藏着从未显露过的取乐本领。于是班里纷纷传开：她说不定是位挺有趣的旅伴呢。那些不能参加远足的女孩子有几分怅然，又免不了眼热，便拿阴惨惨的天气预报朝这些“朝圣者”出气。

===Original===
“It will probably rain all the time and you’ll tramp along like a row of drowned rats,” suggested Beatrice.
“It won’t do anything of the sort. I believe we’re going to have a fine mild spell and it will be just glorious. I’m taking my ‘Brownie,’ so there’ll be some snapshots to show we’ve been enjoying ourselves,” retorted Nora briskly. “You stay-at-homes will be sorry for yourselves when you hear our adventures!”
To allow the weather ample chance of improvement, and perhaps also to give Miss Strong time to rest, the excursion was fixed for the last week of the holidays. One morning in mid-April, therefore, found teacher and pupils meeting together on the platform of Grovebury station to catch the 9:25 train to Carford. They wore jerseys and their school hats, and they carried their luggage according to their individual ideas of convenience. Linda wore her little brother’s satchel slung over her back. Nora had borrowed a knapsack, Kitty preferred a parcel, Verity packed her possessions in a string bag, and Bess carried a neat dispatch-case.
===Chinese===
“多半会一路下雨，你们就像一串落汤鼠似的，深一脚浅一脚往前蹚。”碧翠丝（Beatrice）预言道。
“才不会那样呢！我看我们准会碰上一段晴暖的好天气，那才叫痛快。我要带上我的‘布朗尼’（Brownie），拍些快照回来，好证明我们玩得开心。”诺拉脆生生地回敬，“等听到我们的种种奇遇，你们这些留守的家伙准要懊悔死！”
为了给天气留出充裕的转好机会，或许也是为了让斯特朗小姐歇息歇息，远足定在了假期最后一周。于是，四月中旬的一个早晨，师生一行聚在格罗夫伯里车站的月台上，等候搭乘九点二十五分去卡福德的火车。她们穿着针织运动衫，戴着学校的帽子，行李怎么携带，全凭各人觉得怎么方便。琳达把小弟弟的书包斜挎在背上；诺拉借来一只背包；凯蒂宁愿拎一个包裹；薇丽蒂把随身物件装进一只网兜；贝丝则提着一只精致利落的公文包。

===Original===
“I’d a ripping idea for mine, but it wouldn’t work,” declared Ingred. “I meant to tie my parcel to a balloon and then just lead it along by a string. But I couldn’t get a proper gas balloon for the business, and that’s what you ought to have.”
“And suppose the wind were to blow it away from you, what then?” inquired Miss Strong.
“I suppose I should have to cable it round my waist.”
“Then you might be whisked up with it, and we should see you sailing off into the clouds in a kind of aeroplane holiday instead of a walking tour! I don’t think we can patent your balloon dodge yet.”
===Chinese===
“我的行李本有个绝妙的主意，可惜行不通。”英格丽德宣称，“我本想把包裹拴在气球上，拿根绳子牵着走就是了。可我弄不到干这桩事该有的那种正经氢气球。”
“那要是风把它从你手里吹跑了，你怎么办？”斯特朗小姐问。
“我想我大概只好把绳子拴在腰上了。”
“那你没准儿会跟着它一起被卷上天，我们就要眼睁睁望着你乘着气球飘进云霄，徒步旅行变成飞机度假了！我看你这气球妙计，眼下还申请不到专利。”

===Original===
“What I want,” said Kitty, “is a sort of child’s light mail-cart arrangement that I could wheel along. It’s what Mother always says she needs for shopping﻿—a parcel-holder on wheels. Why doesn’t somebody invent one? He﻿—or she (I’m sure it would be a *she*)﻿—would make a fortune.”
“We might have borrowed a perambulator,” said Belle, quite seriously, “and have packed all our luggage into it.”
“Oh, I dare say! And who would have wheeled it?”
“We could have taken it in turns.”
“With long turns for the willing horses, and short turns for shirkers! No, thanks! Better each to stick to our own.”
===Chinese===
“我想要的，”凯蒂说，“是一种小孩子用的轻便小邮车那样的玩意儿，能推着走。妈妈总说，出门买东西就缺这么个东西——一个带轮子的置物架。怎么就没人发明一个呢？发明的人——不论是男是女（我敢保险准是个*女的*）——准能发一笔大财。”
“咱们本可以借一辆婴儿车嘛，”贝尔说得一本正经，“把行李统统装进去。”
“噢，说得倒轻巧！那由谁来推呢？”
“咱们可以轮班推呀。”
“肯卖力的‘马儿’轮得久，偷懒的轮得短，是不是！不劳驾了，谢谢！还是各人拿各人的好。”

===Original===
“Besides which, forget stiles. We hope to try some field paths as well as high roads,” added Miss Strong. “Also I should decidedly have jibbed at escorting a perambulator. Here comes the train! Let us make a dash for an empty carriage and keep it to ourselves.”
It was only a short journey to Carford, but it took them over twelve rather uninteresting miles and put them down just at the commencement of a very beautiful stretch of country where open uplands alternated with wooded coombes, and where the stone-roofed villages were the prettiest in the county.
Miss Strong, who had had some experience of mountaineering in Switzerland, restrained the pace and kept them all at what she called a “guide’s walk.”
===Chinese===
“再说了，别忘了路上还有栅栏梯。我们除了大路，还打算试试田间的路径。”斯特朗小姐补充道，“何况，要让我护送一辆婴儿车，我是断然不干的。火车来了！咱们冲上去占一节空车厢，独享它！”
到卡福德的火车旅程虽短，却载着她们驶过十二英里乏善可陈的乡野，而后在一段绝佳风光的起点让她们下了车：那里开阔的高地与林木葱茏的幽谷错落相间，石板顶的村庄是全郡最美的。
斯特朗小姐在瑞士有过几分登山经验，一路上约束着步速，让所有人保持她所谓的“向导步”。

===Original===
“It pays in the long run,” she assured them. “If you tear ahead at first, you get tired later on, and we must keep fairly well together. I can’t have some of you half a mile behind.”
The April days were still cold, but very bracing for exercise. Lambs were out in the fields, primroses grew in clumps under the hedgerows, hazel catkins flung showers of pollen to the winds, and in the coppice that bordered the road pale-mauve March violets and white anemone stars showed through last year’s carpet of dead leaves. There was that joyful thrill of spring in the air, that resurrection of Nature when the thraldom of winter is over, and beauty comes back to the gray dim world. The old Greeks felt it, thousands of years ago, and fabled it in their myth of Persephone and her return from Hades. The Druids knew it in Ancient Britain, and fixed their religious ceremonies for May Day. The birds were caroling it still in the hedgerows, and the girls caught the joyous infection and danced along in defiance of Miss Strong’s jog-trot guide walk. Even the mistress herself, so wise at the outset, finally flung prudence to the winds, and skirmished through the coppices with enthusiasm equal to that of her pupils, lured from the pathway by the glimpses of kingcups, or the pursuit of a peacock butterfly.
“All the same, if we tear round like small dogs, we shall never reach Dropwick tonight, and I’ve booked our rooms there,” she assured them. “You don’t want to sleep on the heather, I suppose!”
===Chinese===
“从长远算是划算的，”她向大家保证，“一开头就猛冲，后面就该泄劲了。我们必须大体上走在一起，我可不能让谁落到半英里开外。”
四月的日子依然寒冷，却冷得清新提神，正宜走路。田野里已放出了羊羔，树篱下一丛丛报春花正开，榛树的柔荑把一阵阵花粉扬进风里；在路旁的矮树林中，淡紫的三月紫罗兰和星星点点的白色银莲花，正从去年铺就的枯叶地毯间探出头来。空气里有那股春天特有的欢快悸动——大自然复活了，冬日的桎梏一旦解除，美便重回这灰蒙蒙、暗沉沉的世界。几千年前的古希腊人就感受到它，把它编进珀耳塞福涅（Persephone）自冥府归来的神话；上古不列颠的德鲁伊（Druids）祭司也懂得它，把宗教祭祀定在了五朔节。如今鸟儿仍在树篱间高声唱着它，女孩子们也染上这份欢腾，把斯特朗小姐不紧不慢的向导步全然抛在脑后，一路雀跃而行。连这位老师本人——起初那么老成持重——最后也把谨慎抛到九霄云外，在矮树林里钻进钻出，那股热乎劲儿不输她的学生：一会儿瞥见一丛驴蹄草便离了小径，一会儿又去追一只孔雀蛱蝶。
“话虽如此，咱们要是像小狗似的撒欢乱跑，今晚就到不了德罗普威克了，我在那儿订好了房间呢。”她提醒大家，“我猜你们总不想睡在石楠荒原上吧！”

===Original===
“Bow-wow! Shouldn’t mind!” laughed Kitty. “We could cling together and keep each other warm.”
“You won’t cling to me, thanks! I prefer a bed of my own.”
Nora, having brought a good supply of films for her Brownie camera, was most keen on taking snapshots. She photographed the company eating their lunch on a bank by the roadside, with Miss Strong in the very act of biting a piece of bread and butter, and Ingred with her face buried in a mug. She even went further. She had been reading a book on faked photography, and she yearned to try experiments.
“I’m going to give those stay-at-homes a few thrills,” she declared. “I told them we’d have adventures.”
Nora expounded her plan to Miss Strong, who was sufficiently interested in the subject to promise her collusion and good advice. A mock Alpine scene came first. Nora had brought with her, for this express purpose, a length of rope, which she wore around her jersey like a Carmelite’s girdle. She took it off now and fastened it round the waists of three of her schoolfellows, linking them together in the manner of Swiss mountaineers. Then she found a piece of rock on which were narrow ledges, and, with the help of Miss Strong, posed them in attitudes of apparent peril. Really, they were only a couple of feet from the ground, and a fall would have been a laughing matter, but in a camera they appeared to be clinging almost by their eyelashes to the face of an inaccessible crag and in imminent danger of their lives. Nora took two views, and chuckled with satisfaction.
===Chinese===
“汪汪！睡石楠丛我也不在乎！”凯蒂笑道，“咱们可以挤作一团，你暖我、我暖你。”
“多谢，你可别来贴我！我还是乐意自己睡一张床。”
诺拉为她的布朗尼相机备足了胶卷，一门心思热衷于拍快照。她拍了众人坐在路旁土坡上吃午饭的合影：斯特朗小姐正张口咬一块黄油面包，英格丽德则把脸埋在一只大口杯上喝水。她还嫌不够过瘾。原来她近来读了一本讲特技摄影的书，正手痒难耐，一心想试验一番。
“我要给那帮留守的家伙来点刺激的，”她宣称，“我跟她们说过，咱们会有奇遇。”
诺拉把自己的计划向斯特朗小姐和盘托出；老师对这个题目颇感兴趣，竟答应与她串通一气，还给她出了些好主意。头一幕是假冒的阿尔卑斯山景。诺拉专为这桩事随身带了一捆绳子，像加尔默罗会修士的腰绳似的缠在运动衫外面。此时她解下绳来，系在三位同学的腰间，照瑞士登山客的样子把她们连成一串。接着她寻到一块带窄窄岩檐的岩石，在斯特朗小姐的协助下，让她们摆出险象环生的姿势。其实她们离地不过两英尺，摔下来也不过博人一笑；可到了镜头里，她们便成了全凭睫毛扒在一面人迹不至的峭壁上、眼看性命不保的登山者。诺拉拍了两张，满意得咯咯直笑。

===Original===
“That’ll make their hair stand on end! I’ll fix a few more sensations if I can. Who’s game to run six inches in front of a mild old cow’s horns, while somebody urges her on from behind?”
“How will you guarantee she’s mild?” inquired Bess dubiously. “She might take it into her head to toss us!”
“Not she! It was only the ‘cow with the crumpled horn’ that went in for tossing.”
“Well, I’d rather be in a safer photo, thanks! I’m terrified of cows, anyway.”
Nora’s instincts were really quite dramatic. She photographed Bess crouching in the hollow of a tree, an imaginary fugitive, to whom Francie, in an attitude of caution, handed surreptitious victuals. She posed Linda, apparently lifeless, on the borders of a pond, with Kitty and Verity applying artificial respiration. She bound up Ingred’s head with a handkerchief, and placed her arm in a sling as the result of a fictitious accident, and would have arranged a circle of weeping girls round the prostrate body of Miss Strong, had not that stalwart lady stoutly objected.
“I’m not going to do anything of the sort, so put up that camera, and come along at once. We’ve wasted far too much time already, and we shall have to step out unless we want to finish our walk in the dark. I promise you tea at Ryton-on-the-Heath, if you hurry, but we can’t stop half an hour there unless you put your best foot foremost, so, quick march!”
===Chinese===
“这张准保叫她们头发倒竖！只要办得到，我还要多来几出惊险戏。谁敢到一头温驯老母牛的犄角前六英寸处跑上一趟，再派一个人在后面撺掇它赶上来？”
“你怎么担保那牛是温驯的？”贝丝将信将疑地问，“它兴许一时犯了牛脾气，用角把咱们挑上天呢！”
“它才不呢！爱用角挑人的，只有童谣里那头‘歪角母牛’。”
“谢谢，我还是上安全点儿的镜头吧！反正我一见牛就怕得要死。”
诺拉的天性实在充满戏剧细胞。她拍下贝丝蜷缩在树洞里，扮一名仓皇的逃亡者，弗朗茜则装出一脸警觉，偷偷把干粮递给她。她让琳达直挺挺躺在池塘边，仿佛气绝多时，由凯蒂和薇丽蒂为她做人工呼吸。她用手帕裹住英格丽德的头，又用三角巾把她一条胳膊吊起来，充作一场子虚乌有的事故的后果；她还想布置一圈哭泣的女孩，环绕斯特朗小姐倒卧的“遗体”，无奈那位健壮的女士坚决不干，此事才作罢。
“我绝不来这一套。收起相机，马上赶路。咱们耽搁的时间已经太多，再不加快脚步，就得摸黑走完剩下的路了。你们要是快些，我保准让大家在荒原畔赖顿喝上茶；可要是不拿出最快的脚程，连半小时也停不成——齐步，走！”

## VIII An Unpleasant Experience / 八、一次不愉快的经历


===Original===
The girls filed out from the hockey ground as speedily as possible. There was a train due from Grovebury in about a quarter of an hour. They walked to the station in groups, discussing details of the match as they went. Ingred, Beatrice, and Verity happened to be blocked at the exit by the Clintonian team, and were obliged to wait some minutes before they could pass, and when at last they were through the gate, all their own schoolfellows were disappearing up the road.
“We needn’t run after them﻿—I believe we’ve plenty of time,” said Verity. “We can almost see the station from here. I say, aren’t you fearfully hungry? I’m literally starving. Let’s find a confectioner’s and each buy a bun before we go.”
Both Beatrice and Ingred felt that they required fortifying before they started for home, so they dived into the nearest pastrycook’s and demanded buns. They were eating them rather hastily, when Linda Slater entered the shop in company with a gentleman, evidently her father. She hailed her classmates, and at once began to talk over the match and rejoice at the school victory.
“Who says we’re no good at games now? This has sent up our credit ten percent! I’m proud of the Coll.!”
===Chinese===
姑娘们以最快的速度从曲棍球场鱼贯而出。大约一刻钟后有一班从格罗夫伯里（Grovebury）方向开来的火车。她们三五成群地走向车站，一路还在讨论着比赛的细节。英格丽德（Ingred）、碧翠丝（Beatrice）和薇丽蒂（Verity）恰好在出口处被克林顿（Clintonian）队堵住了去路，只好干等了几分钟才挤出门去；等她们终于通过大门，本校的同学们早已消失在道路尽头。
“咱们用不着追她们——我看时间还宽裕得很。”薇丽蒂说，“从这儿差不多都能望见车站了。喂，我说，你们是不是饿坏了？我可真是饿得前胸贴后背。咱们先找家点心铺，一人买个甜面包再走吧。”
碧翠丝和英格丽德也觉得，动身回家之前得先垫垫肚子，于是钻进最近的一家糕点铺，买了几个甜面包。她们正风卷残云似的吃着，琳达·斯莱特（Linda Slater）陪着一位先生走进店来，那显然是她父亲。她一见同学就打招呼，立刻兴致勃勃地谈起了比赛，为学校的胜利欢呼雀跃。
“现在还有谁敢说咱们打球不行？这一仗把咱们的名声抬高了足足一成！我真为咱们学院骄傲！”

===Original===
“Blossom was A1,” exulted Verity.
“And Janie was simply ripping. Dad thought no end of her. Didn’t you, Dad?”
“Well, I’m glad we made something of a record,” admitted Ingred.
“I say,” declared Beatrice, hastily finishing her bun, “if that clock’s right, we must bolt for our train.”
“As a matter of fact, it’s one minute slow,” exclaimed Linda, consulting her watch. “You’ll have to sprint.”
===Chinese===
“布洛瑟姆（Blossom）打得顶呱呱。”薇丽蒂喜滋滋地说。
“珍妮（Janie）更是棒极了。爸爸对她简直赞不绝口。是不是，爸爸？”
“嗯，我很高兴咱们创下了一项小小的纪录。”英格丽德承认道。
“我说，”碧翠丝一边急急忙忙咽下最后一口面包一边宣布，“要是那座钟没走错，咱们可得撒腿狂奔赶火车了。”
“其实呀，那钟慢了一分钟，”琳达看了看表，大声道，“你们得冲刺才行。”

===Original===
“Aren’t *you* coming?”
“No, we have our car here. It’s outside.”
“Those girls will hardly catch their train,” remarked Mr. Slater to Linda, as the three went to the pay desk to settle for their buns. “Couldn’t we stow them into the car, and take them along with us?”
“Oh, no, Dad!” frowned Linda. “There really isn’t room. You promised you’d call at Brantbury and bring Gerald and Eustace back for the afternoon. We couldn’t cram them all in the car!”
“There isn’t time for them to get the train.”
“Oh, yes! You don’t know how they can run!”
===Chinese===
“*你*不跟我们一起走吗？”
“不了，我们的汽车就在这儿，停在外头呢。”
“那几个姑娘八成赶不上火车了。”那三人走向柜台去付面包钱时，斯莱特先生对琳达说，“咱们能不能把她们塞进车里，捎带她们一程？”
“哎呀，不行，爸爸！”琳达皱起眉头，“车里实在腾不出地方。你答应过去布兰特伯里（Brantbury）接杰拉尔德（Gerald）和尤斯塔斯（Eustace）回来过下午的。总不能把这么多人全硬塞进车里呀！”
“可她们来不及赶上火车了。”
“来得及！您不知道她们能跑得多快呢！”

===Original===
Quite unaware of the kindly offer which had been rejected on their behalf, Beatrice, Verity, and Ingred fled from the shop, and hurried with all possible speed in the direction of the railway station. They could see the train coming along the top of the embankment, and it had drawn up at the platform before they reached the passenger entrance. They were not the only late comers. It was Saturday, and a crowd of work people from various factories near were returning to Grovebury.
In company with a very mixed and motley crew they pushed their way up the long flight of steps. A collector stood at the top, and just as they were nearing their goal, he slammed the gate and refused further admission to the platform. They could hear the whistle, and the general bumping of chains that betokened the starting of the carriages. They were exactly half a minute too late! When the train was well out of the station, the collector once more opened his barrier, and the crowd surged on. The three girls, who disliked pushing among a rough assembly, stood on one side to let the people pass by. There was no hurry now, and no object to be gained by forcing their way ahead. Last of all, therefore, they presented themselves at the gate.
“Tickets, please!” repeated the collector automatically.
All three felt in their pockets, but felt in vain. Return tickets and purses were alike missing, and even penknives and handkerchiefs had vanished, Ingred’s pocket, indeed, was neatly turned inside out. Here was a dilemma! They had evidently been robbed on the stairs by a professional thief, who had appropriated all their portable belongings. In utter consternation they looked at one another.
===Chinese===
碧翠丝、薇丽蒂和英格丽德全然不知有人替她们回绝了一片好意，冲出店铺，拿出浑身解数朝火车站方向飞奔。她们眼见火车沿着路堤顶端开了过来，还没等赶到旅客入口，它已经停稳在站台边。赶不上这班车的并不只她们几个。这天是星期六，附近各家工厂的大批工人也正要返回格罗夫伯里。
她们混杂在形形色色、杂七杂八的人群里，挤着登上长长的台阶。台阶顶上站着一名查票员，眼看她们就要到达，他却砰的一声关上栅门，不再放人进站台。她们听得见汽笛声，还有车轮启动前链条一阵阵的撞击声。她们恰恰迟了半分钟！等火车开出了车站，查票员才重新打开栅栏，人群随即又涌了上去。三个姑娘不喜欢在粗鲁的人群里推推搡搡，便站在一旁，先让人流通过。此刻已经不着急了，抢先挤过去也没有任何意义。于是，她们最后一个来到栅门前。
“请出示车票！”查票员机械地重复道。
三个人都往口袋里掏，却掏了个空。来回车票和钱包一概不见了，连铅笔小刀和手帕也无影无踪——英格丽德的口袋干脆被人整整齐齐地翻了个里朝外。这可怎么办！她们显然是在楼梯上遭了职业扒手的毒手，随身能拿的东西全被席卷一空。她们大惊失色，面面相觑。

===Original===
“We’ve lost our tickets!” faltered Beatrice.
“They’ve been stolen!” added Ingred.
“Do please let us through!” entreated Verity.
In ordinary circumstances the collector would no doubt have listened to the girl’s story, and taken them to interview the stationmaster, but today he had to do double duty, and could scarcely cope with the extra work. He had to deal with crowds, and to keep a sharp eye to see that no one defrauded the railway company by travelling without paying the fare. A train was due at the next moment on the other side of the platform, and his services were urgently required at the opposite exit.
“Haven’t you got your tickets?” he demanded curtly. “Then I must close the gate. No one’s allowed on the platform without tickets.”
The advancing train whistled as it ran through the cutting, and, disregarding the girls’ remonstrances, the official locked the barrier. He bolted across the line in front of the engine, just in time to take his place at the other gateway before the rush of passengers began, and probably never gave another thought to the three whom he had just excluded. Left shut out on the top of the station steps, the unlucky trio ruefully reviewed the situation.
===Chinese===
“我们的车票丢了！”碧翠丝结结巴巴地说。
“是被人偷走了！”英格丽德补上一句。
“求求您放我们进去吧！”薇丽蒂恳求道。
换作平常，查票员无疑会听姑娘们把话说完，再带她们去见站长，可今天他一个人得当两个人使，实在应付不过来这额外的活计。他既要招呼大批旅客，又要睁大眼睛提防有人不买票白坐车，占铁路公司的便宜。眼下一班火车马上要在站台另一侧进站，对面那个出口也急需他去把守。
“没拿车票？”他没好气地问，“那我只好关门了。没有车票，谁也不准上站台。”
正进站的火车鸣着汽笛穿过路堑，那位站员不顾姑娘们的连声抗议，锁上了栅栏。他从火车头前面飞跑着横穿铁轨，赶在旅客涌出之前及时到达对面闸口就位，多半再没想过刚被他拒之门外的三个人。倒霉的三人组被关在车站台阶顶上，愁眉苦脸地合计着眼下的处境。

===Original===
“What *are* we to do?” demanded Ingred breathlessly.
“Goodness only knows!” sighed Verity.
“We’re in a very awkward fix!” admitted Beatrice.
They were much too far from Grovebury to make walking possible.
“I wonder Miss Giles didn’t miss us!” fretted Verity, trying to throw the blame on somebody.
“It isn’t her fault﻿—fair play to her!” urged Beatrice. “She wasn’t looking after us officially today, you know. On Saturdays we’re supposed to be on our own.”
===Chinese===
“我们*到底*该怎么办哪？”英格丽德气喘吁吁地问。
“天知道！”薇丽蒂叹了口气。
“咱们这回可真是陷进死角了！”碧翠丝承认道。
这里离格罗夫伯里实在太远，靠两条腿走回去是不可能的了。
“真奇怪，贾尔斯小姐（Miss Giles）居然没发觉我们不见了！”薇丽蒂焦躁地抱怨，一心想把责任推到别人头上。
“这不能怪她——得对她公平些！”碧翠丝赶紧说，“要知道，今天她并没有正式负责照看咱们。星期六，我们本来就该自己照管自己。”

===Original===
“I lay the blame on buns!” said Ingred. “We’d have kept with the rest of the school if we hadn’t stopped at that confectioner’s.”
“Well, it’s no use crying over spilt milk now! What we’ve got to do is to find some means of getting home. We can’t stay here all day.”
“I believe it’s not very far to Waverley from Denscourt,” ventured Beatrice. “If we can manage to walk, I know some people who live at a house there. I’d ask them to lend us our fares, and we could catch a train at Waverley station.”
The idea seemed feasible, and, as it was the only one that suggested itself, they unanimously decided to adopt it. They walked down the steps again, therefore, on to the high road, and, stopping a girl who was passing, asked the way to Waverley.
“It’s a good four miles by the road, but it’s only about two by the fields,” she volunteered in reply. “I think you’d find the path. You go down the road to the right, and turn through the first gate across a field to a farm. Then you keep along the river bank, on the left. You can’t miss it.”
To save two miles in their present predicament was a matter of importance, and they all felt that they would greatly prefer walking through fields to tramping along a dusty high road. Thanking their informant, they took her advice, and set off in the direction which she indicated. After all, the affair was rather an adventure.
===Chinese===
“依我看，罪魁祸首就是面包！”英格丽德说，“要不是在那家点心铺停了一停，我们早就跟着全校大部队一块儿走了。”
“唉，如今后悔也晚了！咱们眼下要做的是想个法子回家，总不能在这儿干待一整天吧。”
“我记得从登斯考特（Denscourt）到韦弗利（Waverley）并不太远，”碧翠丝试探着说，“要是腿脚还走得动，我认识一家人就住在那儿。我想请他们借我们几个车钱，然后在韦弗利车站搭火车。”
这个主意看来可行，何况一时也想不出别的法子，她们便一致决定照办。于是她们重新走下台阶，来到大路上，拦住一位路过的姑娘，打听去韦弗利的路。
“走大路足足四英里，穿田野只有两英里上下，”她热心地答道，“你们能找到那条小路的。顺着大路朝右走，穿过头一道栅门，横过一块田就到一个农庄。然后沿着河岸一直走，靠左手边。错不了。”
眼下这步田地，能省下两英里可是件大事，何况她们都觉得，与其在尘土飞扬的大路上跋涉，倒不如穿田野走来得惬意。她们谢过指路的姑娘，依着她的建议，朝她指的方向出发了。说到底，这事儿倒也算得上一场小小的奇遇。

===Original===
“The Mortons are sure to offer us lunch when we get there,” affirmed Beatrice; “of course we shall be fearfully late home, and our people will be getting very anxious about us, but we can’t help that. I was to have gone to a matinée of *Carmen* this afternoon, but it’s off, naturally! I expect Doris will use my ticket, when I don’t turn up.”
“I meant to wash our dog when I got back!” laughed Ingred. “He’ll have to look dirty on Sunday, now.”
“And I meant to do a hundred things; but what’s the use of talking about them now?” groaned Verity. “Here’s our farm, and that appears to be the river over there. Didn’t that girl say: ‘Keep along to the left’? Perhaps we’d better ask again.”
They verified their instructions from a boy who was standing in the farmyard, whittling a stick, and trudged away over a stubble field and through a turnstile gate. It was quite pretty along the path by the river. There was a tall hedge where hips and haws showed red, and a grassy border where a few wild flowers still bloomed. The sun shed a soft golden autumnal haze over the fields and bushes and the lines of yellow trees.
The girls rather enjoyed themselves; it was an unexpected country excursion, and had all the charm of novelty. They walked about half a mile, chatting about school matters as they went, then suddenly they were confronted by an alternative. A bridge spanned the river, and the broad, well-trodden path along which they had come turned over the bridge. There was indeed a track that continued along the left bank, but it was overgrown, and looked little used. Which were they to take?
===Chinese===
“咱们一到，莫顿家（the Mortons）准会留我们吃午饭，”碧翠丝满有把握地说，“当然，我们到家要晚得吓人，家里人准会急得团团转，可这也是没法子的事。我今天下午本来要看《卡门》（Carmen）日场的，这下自然去不成了！我猜等我到不了场，多丽丝（Doris）就会拿我的票去用。”
“我本来打算回去给咱们的狗洗个澡呢！”英格丽德笑道，“这下它星期天只好一身脏毛见人了。”
“我本来还打算做一百件事呢，可现在还提它们做什么？”薇丽蒂唉声叹气，“喏，农庄到了，那边大概就是那条河。那姑娘是不是说过‘一直靠左走’？咱们最好还是再问问。”
她们向一个站在农家院里削木棍的男孩核实了路线，便深一脚浅一脚地走过一片收割后的茬子地，穿过一道旋转栅门。沿河的小路景致相当不错。高高的树篱上，蔷薇果和山楂果红得醒目；长草的路边上，还有几朵野花开着。太阳给田野、灌木和一排排泛黄的树木罩上一层柔和的金色秋霭。
姑娘们倒也走得兴致盎然；这是一场不期而至的乡间远足，处处透着新鲜的魅力。她们走了约莫半英里，一路聊着学校里的事，忽然，一个岔口摆在了面前。河上架着一座桥，她们一路走来的那条被踩得瓷实的大道过了桥往对岸去。左边河岸上倒确实有一条小径继续延伸，却杂草丛生，看样子少有人走。该走哪一条呢？

===Original===
That was a question which required discussion.
“The girl said: ‘keep along the river bank on the left,’ ” urged Ingred.
“Yet the path so plainly goes across here,” demurred Verity.
“That’s certainly the left bank, but that way looks as if it led to nowhere,” vacillated Beatrice.
“Can’t we ask anybody?”
“There isn’t a soul in sight.”
===Chinese===
这个问题非得讨论一番不可。
“那姑娘说过：‘沿着河岸靠左一直走。’”英格丽德力争。
“可这路明摆着是从这儿过桥呀。”薇丽蒂表示异议。
“那边确实是左岸，可那条道瞧着就像哪儿也通不了。”碧翠丝摇摆不定。
“咱们就不能找个人问问吗？”
“四下里连个人影也没有。”

===Original===
“Isn’t there a signpost?”
“Nothing of the sort.”
“Then which way *shall* we go?”
“Better take votes on it.”
“Right-o! I’m for ‘bypath meadow.’ ”
“And I’m for the ‘king’s highway.’ ”
===Chinese===
“那有没有路标？”
“压根儿没有那玩意儿。”
“那我们*究竟*走哪条？”
“最好投票表决。”
“好嘞！我投‘田间小径’一票。”
“我投‘国王大道’一票。”

===Original===
“So am I, so we’re two to one!”
“I’ll give in, then,” said Ingred, “only I’ve a sort of feeling we’re going wrong, all the same!”
The new path led along the opposite bank, and was very much a replica of the former. It ran on and on for what seemed quite a long distance, but they met nobody from whom they could inquire the way. For nearly a quarter of a mile a belt of trees obscured the view, and when at last the prospect could once more be seen, Beatrice stopped short with a groan of despair. On the other side of the water was the unmistakable spire of Waverley church.
“We’ve come wrong, after all!”
“Oh, good night! So we have!”
“What an absolute swindle!”
===Chinese===
“我也投大道，二比一，多数取胜！”
“那我让步，”英格丽德说，“只是我心里总隐隐觉得，咱们这么走不对头！”
新的路沿着对岸延伸，同先前那条几乎是一个模子里出来的。它一直往前，似乎走了老远，她们却没遇上一个可以问路的人。将近四分之一英里的路段上，一带树木挡住了视线；等到视野重新开阔，碧翠丝一声绝望的呻吟，猛地收住了脚。河对岸赫然立着韦弗利教堂的尖塔，错不了。
“到头来咱们还是走错了！”
“哎呀，我的天！可不是嘛！”
“这也太坑人了！”

===Original===
The girls were certainly not in luck that day. They had missed their path as effectually as they had missed their train. The chimneys of Waverley were in sight, but separated from them by a wide stream, and unless they were prepared to wade, swim, or fly, there was no way of reaching the village.
“There’s nothing for it but to turn back!”
“Why, but that’s *miles*!”
“Are you sure it’s Waverley over there? Can we ask anybody?”
“No one to ask, worse luck!”
“Yes, there is! I can see some people coming along in a boat.”
===Chinese===
这一天，姑娘们的运气实在糟透了。她们把小路错过去，正如把火车误过去一样干净彻底。韦弗利的烟囱近在眼前，中间却横着一条宽阔的河，除非她们打算蹚水、游泳，或者飞过去，否则没有任何法子进村。
“没办法，只好往回走了！”
“哎呀，那可有*好几英里*呢！”
“你敢肯定那边就是韦弗利吗？能找人问问吗？”
“倒霉就倒在，一个人也碰不上！”
“有哇！我看见有条船，船上坐着几个人，正往这边来呢。”

===Original===
Rendered desperate by the emergency, Ingred struggled through the reeds to the very edge of the river, and lifted up her voice in an agonized cry of “Help!”
A punt was drifting slowly with the current, and its occupants, a lady and gentleman, looked with surprise at the agitated girl who was hailing them from the bank. The gentleman at once paddled in her direction, and, running his little craft among the reeds, inquired what was the matter.
“Oh, please, is that Waverley over there?” asked Ingred anxiously. “We’ve lost our way, and we’ve walked miles! Is there any bridge near?”
“That’s certainly Waverley, but there’s no bridge till you come to one a mile and a half down stream.”
Ingred’s face was tragic. She turned to Beatrice and Verity, who had joined her.
“It’s no use! We shall have to go back!”
===Chinese===
事出紧急，英格丽德也顾不得许多，拨开芦苇直挤到河边，扯开嗓子凄声高呼：“救命啊！”
一条方头平底船正顺流缓缓漂来，船上一男一女惊讶地望着岸边这个焦急招呼他们的姑娘。绅士当即把船朝她撑过来，让小船滑进芦苇丛，问出了什么事。
“哦，请问，那边是韦弗利吗？”英格丽德急切地问，“我们迷了路，已经走了好几英里啦！附近有桥吗？”
“那确实是韦弗利，不过要往下游走一英里半才有一座桥。”
英格丽德一脸悲戚。她转向已经赶上来的碧翠丝和薇丽蒂。
“没指望了！我们只好回去！”

===Original===
But the lady was whispering something to the gentleman, and he beckoned to the girls with a smile.
“Don’t run away!” he said. “Look here, we’ll punt you across if you like.”
“Like!” The girls hardly knew how to express their gratitude.
“The three of you’d be too heavy a load. I think I’d better take just one at a time. Can you manage to get in? It’s rather swampy here. Give me your hand!”
Ingred splashed ankle deep in oozy mud as she scrambled on board, but that was a trifle compared with the relief of being ferried over the river. Her knight-errant was neither young nor handsome, being, indeed, rather bald and stout, but no orthodox interesting hero of fiction could have been more welcome at the moment. She tendered her utmost thanks as she landed, again with damage to her shoes, on the rushy bank opposite. Their friends in need, having successfully punted over Beatrice and Verity also, bade them a laughing goodbye, and resumed their easy course down stream, leaving three very grateful girls behind them.
“That’s helped us out of a fix! Don’t say again we’ve no luck!” cried Beatrice, wiping her boots carefully on the grass.
===Chinese===
可那位女士正凑在绅士耳边低语着什么，绅士微笑着朝姑娘们招了招手。
“别急着走！”他说，“听我说，要是你们愿意，我们撑船送你们过河。”
“愿意！”姑娘们简直不知道该如何表达感激之情。
“你们三个一起上，分量可太重了。我看还是一次载一个为好。你们上得来吗？这一带挺泥泞的。把手给我！”
英格丽德爬上船时，脚踝深陷烂泥，溅得一片泥点，可比起被摆渡过河的如释重负，这点代价不过小事一桩。她这位游侠骑士既不年轻，也不英俊——实际上头顶已相当光秃，身材也颇发福——可此时此刻，哪怕是小说里最正统的迷人男主角，也不见得比他更受欢迎。她登上灯芯草丛生的对岸——鞋子再度惨遭殃及——千恩万谢。这对雪中送炭的朋友又相继把碧翠丝和薇丽蒂稳稳撑过河来，笑吟吟地道了别，悠悠然继续顺流而下，留下三个感激不尽的姑娘。
“这下可解了围！看你们往后还敢说咱们没运气！”碧翠丝喊道，一面在草地上仔细蹭净靴子。

===Original===
“They were angels in disguise!” sighed Ingred.
“Rather stout angels!” chuckled Verity. “Now, how are we going to get out of this field?”
“Over the hedge, I suppose. There’s a piece of fence that looks climbable!” returned Beatrice, swinging herself up with elephantine grace, and dropping with a heavy thud on the other side. “Oh! good biz! We’re on a cinder path!”
They were indeed in a back lane which led at the bottom of some gardens, then behind a row of stables, and finally through a gate on to the high road.
“I know where we are now!” exclaimed Beatrice gleefully. “It’s only quite a short way to the Morton’s. They live in the next terrace but two. I believe we’re within measurable distance of some lunch.”
This was such good news that they strode along in renewed spirits. Considering all, they thought the adventure was turning out well. A meal would undoubtedly be most acceptable, if Beatrice’s friends were hospitable enough to offer it.
===Chinese===
“他们简直是乔装下凡的天使！”英格丽德叹道。
“还是相当富态的天使！”薇丽蒂咯咯直笑，“好了，咱们怎么从这块田里出去？”
“翻树篱呗，那儿有一截栅栏瞧着还爬得过去！”碧翠丝答道，以大象一般的优雅身姿攀了上去，咕咚一声沉沉落在另一边。“哦！妙极了！咱们踩上煤渣小路了！”
她们确实走在一条后巷里，巷子先沿着几户人家花园的尽头延伸，接着绕到一排马厩后面，最后穿过一道门，通上大路。
“我知道咱们眼下在哪儿了！”碧翠丝喜形于色地叫道，“去莫顿家只剩很短一截路了。他们家就在前面隔两排的排屋里。依我看，一顿午饭已经近在眼前了。”
这消息太提气了，她们重新振作精神，大步流星地赶起路来。通盘算来，她们觉得这场奇遇正往好的方向转。只要碧翠丝的朋友够好客、肯留饭，一顿饭无疑是最受欢迎不过的了。

===Original===
“It’s the fourth house,” said Beatrice, “the one with the copper beech over the gate. Linden Lea﻿—yes, here we are! Oh, I say, what are all the blinds down for?”
The girls faced each other blankly.
“Is anyone dead?” faltered Ingred.
“I’ll ring and inquire, at any rate,” murmured Beatrice.
So she rang, and rang again and yet again. She could hear the bell clanging quite plainly and unmistakably somewhere in the back regions, yet nobody came to the door.
“It’s funny! I don’t hear anybody in the house either,” she remarked. “Their dog generally barks at the least sound.”
===Chinese===
“是第四栋房子，”碧翠丝说，“门口上头长着紫叶山毛榉的那栋。林登利（Linden Lea）——没错，就是这儿！咦，我说，怎么百叶窗全放下来了？”
姑娘们面面相觑，一脸茫然。
“莫不是……家里死了人？”英格丽德声音发颤地问。
“不管怎么说，我按按铃问问。”碧翠丝低声道。
于是她按了铃，按了一遍又一遍。她能清清楚楚、明明白白地听见铃声在屋子后部什么地方当当作响，可就是没有人前来开门。
“真怪！屋里也听不见一点人声。”她说，“他们家那条狗，平时稍有一点动静就要叫的。”

===Original===
At that moment a small face peeped over the top of the wall which divided the garden from that of the next house, and a childish voice asked:
“Do you want the Mortons?”
“Yes. Isn’t anybody in?”
“They’re all gone away to Llandudno, for a month.”
“All? Isn’t anyone here?”
“No, the house is locked up.”
===Chinese===
就在这时，一张小脸从隔开这座花园与邻家花园的墙头上探出来，一个孩子的声音问道：
“你们找莫顿家的人吗？”
“是啊。家里没人吗？”
“他们全家都去兰迪德诺（Llandudno）了，要去一个月呢。”
“全家？一个人也没留下？”
“没有，房子都锁上了。”

===Original===
Here a warning call of “Willie!” caused their informant to disappear as suddenly as he had come, but the girls had heard enough. All their hopes were suddenly blighted. They had arrived at the end of their journey only to draw a blank. They were indeed in a worse position than when they had missed the train at Denscourt, for they were farther from home, and it was much later. Almost ready to cry, they turned down the garden again.
“We’ve got to get home tonight somehow!” said Ingred through her set teeth.
“Shall we go to the police station?” quavered Verity.
“And give ourselves up like lost children? No, it’s too undignified! Wait a moment, I’ve got an idea!” said Beatrice. “We passed the post office just now, and I noticed it had a ‘Public Telephone.’ I’ll ring up Mother and tell her where we are, and ask her to come over for us.”
“But you can’t telephone for nothing, and we haven’t so much as a solitary penny amongst us!”
“I know. I thought I’d explain that to the people at the post office, and ask them to let me have the call, and Mother will pay when she comes. I could give them my watch as a security.”
===Chinese===
这时一声“威利（Willie）！”的呼喊传来，小家伙来得突然，去得也仓促，眨眼就不见了，不过姑娘们该听的已经听够了。她们满腔的希望骤然枯萎。一路走到旅程的尽头，却扑了个空。说实话，眼下的处境比在登斯考特误车那会儿更糟：离家更远，时候也更晚。她们几乎要哭出来，转身沿花园小径折返。
“今晚说什么也得想法子回家！”英格丽德咬着牙说。
“咱们去警察局好不好？”薇丽蒂声音打颤。
“像走丢的孩子似的去投案？不行，太失体面了！等一等，我有主意了！”碧翠丝说，“咱们刚才路过邮局，我注意到那儿有‘公用电话’。我打电话给妈妈，告诉她咱们在哪儿，请她过来接咱们。”
“可打电话不是白打的呀，咱们三个人身上连一个子儿都没有！”
“我知道。我想把情形跟邮局的人说清楚，求他们让我先打，等妈妈来了再付钱。我可以把我的表押在他们那儿作担保。”

===Original===
“It’s worth trying!”
So, with just a little grain of hope, they retraced their steps to the post office, which was also a stationer’s and newsagent’s. Nobody was in the shop, but when the girls thumped on the counter a rosy-cheeked young person appeared from the back regions.
“Want to telephone without paying? It’s against the post office rules,” she snapped, as Beatrice briefly explained the circumstances.
“My mother will pay when she comes, and if you’d take my watch﻿—”
“I can’t go against post office rules! All calls must be paid for beforehand. That’s our instructions.”
“But just for once﻿—”
“What’s the matter, Doris?” asked a voice, and a kindly-looking little man emerged from the back parlor, wiping his mouth hastily, and took his place behind the counter. Beatrice turned to him with eagerness, and again stated the urgency of their peculiar situation.
===Chinese===
“值得一试！”
于是，她们揣着仅存的一丝希望，原路折回邮局。这家邮局兼营文具和报刊。店里空无一人，可姑娘们一敲柜台，一个脸颊红扑扑的年轻姑娘便从后屋走了出来。
“想打电话不付钱？这可违反邮局的规定。”听碧翠丝简单说明了缘由，她劈头就没好气地回了一句。
“我妈妈来了就会付钱的，要是您肯收下我的表——”
“我不能坏了邮局的规矩！所有电话都得预先付钱。这是上头的规定。”
“可就通融这一回——”
“怎么回事，多丽丝？”一个声音问道。一位面容和善的小个子男人从后屋出来，匆匆擦了擦嘴，走到柜台后站定。碧翠丝急忙转向他，又把她们这桩非同小可的窘况急切地讲了一遍。

===Original===
“Well, of course we’ve our instructions from the post office, and we’ve got to account for the calls, but in this particular case we might let you have one, and pay afterwards,” he replied. “Oh, never mind the watch; it’s all right!”
Beatrice lost no time in ringing up Number 167 Grovebury, and to her immense delight, when she got the connection, she heard her mother’s voice at the instrument. A short explanation was all that was necessary.
“Stay where you are at the Waverley post office, and I will get a taxi and fetch you myself immediately,” returned Mrs. Jackson. “It’s the greatest relief to know what has become of you. I was going to ring up the police station, and describe you as ‘missing!’ ”
The girls had to wait nearly three-quarters of an hour before the taxi made its appearance, and the welcome form of Mrs. Jackson stepped out of it. She paid what was owing for the call, thanked the postmaster for his civility, and hustled the girls into the conveyance as quickly as possible.
“I suppose girls will be girls,” she said, “but I think you’ve been very silly ones today! Why didn’t you keep with the rest of the school, as you ought to have done?”
===Chinese===
“呃，我们自然有邮局的章程，每通电话都得报账，不过眼下这种情形，就让你们先打一通，回头再付，也未尝不可。”他答道，“哦，表就免了；没关系的！”
碧翠丝立刻给格罗夫伯里167号挂了电话，令她大喜过望的是，电话一接通，听筒里就传来了妈妈的声音。三言两语解释一下就够了。
“你们就待在韦弗利邮局别动，我马上叫辆出租汽车，亲自去接你们。”杰克逊太太（Mrs. Jackson）回话道，“总算知道你们的下落了，真是莫大的宽慰。我本来正要打电话到警察局，把你们当作‘失踪人口’报上去呢！”
姑娘们等了将近三刻钟，出租汽车才终于露面，杰克逊太太那备受欢迎的身影从车上下来。她付清了电话费，谢过邮政所长的殷勤关照，便尽快把姑娘们塞进了车里。
“俗话说，女孩子终归是女孩子，”她说，“可我认为，你们今天傻得也实在出格了！本来就该跟着全校大部队一起走，你们为什么不呢？”

===Original===
“It sounds a most horrible greedy confession,” replied Beatrice guiltily, “but I’m afraid it was all the fault of﻿—buns! They just threw us late, and we missed the others. We’ll never buy buns again! Never! Never! *O peccavi!* We have sinned!”
And she looked so humorously contrite that Mrs. Jackson, who was inclined to scold, laughed in spite of herself, and forgave the delinquents.
“On condition that such a thing doesn’t happen again!” she declared.
“Trust us! We wouldn’t go through such an experience again for all the buns in the world! Next time we’ll cling to the College apron strings like﻿—like﻿—”
“Like adhesive sticking-plaster!” supplied Ingred gently.
“Or oysters to a mermaid’s tail!” murmured Verity.
===Chinese===
“这份供词听起来馋得吓人，”碧翠丝心虚地答道，“可恐怕这全是——面包惹的祸！就为了它们，咱们才晚了一步，落下了大部队。我们再也不买面包了！绝不！绝不！*噢，我认罪！*我们犯了罪！”
她那副悔罪的模样实在滑稽，本打算训斥几句的杰克逊太太也不由自主地笑了，饶恕了这几个小闯祸精。
“条件是这种事再也不许发生！”她郑重宣布。
“放心吧！天底下的面包全给我们，我们也不肯再经历一回这种事了！下回我们要紧紧拽住学院的围裙带，就像——就像——”
“就像黏膏药似的！”英格丽德轻声替她补上。
“要不就像扒着美人鱼尾巴的牡蛎！”薇丽蒂喃喃道。

## XVIII Bess at Home / XVIII 贝丝在家

===Original===
The Pilgrims, after a glorious tramp down the dale of Beechcombe, reached Pursborough without further adventure, and spent the night there. They gave an hour next morning to inspecting the glorious old church and the ruins of the castle, then once more resumed the Roman road. It was the last day of their tour, so they made the best of it. They explored some delightful woods, followed the course of a fascinating stream, ate their lunch in a picturesque quarry, had an early tea at a wayside inn which rivalled “The Pelican” in quaintness, and finally reached Ribstang in time to catch the 5:20 train to Grovebury. The conclusion of the excursion meant the close of the holiday, for school would begin again on the following Monday. Everybody had enjoyed it immensely, and everybody was only too sorry it was over. To Ingred it marked an epoch. She had suddenly made friends with Bess Haselford. Now she viewed Bess with unprejudiced eyes she realized what an exceedingly nice and attractive girl she really was. The adventure in the field had flung them together, and﻿—much to the astonishment of the others, who did not know their secret﻿—they had walked the whole way from Pursborough to Ribstang in each other’s company.
“I can’t make out Ingred!” declared Verity. “Here she’s been abusing Bess, and calling her a bounder, and now she’s hanging on her arm! The way some people turn round is really most extraordinary﻿—”
“ ‘There’s naught so queer as folks!’ ” quoted Linda. “Glad Ingred’s come to her senses, at any rate. I always thought she was perfectly beastly to Bess!”
“So she was. I wonder Bess will put up with her now. I’m sure I wouldn’t!”
===Chinese===
“朝圣者”一行（The Pilgrims）沿着比奇科姆（Beechcombe）山谷畅快地徒步而下，一路再无波折，抵达珀斯伯勒，在那里过了一夜。第二天上午，她们花了一个钟头参观那座宏伟的古老教堂和城堡遗迹，随后重新踏上罗马古道。这是此行的最后一天，她们便尽情地享受了一番。她们探访了几片宜人的树林，循着一条迷人的溪流前行，在风景如画的采石场里吃了午饭，赶早在一家古雅程度不输“鹈鹕客栈”（The Pelican）的路边小店喝了下午茶，最后及时赶到里布斯唐（Ribstang），搭上五点二十分开往格罗夫伯里的火车。远足的结束便意味着假期的终结，因为下周一就要开学了。人人都玩得痛快极了，也人人都惋惜它就此落幕。而对英格丽德来说，这一趟更是个划时代的转折。她忽然间和贝丝·哈泽尔福德（Bess Haselford）交上了朋友。如今她不带成见地打量贝丝，才认识到她实在是个极其可爱、极有魅力的姑娘。田野里的那场奇遇把两人拴到了一起，于是——让不知内情的同伴们大为诧异的是——从珀斯伯勒到里布斯唐，她们俩竟一路结伴同行。
“我真搞不懂英格丽德！”薇丽蒂宣称，“她先前还一个劲儿数落贝丝，骂人家是个粗俗鬼，现在倒好，成天挽着人家的胳膊！有些人变起脸来之快，真叫人叹为观止——”
“‘天底下再没有比人更古怪的东西啦！’”琳达引了一句俗语，“不管怎么说，英格丽德总算回过味儿来了。我一向觉得她对贝丝真是太差劲了！”
“可不是嘛。真不知道贝丝这会儿怎么还容忍得了她。换了我，肯定不干！”

===Original===
Bess, however, was of a forgiving disposition, and let bygones be bygones. It is the only plan at schools, for girls are generally so frank in the nature of their remarks that if you begin to treasure up the disagreeable things said to you, and let them rankle, you will probably find yourself without a chum in the world. Though the fashion may be for plain speaking, it is often a matter of mood, and the mate who genuinely believes you a “blighter” one day, will claim you as a “mascot” with equal persuasion on the next. It is all part of the wholesome rough-and-tumble of your education, and proves of as much use in training you and rounding your projecting corners as the lessons you learn in your form. The girls thought Ingred’s new infatuation would soon wear off, but it had come to stay. She herself was quite surprised at the force of the attraction. It was almost like falling in love. She marched with Bess at drilling, chose her for her partner at tennis, and would have changed desks to sit next to her, had not Miss Strong refused permission. As a natural result of this new state of affairs came a shy invitation from Bess asking Ingred to tea at Rotherwood. After the many previous refusals she would hardly have ventured to give in but for several hints which paved the way. Circumstances, however, alter cases, and Ingred, who had declared that nothing should induce her to set foot in her old home, was now all eagerness to go. She was delighted to find that she was to be the only guest. She felt that on this particular visit even Verity would be de trop.
On a certain Tuesday afternoon, therefore, with full permission from Miss Burd, she absented herself from the hostel tea-table, and walked home with Bess instead. It gave her quite a thrill to turn in at the familiar gate of Rotherwood. The lawns were in beautiful order, and the beds gay with tulips, aubrietias, forget-me-nots, and a lovely show of hyacinths. So far from being neglected, the place seemed even better kept than in the old days. The house, with its pretty modern black-and-white front, its many gables, and its cheerful red-tiled roof, looked the same as formerly; but indoors there were great changes. The hall, which used to be Moorish, was now hung with tapestry, and furnished in old oak; the drawing-room was yellow instead of blue, with a big brocade-covered couch and a Chappell piano; the dining-room had rows of bookcases and some good oil-paintings; the morning-room was a cheerful chintz boudoir with a gilt mirror and Chippendale chairs; the conservatory was full of choice flowers, and an aviary had been added to it.
“Mother is so fond of birds,” explained Bess. “They amuse her when her head’s bad and she doesn’t care to see anybody. She’s made most of them wonderfully tame.”
===Chinese===
不过贝丝天性宽厚，不念旧恶。在学校里，这也是唯一的相处之道：女孩子说话向来直率，你要是把那些刺耳的话一一记在心里、任其隐隐作痛，到头来恐怕会发现自己在这世上一个知己也没有。有话直说固然是一时风气，其实多半看心情：昨天还真心当你是“讨厌鬼”的伙伴，今天又能同样言之凿凿地认你做她的“福星”。这些都是你成长教育中有益的摸爬滚打，对于锤炼你、磨圆你冒尖的棱角，其用处丝毫不亚于你在班里学的功课。姑娘们原以为英格丽德这股新鲜劲儿很快就会过去，谁料它偏偏扎下根来。连她自己都为这股吸引力的强烈而吃惊。这简直像堕入情网一般。上操时她与贝丝并肩而行，打网球时挑她做搭档，若不是斯特朗小姐不点头，她连课桌都要换到贝丝旁边去。这番新局面顺理成章的结果，便是贝丝怯生生地递来邀请，请英格丽德去罗瑟伍德喝茶。此前屡遭回绝，若不是有几句话先探了口风、铺好了路，她几乎不敢再开这个口。然而此一时彼一时，英格丽德先前还声言说什么也不肯再踏进旧居一步，如今却巴不得立刻就去。听说自己将是唯一的客人，她更是满心欢喜。她觉得这一趟登门，就连薇丽蒂跟了去也是多余的。
于是在某个星期二的下午，她征得伯德小姐的准许，没去寄宿舍用茶点，而是同贝丝一道回她家去。从罗瑟伍德那扇熟悉的大门拐进去时，她心头一阵激荡。草坪修剪得整整齐齐，花坛里郁金香、南庭霁、勿忘我开得热热闹闹，还有一片明媚动人的风信子。这宅子非但不见荒疏，反倒比往昔打理得更好。房子还是从前的模样——漂亮的现代式黑白门面、重重山墙、明快的红瓦屋顶；屋里却已大变样了。原先摩尔风格的大厅如今挂起织锦挂毯，摆着老橡木家具；客厅由蓝改成黄色，添了一张包锦缎的大长沙发和一架查佩尔钢琴；餐厅立起一排排书柜，挂着几幅上好的油画；晨间起居室成了明快的印花棉布小闺房，配着镀金镜子和齐彭代尔式椅子；暖房里名花济济，还添建了一座鸟舍。
“妈妈特别喜欢鸟儿，”贝丝解释道，“她头疼犯病、不愿见人的时候，鸟儿能给她解闷。大半的鸟儿都被她驯得乖顺极了。”

===Original===
Mrs. Haselford proved to be a gentle pleasant lady who shook hands kindly with Ingred, then excused herself on the score of ill-health, and retired to her room, leaving the girls to have tea by themselves.
“Mother’s never been really well for three years,” said Bess. “Not since Bert and Larry﻿—”
She did not finish her sentence, but her eyes turned to the wall where hung two portraits of lads in khaki. Ingred understood. She knew that Bess had lost both brothers in the war, and she had heard that poor Mrs. Haselford had shut herself up in her grief and refused all comfort, sometimes even to the extent of remaining for days upstairs, and neglecting the company of husband and child. Her attitude to Bess was often peculiar, it was almost as if she resented her daughter being left when her adored boys had been taken from her. It began to dawn on Ingred how very lonely her friend must be. She had secretly envied her the possession of Rotherwood, but now she realized how little the house itself would mean without the happy home life in which brothers and sister had borne their part.
“I’d rather have the bungalow with the family, than Rotherwood all alone!” she ruminated. “As for Muvkins, she’s one in a million. I believe she’d be cheery in a coal cellar, so long as she’d a solitary chick to keep under her wing. Why, if we’d lost *our* boys, she’d have been trying to make it up to Queenie and me for not having brothers. I know her! That’s her way!”
===Chinese===
哈泽尔福德太太（Mrs. Haselford）是位温婉和善的太太，她亲切地同英格丽德握了握手，随即以身体不适告退，回自己房里去了，留下两个姑娘自家用茶。
“妈妈三年来就没真正好过，”贝丝说，“打从伯特（Bert）和拉里（Larry）——”
她没有把话说完，目光却转向墙上，那里挂着两幅穿卡其军装的少年肖像。英格丽德明白了。她知道贝丝的两个哥哥都牺牲在这场战争里，也听说可怜的哈泽尔福德太太把自己关在哀伤里，拒绝一切安慰，有时甚至一连几天待在楼上，连丈夫和女儿也不愿亲近。她对贝丝的态度常常很古怪，几乎像是在怨怪：她心爱的儿子们被夺走了，凭什么女儿偏偏留了下来。英格丽德这才渐渐懂得，她的朋友该有多么孤单。她曾暗暗羡慕贝丝坐拥罗瑟伍德，此刻却明白了：若没有那份兄妹姊妹共享天伦的家常日子，这房子本身又算得了什么呢。
“宁肯一家人挤在小平房里，也不愿独个儿守着罗瑟伍德！”她暗自思量，“至于老妈咪（Muvkins），她可是万里挑一。我相信哪怕把她放进煤窖，只要翅膀底下还有一只小鸡可护，她也能乐呵呵的。哼，倘若失去的是*我们的*哥哥，她准会想方设法补偿我和奎妮（Queenie），弥补我们没有兄弟的缺憾。我太了解她了！她就是那样的人！”

===Original===
Bess had much to show to her visitor when tea in the dainty morning-room was over. There were her books, and her photographs and postcard albums, and all kinds of girlish possessions, and a cocker spaniel with three puppies as fat as roly-poly puddings, and a fern-case opening out of one of her bedroom windows, and a collection of pressed wild flowers, and a green parroquet that would sit on her wrist, and allow her to stroke its head, though it snapped at strangers. They had been working upwards through the house, and finally Bess led the way to the top landing of all. She paused for a moment before the door of an attic room.
“I expect you’ll know this place!” she remarked shyly, ushering in her guest.
Ingred looked round in amazement. It was a little sanctum which she and Quenrede had shared in the old days as a kind of studio. Here they had been allowed to try experiments in poker work, painting, fret-carving, spatter-work, or any other operations which were considered too messy to be performed in the schoolroom downstairs. They had loved their “den,” as they called it, and had taken a particular pleasure in covering its walls with pictures, cut, most of them, from magazines, and stuck on with glue or paste. During the occupation of Rotherwood by the “Red Cross,” this room had been locked up, and Ingred had imagined that Mr. Haselford would have had it papered when the rest of the house was decorated. She was delighted to find it in this untouched condition. All her dear former treasures adorned the walls, and she ran from one to another rejoicing over them. There was even a further surprise. Years ago an artist cousin had sketched her portrait in pastel crayons upon the color-wash of the wall. It had been done as a mere artistic freak, but like many such spontaneous drawings it had been an admirable likeness and a very pretty picture. It bore her name, “Ingred,” in flourishy letters underneath. The whole of this had now been protected with a sheet of glass and enclosed by a frame. A table in the room, an easy chair, and a gas-fire seemed to point to its occasional occupation.
===Chinese===
在雅致的晨间起居室里用过茶点，贝丝有许多东西要领客人去看。有她的书、照片册和明信片册，还有种种女孩子家的小物件；一只可卡犬带着三只狗崽，一个个胖得像圆滚滚的布丁卷；一具从卧室一扇窗子推出去的蕨草玻璃柜；一册压制的野花标本；还有一只绿鹦哥，肯站在她手腕上任她抚摸头顶，见了生人却要啄人。她们一层层看上来，最后贝丝领着路，走到了顶层的楼梯平台。她在一间阁楼房间的门前停了片刻。
“这地方你一定认得吧！”她腼腆地说着，把客人让了进去。
英格丽德环顾四周，惊讶不已。这是她和奎恩丽德（Quenrede）旧日合用的一方小天地，充作两人的画室。当年她们获准在这里尝试烙画、绘画、线锯镂雕、喷点画，以及一切被认为太脏、不宜在楼下书房里摆弄的名堂。她们珍爱这个自唤作“小窝”的地方，还格外以在墙上贴满图画为乐——那些画多半是从杂志上剪下来的，用胶水或浆糊粘上去。罗瑟伍德被“红十字会”征用期间，这间屋子一直锁着，英格丽德原以为哈泽尔福德先生（Mr. Haselford）在全屋重新装潢时早给它换上新墙纸了。她却欣喜地发现，这屋子竟原封未动。墙上依旧缀满她昔日那些心爱的宝贝，她欢天喜地地挨个儿跑过去端详。甚至还有一重意外的惊喜。多年前，一位会画画的表亲曾用彩色粉笔在涂了色浆的墙面上给她画过一幅肖像。那不过是一时兴起的手笔，但正如许多即兴之作一样，它画得惟妙惟肖，本身又是一幅很美的画。画像下方用花体字签着她的名字——“英格丽德”。如今这整幅画已罩上一块玻璃，嵌进画框里。屋里一张桌子、一把安乐椅、一座煤气暖炉，看来这房间时不时会有人来用。

===Original===
“You actually haven’t had this changed!” exclaimed Ingred. “I thought it must all have been swept away by now!”
