“Bess Haselford, you’re the biggest trump I’ve ever met! It’s only one girl in a thousand who’d want to cover up a thing like that. Most people would make *such* a tale of it, and pose as an injured martyr whom I’d nearly murdered. I’m sure Francie would, or even Verity.”
“You put yourself into danger to come and warn me!”
“Well, it was the least I could do!”
===Chinese===
“不提？”
“实在没有必要。事情已经过去，了结了。我不想让你惹麻烦。依我说，咱们把它烂在肚子里就完了。”
英格丽德停住脚，一只手搭在栅门上，由衷惊讶地望着同伴。
“贝丝·哈泽尔福德，你真是我平生仅见的大好人！这种事儿肯替人捂住的，一千个女孩里也挑不出一个。换了大多数人，早把它渲染得天花乱坠，还把自己装扮成差点叫我害死的受难圣女。弗朗茜保准会这样，就连薇丽蒂也未必不这样。”
“你自己冒着性命危险，跑来给我报信！”
“呃，那不过是我起码该做的！”

===Original===
“Let’s forget about it then. And don’t tell any of the girls, in case they blab. It would make Miss Strong so nervous, she’d be scared about our going into any fields for ever afterwards.”
“Right-o, I won’t tell, but I shan’t forget. As I said before, I think you’re the biggest trump on the face of the earth.”
“Cuckoo!” rang out Linda’s voice from the bank.
“Where are you girls?” shouted Miss Strong from the lane.
“Coming!” called Ingred, as she latched the gate and hurried with Bess to rejoin the rest of the party.
===Chinese===
“那咱们就把这事忘了吧。也别告诉任何一个姑娘，免得她们嘴快说漏。那会把斯特朗小姐吓得提心吊胆，往后永远不敢让咱们进任何一块田野了。”
“好嘞，我不说。可我不会忘的。我再说一遍——你是天底下头一号的大好人。”
“布谷！”琳达的声音从坡上清亮地传来。
“你们这些丫头在哪儿哪？”斯特朗小姐在小路上喊。
“来啦！”英格丽德扬声应着，闩上栅门，同贝丝一起匆匆赶回队伍里去。

## VI The School Parliament / 六　学校议会

===Original===
During the excitement of the concert Ingred had hardly time to realize the greatness of the honor thrust upon her in being chosen as warden to represent her form. All it stood for struck her afterwards.
“My word! You’ll have to sit up and behave yourself after this, Madame!” remarked Quenrede, when she mentioned the matter at home.
“Yes, of course they’ll all look to you now as an example!” added Mother.
“Oh, I don’t think they will!” declared Ingred, who had not considered her new office from that point of view. “I’ve just to speak up for the interests of the form, you know.”
“There are obligations as well as interests,” said Mother seriously. “Try to make **Va** a useful factor in the school. That would be something worth doing, wouldn’t it?”
In arranging for the School Parliament, Miss Burd had allowed wardens to be chosen by each form, from **IIIb** upwards, but had decided that the smaller girls were too young to take part in public affairs. Every form that sent a representative constituted itself into a kind of club, and chose a special name. These were placed on the Council Register as follows:
===Chinese===
音乐会正热闹的当口，英格丽德（Ingred）几乎无暇体会这份骤然落到自己头上的荣耀有多重——她当选了班代表，要在议会里替全班发声。这职务意味着什么，她事后才猛然意识到。
她在家里提起这件事时，奎恩丽德（Quenrede）发表议论道：“我的天！往后您可得打起精神、规规矩矩做人啦，夫人！”
“可不是嘛，今后人家都要拿你当榜样看了！”妈妈又补上一句。
“哦，我看不至于吧！”英格丽德声称，她还从没从这个角度掂量过自己的新职务，“我不过是要替班里的利益说说话罢了。”
“有利益，就有义务。”妈妈正色道，“想法子让**五甲班**成为学校里有用的一股力量吧。那才是一件真正值得做的事，你说是不是？”
筹组学校议会时，伯德小姐（Miss Burd）准许各班自选班代表，从**三乙班**往上都有份，只是认定低年级的小姑娘们年纪尚幼，不宜参与公共事务。凡派出代表的班级，都自行组成一个俱乐部式的小团体，并各起了别致的名号，登记在议会名册上如下：

===Original===
> **VI**
> The True Blues.
> **Va**
> The Pioneers.
> **Vb**
> The Amazons.
> **IVa**
> The Old Brigade.
> **IVb**
> The Mermaids.
> **IIIa**
> The Dragonflies.
> **IIIb**
> The Cuckoos.
“You can compare marks every fortnight,” said Miss Burd, “and whichever gets the best average shall hold a cup that I intend to present. The marks of the whole form will count, so that slackers will be a distinct drawback to their own companies. Any girl who loses a mark hinders her form from gaining the cup, and of course vice versa, those who work will help.”
The question of marks had been a much debated subject with Miss Burd. She had discussed it in detail at several educational conferences, and had come to the conclusion that, on the whole, the system was highly desirable.
“It’s all very well to talk about the evils of emulation, and work for work’s sake,” she confided to Miss Strong, “but you can’t get children to see things altogether in the same light as grownups. I own that, when I was a child myself, I made tremendous efforts so that I might be head of my form, and when the arrangements were changed at our school, and, instead of carefully-registered marks and places, we only had first, second, or third class, I slacked off considerably. I knew that a lesson not quite so perfectly learnt, or an exercise with one or two mistakes, would still find me in the First Class, so why should I make such enormous exertions? When every slip might mean the loss of my chance to be top, I was far more careful. Of course I know that Emulation, with a big *E*, is supposed to be all wrong, but really I think people make too much fuss about it. It was quite friendly rivalry when I was at school, and the girls with whom I competed were my dearest chums. I believe my new system here is going to unite both methods. Every girl will work for herself, but her marks will also count for her form, and if she slacks, and so pulls down the standard, I hope her companions will give her as bad a time as they do to a ‘butterfingers’ at cricket, and that’s saying something!”
===Chinese===
> **六年级**
> 真蓝队。
> **五甲班**
> 先锋队。
> **五乙班**
> 亚马逊队。
> **四甲班**
> 老近卫队。
> **四乙班**
> 美人鱼队。
> **三甲班**
> 蜻蜓队（The Dragonflies）。
> **三乙班**
> 布谷鸟队（The Cuckoos）。
“你们可以每两星期比一比各班的分数，”伯德小姐说，“平均分最高的班，便可捧走我打算颁赠的一只奖杯。全班每个人的分数都算数，所以偷懒的人势必拖累自己那一队。哪个姑娘丢了一分，就害全班离奖杯远一分；反过来当然也一样，用功的人就是在帮全班的忙。”
积分制度这个问题，伯德小姐素来反复斟酌。她曾在好几次教育会议上详加讨论，最终得出结论：总体而言，这套制度极为可取。
“竞争的坏处啦，为做事而做事啦，这些话说说总是动听的，”她向斯特朗小姐（Miss Strong）吐露心声，“可你不能指望孩子们凡事都拿大人的眼光去看。我承认，我自己当学生的时候，为了争全班第一，下过天大的苦功。后来母校改了章程，不再逐项登记分数和名次，只分甲、乙、丙三等，我立刻懈怠了不少。我心里明白，功课背得不那么烂熟，练习里错上一两处，我照样稳稳落在甲等，那又何苦费那么大的劲？可要是每一点小错都可能断送我夺魁的机会，我就仔细得多了。当然，我知道大写‘**E**’的‘竞争’（Emulation）照时兴的说法一无是处，可依我看，人们实在小题大做。我上学那会儿，这全是友善的较量，同我争名次的姑娘个个是我最亲的伙伴。我相信我在这具新立的制度能兼收两法之长：每个姑娘为自己用功，她的分数同时也记在全班的账上；她若是偷懒，扯了全班的后腿，我希望同学们给她点厉害瞧瞧——就像板球场上大伙儿待‘黄油手’那样毫不留情，那可不是闹着玩的！”

===Original===
The idea of each form constituting a club appealed to the school. It was far more interesting to be “Amazons” or “Cuckoos” than merely **Vb** or **IIIb**, and as awards were to be according to averages, it was thrilling to feel that girls of twelve could wrest away the silver cup from the hands of the very prefects themselves.
“It makes it just like playing a game!” declared Ida Brooke.
“Yes, a sort of tug-of-war when everybody’s got to pull, and mustn’t let go!” added Cissie Barnes, “Do you remember playing ‘Oranges and Lemons’ once with the Sixth? *We* all held on to each others’ waists like grim death, and Janie Potter gave way and broke their chain, so we won!”
“We’ll beat them again, too! I’d like to see that cup on our mantelpiece!”
“The Pioneers,” otherwise **Va**, were as anxious as any of the other forms to carry off laurels. Even Fil, much under protest, really made quite an effort to work.
===Chinese===
各班自成俱乐部的点子很合全校的心意。当“亚马逊队”或“布谷鸟队”，可比干巴巴地叫**五乙班**、**三乙班**有意思多了；况且评奖以平均分计，一想到十二岁的小姑娘也能从级长们手里把银杯夺走，人人都激动不已。
“这样一来，简直就像做游戏一样！”艾达·布鲁克（Ida Brooke）宣称。
“是啊，活像一场拔河，人人都得使足劲，还千万不能撒手！”西茜·巴恩斯（Cissie Barnes）附和道，“记得有一回跟六年级玩‘橘子和柠檬’（Oranges and Lemons）吗？**咱们**个个死死搂住前面人的腰，寸步不让，倒是珍妮·波特（Janie Potter）吃不住劲儿松了手，把她们的链子冲断了，咱们这才赢了！”
“往后咱们还要再赢她们呢！真想瞧瞧那奖杯摆上咱们壁炉架的样子！”
“先锋队”，也就是**五甲班**，同别的班一样一心想摘取桂冠。就连菲尔（Fil），尽管一百个不情愿，也着实下了一番功夫。

===Original===
“You ought to help me with my exercises, though, Ingred,” she wheedled. “Remember, it’s for the benefit of the form. If you let me make mistakes, well﻿—it’s the form that will suffer. You can’t call it *my* fault, it’s on your own head. You know as well as I do that I simply can’t spell, and it takes me hours to hunt up words in the dictionary. I’m looking for ‘phenomenon’ now.”
“You certainly won’t find it in the *F*’s,” laughed Ingred. “What an infant in arms you are! Here, then, go ahead, and I’ll act as dictionary. You’ve only written half a page yet. You’ll be a week of Sundays at this rate.”
“And I haven’t touched my Latin or French!” sighed Fil dismally. “I wish I could go to a school where there isn’t any homework, and that somebody would invent a typewriter that would just spell the words ready-made when you press a button.”
“There’s a fortune waiting for the man who does!” agreed Ingred. “ ‘The Royal-Road-to-Learning Typewriter: spells of itself.’ It would sell by the million, I should think.”
Ingred washed her hands, plaited her hair, and put on her best brooch and her new bangle to attend the first meeting of the School Parliament. The function was held in the Sixth Form room, which she thought slightly unfair, for the prefects, being on their own ground, felt a distinct advantage, and acted as hostesses. There were four of them, so with the games captain they made a party of five from the Sixth, as opposed to six representatives of lower forms, a quite undue proportion in the opinion of the younger girls. Whatever successes the intermediates might win later on, “The True Blues” had carried all before them so far, and had won the cup by an average at least a dozen marks in advance of “The Mermaids,” who came second. The trophy stood on their mantelpiece, and they had brought an ornamental glazed tile on which to place it, as if they meant it to stay there.
===Chinese===
“不过英格丽德，你总该帮我做做练习吧。”她好声好气地磨着，“想想看，这也是为全班好呀。你由着我出错，遭殃的可是咱们班。这就不能算**我**的错，得记在你们自己头上。你和我一样清楚，我压根儿不会拼写，在词典里翻一个词要花上老半天。我这会儿就在查‘现象’（phenomenon）这个词。”
“这个词你翻到‘**F**’部可是绝对找不着的。”英格丽德笑道，“你可真是个襁褓里的小娃娃！来，往下写吧，我给你当活词典。你这才写了半页，照这个速度，写到猴年马月去？”
“拉丁文和法文我更是碰都没碰！”菲尔愁眉苦脸地叹道，“真想上一所没有作业的学校，再盼着哪位能人发明一种打字机，一按按钮，拼写好的词就自个儿蹦出来。”
“谁发明出来，谁就等着发财吧！”英格丽德深表赞同，“‘皇家求学捷径’牌打字机——自动拼写。我看呀，能卖出上百万台去！”
去出席学校议会第一次会议之前，英格丽德洗净双手，编好辫子，别上最漂亮的胸针，戴上新手镯。会议设在六年级教室，她觉得这多少有些不公：级长们占着自家地盘，分明占了便宜，还拿出女主人的做派。她们一共四位，加上运动队队长，六年级一方就有五人之众，对面却是六个低年级代表——在小姑娘们看来，这比例实在悬殊。且不说中级部往后能否争气，眼下“真蓝队”可是所向披靡，以领先第二名“美人鱼队”至少十二分的平均分把奖杯赢到了手。那座奖杯就立在她们的壁炉架上，她们还特意带来一块上釉的花砖垫在底下，俨然打算让它在那儿长住下去。

===Original===
On the whole they received the other wardens very graciously, and gave them opportunities to speak and air their views. Questions such as the due apportioning of the asphalt tennis-courts, basketball and hockey fixtures, and various school societies were discussed, and the general business of the term got under way.
“It helps things to be able to talk it over and know what you all think,” said Lispeth. “We’re making so many changes with coming into the new building, that it’s almost like an entirely fresh start. Miss Burd wants us to get up a sort of Reconstruction Society in the school. She hasn’t quite planned it out yet, but she told me a little about it, and I think it’s ever so nice. As soon as it’s quite fixed up, I’m going to call a general meeting, and explain it to everybody. I expect that will be next Wednesday. Will you give me power to do this on my own, or must I call a special committee on Monday to discuss it first, before I put it to the school?”
“It’s my music lesson on Monday, I couldn’t come,” demurred Ingred.
“And I have to go to the dentist immediately after four,” chimed in Alys Horner, the warden of “The Amazons.”
“If Miss Burd has arranged it, I suppose it’s all serene,” said Mabel Hughes, of “The Old Brigade.”
“You’ll like it, I know. I’d explain now, only I haven’t got any of the papers, and besides, it would take such a long time, and it’s rather late, and I want to be getting home. Anyway, I hope we shall all take it up hot and strong. Be sure to keep Wednesday free, though I’m going to ask Miss Burd to let us have the meeting in school hours if possible, then we’re absolutely sure of everybody.”
===Chinese===
总的说来，她们接待其余各位班代表还算和气，也给大家发言、畅抒己见的机会。柏油网球场的妥善分配、篮球和曲棍球的赛程，以及各个学校社团的事务，都一一议过，本学期的各项常规事务便就此铺开了。
“大家能凑在一起谈谈，知道彼此心里怎么想，事情就好办多了。”丽丝佩丝（Lispeth）说，“搬进新楼以后，我们处处都在变，几乎等于从头来过。伯德小姐想让我们在学校里办一个‘重建协会’（Reconstruction Society）之类的组织。她还没有完全筹划好，不过跟我透了一点口风，我觉得这主意好极了。等一切定妥，我就召集全体大会，向大家说明。我想就定在下周三。你们是授权我一个人去办呢，还是我得周一先开个特别委员会讨论一遍，再向全校宣布？”
“周一是我的音乐课，我来不了。”英格丽德表示为难。
“我四点一过还得去看牙医。”亚马逊队的班代表艾莉丝·霍纳（Alys Horner）也搭腔道。
“既是伯德小姐安排的，想必错不了。”老近卫队的梅布尔·休斯（Mabel Hughes）说。
“我知道你们准会喜欢的。我本想现在就讲，只是那些文件一张也不在我手上，再说讲起来太费工夫，天也不早了，我还想早点回家去。总而言之，希望大家到时候都热火朝天地干起来。周三务必空出来；我还要去求伯德小姐，尽量把大会安排在上课时间开，那样人就保准到得齐。”

===Original===
“Right you are!” agreed the wardens, separating in a rather unparliamentary fashion to admire a vinaigrette, scented with heliotrope, which Althea took from her pocket and handed round for appreciative sniffs.
All the girls felt that Lispeth Scott was to be trusted. She was a worthy leader for the new order of things. She was a tall, stout, fair girl of almost eighteen, and rather grownup for her age. She was the youngest member of a large family who had made enormous exertions during the war, and, with sisters who had nursed in Serbia, driven motor-ambulances in France, served in canteens, in Y.M.C.A. huts, and worked at munitions, she had excellent examples of what it is possible to do for one’s country. She was a decided favorite in the College, being athletic as well as clever, and of a very jolly merry temperament with a vein of great earnestness. Though the girls sometimes called her “Jumbo,” they meant the nickname in token of friendship, and submitted to her dictatorship far more readily than they would have done to that of any other member of the Sixth who had been put in her place. Miss Burd had great confidence in Lispeth, and consequently, when they had talked over the matter of the new society which she wished to be formed in the school, she decided to leave its institution entirely in the hands of her head girl.
“It will be far better for the mistresses not to be present at the meeting,” she said. “I can trust you, Lispeth, to explain things, and the girls will like it much more if it seems to emanate from the new Council. Talk to them in your own way, and they’ll understand you. I want the Society to be an absolutely voluntary one, or it’s of no use. Don’t let them think they must join merely to please *me*. I’d rather have a dozen who are in earnest over it than a hundred halfhearted members. Only those who feel enthusiastic need give in their names. I don’t mind if it begins in quite a humble way. Indeed, I only expect a small membership at first.”
“On the contrary, Miss Burd, I think it will catch on,” replied Lispeth.
In consequence of this conversation, the head prefect pinned a paper on the notice-board, convening a general meeting of all girls over twelve years of age, to be held in the big hall on Wednesday afternoon at 3:30 sharp, the last lesson of the day having been remitted by orders from the Study. There was a universal feeling that something important was on foot, so those forms that were eligible trooped in a body to the hall, while the disappointed juniors tried to console themselves with the reflection that they would be able to go home half an hour earlier than their elders. After considerable shuffling about, places were taken. Unwilling to waste further time, Lispeth mounted the platform, and rang the bell for silence.
===Chinese===
“一言为定！”各位班代表齐声应道，随即颇不合议会体统地散开来，传看阿尔西娅（Althea）从口袋里掏出的一只天芥菜香型的小香盒，你嗅一下我嗅一下，啧啧称赞。
姑娘们都觉得丽丝佩丝·斯科特（Lispeth Scott）信得过，作为开创新局面的领袖，她当之无愧。她是个高挑壮实的白肤金发姑娘，年方十八不到，言行举止却比年龄老成。她出身一个人丁兴旺的大家庭，是家里最小的一个；一家人在战争中出过大力——姐姐们有的在塞尔维亚（Serbia）当过护士，有的在法国开过救护车，有的在战地食堂和基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）的棚屋里服务过，还有的进厂造过军火——什么叫做报效祖国，她身边有的是现成的榜样。她在学院里是人见人爱的红人，既聪明又擅运动，性子爽朗快活，骨子里却有一股极认真的劲儿。姑娘们有时管她叫“大象”（Jumbo），这绰号里满是亲热的意味；换了六年级随便哪一位坐上她的位子，大家决不会像对她那样心悦诚服地听其号令。伯德小姐对丽丝佩丝极为信任，因此，两人把想在学校里设立的新社团商谈停当之后，她便决定把创办事宜全权交到她的学生长手里。
“开会时，教师们还是不到场的好。”她说，“丽丝佩丝，把事情讲清楚，我信得过你。让姑娘们觉得这主意出自新议会，她们才会加倍喜欢。照你自己的路子去讲，她们听得懂。我要这个社团完全出于自愿，否则便毫无意义。别让她们觉得非入会不可，仿佛是为了讨好**我**。我宁可要十二个真心实意的人，也不要一百个三心二意的会员。只有真心热忱的姑娘才需要报名。起初规模小些、起点低些，我毫不在意。说实话，我头一步只指望一小批会员。”
“恰恰相反，伯德小姐，我看它准会风行起来。”丽丝佩丝答道。
这番谈话之后，学生长便在布告栏上贴出一张通告，召集全体年满十二岁的姑娘于周三下午三点半整到大礼堂开会，当天最后一节课已奉校长室之命免上。人人都预感有大事将临，于是够格的各班成群结队涌向礼堂，没份儿参加的低班小同学闷闷不乐，只得这样宽慰自己：好歹能比学姐们早半个钟头回家。一阵拖拖沓沓的推搡挪动之后，众人总算各就各位。丽丝佩丝不愿再耽搁，登上讲台，摇铃请大家安静。

===Original===
“Are we all here? Well, I can’t wait for anybody else. Those who come in late will have to hear what they can, and you must tell them the rest afterwards. Oh, here they are! Quietly, please! There’s plenty of room over there. Violet, will you shut the door? Now that we’re all together, I want to have a talk with you. You know I’m what may be called ‘Prime Minister’ of our School Parliament, and, though your wardens will report all we say in council, I think it is well to have a public meeting sometimes. This term everything seems to have made a fresh start. We’re in new buildings, and we have new rules, and our very Parliament is a new institution. You’re all in new forms, and I’m the new Head Prefect. It’s not only in school that everything’s different, but in the outside world as well. This is our first term since peace was signed. I can remember our first term after War was declared. I was only in **IIIa** then﻿—quite a youngster! Hetty Hughes, who was the head girl, made a speech, and told us what we ought to do to try to help our country. I think some of us who were here have never forgotten that. We nearly hurrahed the roof off, and we formed a Knitting Club and a Soldiers’ Parcel Society on the spot. You know for yourselves how we worked to keep those up. Well, today the Empire is at peace, but our country needs our help as much as ever, or even more. It’s making a fresh start, and we want the new world to be a better place than the old. Hundreds of thousands of gallant young lives have been gladly given to establish this new world﻿—in this school alone we know to our cost﻿—and we owe it to our heroic dead not to let their sacrifice be in vain. We want a better and purer England to rise up and make a clean sweep of the bad things that disgraced her before. I expect you’ll say: ‘Oh, that’s for politicians, and not for us schoolgirls!’ but it isn’t. Popular opinion is a mighty thing. The schoolgirls of today are the women of tomorrow, and the women of a country have an enormous amount to do with the formation of public opinion﻿—more nowadays than ever before﻿—and their influence will go on increasing with every year that passes. If each of us tries to help the world instead of hindering it, think what an asset each one may be to the country! It’s really a tremendous honor to know that we can all take our part in the reconstruction of England. It’s like each being allowed to lay a brick in the foundation of a new building. Of course you’ll ask me: ‘Well, and how are we going to help?’ That’s just what I want to talk about. We pride ourselves on being practical at the College. Some of us thought we might start a new society, to be called ‘The Rainbow League.’ It’s a sort of ‘Guild of Helpers,’ and we want to do all kinds of jolly things to help in the town, something like our old ‘Knitting Club’ and ‘Soldiers’ Parcel Society,’ only of course different. We could give concerts and make clothes for war orphans, and toys for the hospitals, and scrapbooks for crippled children. There are heaps of nice things like that you’ll just love doing. It’s called ‘The Rainbow League,’ because a rainbow was set in the sky after the Flood, to help people to remember, and we want, in our small way, not to let the Great War be forgotten, but to do our bit to help with the future of the race.
“I’m not any great hand at speaking or explaining, so I want you each to take a copy of the rules of ‘The Rainbow League’ and to read them quietly over at home. Then any girl who likes to join can put her name down. All the Sixth want to become members, and I hope lots of others will too. That’s all I have to say. I’m afraid I’m rather a bungler, but you’ll understand everything if you read the papers. I’m going to give them out now.”
Lispeth, very red in the face, came down from the platform, and, aided by her fellow-prefects, began to distribute papers right and left to the girls as they filed from the benches. Amongst the others, Ingred took hers, and put it in her pocket. She did not care to discuss it with the crowd, so retired to a corner of the hostel garden, and, amid a shower of falling autumn leaves, opened the typewritten sheet, and read as follows:
===Chinese===
“人都到齐了吗？好，我可不能再等后头的了。迟到的只好听见多少算多少，回头你们再把其余的补讲给她们听。哦，来了来了！请安静些！那边空位多着呢。维奥莱特（Violet），劳驾把门关上。既然大家都到了，我想跟你们谈谈心。你们知道，我在咱们学校议会里，勉强算得上是‘首相’。各位班代表固然会把我们在议事会上的话向各班转达，不过我想，偶尔开一次公开大会也不无益处。这个学期，样样事情都像重新开了头：我们住进了新校舍，订了新规章，连议会本身也是新设的机构；你们都在新的班级，我也是新上任的学生长。不只是学校里气象一新，外面的世界同样变了样。这是和约签订以来的第一个学期。我还记得宣战后的第一个学期，那时我才上**三甲班**——十足的小不点儿！当年执任学生长的赫蒂·休斯（Hetty Hughes）发表了一番演讲，告诉我们该怎样尽自己的力量去帮国家一把。我想，我们这些亲耳听过的人，一辈子也忘不了。我们差点儿把屋顶都欢呼掀了，当场就成立了一个编织社（Knitting Club）和一个士兵包裹会（Soldiers' Parcel Society）。这两摊事我们后来是怎样苦苦撑持的，大家都亲见过。如今帝国总算太平了，可国家需要我们出力之处，不减往日，甚至有过之而无不及。国家正在重新起步，我们要让新世界比旧世界更美好。为了缔造这个新世界，成千上万英勇的年轻生命慨然捐躯——单是我们学校，就有切肤之痛——我们不能辜负死难的英雄，不能让他们的牺牲付诸东流。我们要看到一个更美好、更纯洁的英格兰崛起，把从前玷污她的种种丑恶一扫而光。我料想你们会说：‘哦，那是政客的事，跟我们女学生有什么相干！’其实不然。民意是一股强大的力量。今天的女学生，就是明天的妇女；一国妇女对舆论的形成举足轻重——今日尤甚于往昔——而且这份影响力还将一年大过一年。倘若我们每个人都不去拖世界的后腿，反倒拉它一把，想想吧，我们每个人对国家会是多么宝贵的一笔财富！想到我们人人都能在重建英格兰的事业中尽一份力，这实在是无上的光荣，就好比新大厦奠基，人人获准砌上自己的一块砖。你们准会问我：‘那么，我们究竟怎么个帮法？’这正是我要说的。咱们学院向来以务实自诩。我们几个人合计，不妨创办一个新社团，就叫‘彩虹联盟’（The Rainbow League）。它有点像一个‘互助公会’（Guild of Helpers），我们要做种种有趣的事情来帮助本地镇区，有些像从前那个‘编织社’和‘士兵包裹会’，当然又不尽相同。我们可以办音乐会，给战争孤儿缝衣裳，给医院做玩具，给残疾儿童编剪贴簿。这类讨人喜欢的好事多的是，保管你们个个乐意做。它之所以叫‘彩虹联盟’，是因为大洪水之后，天上高悬一道彩虹，提醒世人铭记前事；我们也想尽自己的绵薄之力，不叫这场大战被人遗忘，同时为民族的未来出一份力。”
“我天生不擅长演讲，也不大会解释，所以想请你们每人拿一份‘彩虹联盟’的章程，回家安安静静地读一遍。之后，愿意入会的姑娘尽可把名字登记上。六年级的同学们全都打算入会，我希望其他人也有许多报名。我要说的就这么多。我这人笨嘴拙舌的，不过你们只要把章程读一遍，就什么都明白了。我这就发下去。”
丽丝佩丝涨红着脸走下讲台，在几位级长同伴的帮衬下，给鱼贯离座的姑娘们左一份右一份地分发章程。英格丽德也接了一份，放进口袋。她不愿挤在人堆里议论，便退到寄宿舍花园的一角，在纷纷飘坠的秋叶中展开那张打字的纸，读到如下内容：

===Original===
> **What the League holds**
> That every soul is of infinite and equal value, because all are the children of one Father.
> That every girl must do her best to help all other girls, and to advance the Sisterhood of Women.
> That woman’s greatest and strongest weapons are love and sweetness.
> That by conscious radiation of unselfish love to her fellow-beings, a girl may undoubtedly raise the moral atmosphere of the world around her.
> That every girl, however young, can help this glorious old country, and that, joined together for good, the schoolgirls of a nation can influence the well-being of a race.
> That good can always triumph over evil, and that love and unselfishness will wipe out many social blots, and put beauty in their place.
> As the rainbow has seven prismatic colors, these may stand for seven talents of woman.
> Violet
> = **Virtue**﻿—the bedrock of woman’s influence.
> Indigo
> = **Industry**﻿—which means willing service.
> Blue
> = **Beauty**﻿—in its many and varied forms.
> Green
> = **Generosity**﻿—to give of our best to others.
> Yellow
> = **Youth**﻿—to offer our best years to God.
> Orange
> = **Order**﻿—which includes organization.
> Red
> = **Radiation**﻿—the Love Force going out to others.
> **Fellowship**
> Every member of the League shall pledge herself to forward its objects and to take an active part in any schemes of help that may be instituted in connection with it.
> Flower Emblem.
> The Iris.
> Motto.
> “Freely ye have received, freely give.”
Ingred sat for a moment or two, watching the petals blow from the last roses on the bush that hung over the worn stone wall. The old Abbey lay on one hand, the buildings of the new school on the other. They seemed the very personification of ancient and modern.
“The world can’t stand still,” she thought, “and if it’s got to move on, I suppose I’d better help to give it a shove in the right direction.”
Walking into the hostel, she met Nora and Fil walking arm-in-arm.
“Hullo, Ingred! Have you read the paper about the Rainbow League?” asked Fil eagerly. “I think it’s ripping! Nora and I are both going to join.”
“And so am I,” said Ingred, as she passed by them, and went upstairs.
===Chinese===
> **联盟信条**
> 信每一个灵魂都有无限而平等的价值，因为众人同是一位天父的儿女。
> 信每个姑娘都应尽力扶助其他姑娘，促进天下女子的姐妹情谊。
> 信爱与温柔是女性最伟大、最强有力的武器。
> 信姑娘只要自觉地向同胞播撒无私的爱，必能提升她周遭世界的道德风气。
> 信每个姑娘无论多么年幼，都能为这个光荣的古老国家出力；全国的女学生同心向善，便能影响一个民族的福祉。
> 信善终能胜恶；爱与无私将拭去世间许多社会污点，以美代之。
> 彩虹含七色棱光，正可象征女性的七种才德。
> 紫
> = **德行**——女性影响力的基石。
> 靛
> = **勤勉**——意为甘心乐意的服务。
> 蓝
> = **美**——千姿百态之美。
> 绿
> = **慷慨**——把我们的至善奉献他人。
> 黄
> = **青春**——把最好的年华献给上帝。
> 橙
> = **秩序**——亦含组织之义。
> 红
> = **辐射**——爱的力量外及他人。
> **会员盟约**
> 凡联盟成员，均应立誓推进联盟宗旨，并积极参与联盟日后设立的各项扶助事业。
> 盟花。
> 鸢尾。
> 盟训。
> “白白得来，白白施与。”
英格丽德呆坐了片刻，望着探出斑驳石墙的那丛玫瑰仅存的几朵花，花瓣正被风一片片吹散。一边是古老的修道院（the Abbey），一边是崭新的校舍，恰似“古”与“今”各自的化身。
“世界是停不下来的，”她心想，“既然它非往前走不可，我不如帮着朝正道上推它一把。”
走进寄宿舍时，她迎面碰上诺拉（Nora）和菲尔挽着胳膊走来。
“喂，英格丽德！彩虹联盟那张传单你看了吗？”菲尔急切地问，“我觉得棒极了！我和诺拉都要入会。”
“我也入。”英格丽德说着，从她们身边走过，上楼去了。

## X The Whispering Stones / X 低语石

===Original===
The Saxon family had squeezed themselves and certain of their possessions into the little home at Wynch-on-the-Wold, and while flowers still bloomed in the garden and apples hung ripe on the trees it seemed a kind of continuation of their summer holiday; but as the novelty wore off, and stormy weather came on, their altered circumstances began to be more evident. Most of us can make a plucky fight against fate at first﻿—there had been something rather romantic about retiring to the bungalow﻿—but the plain prose of the proceeding was yet to come, and there were certainly many disadvantages to be faced. Mr. Saxon was worried about business affairs; he was a proud, sensitive man, and felt it a great “come down” to be obliged to resign Rotherwood, and the social position it had stood for, and confess himself to the world as one of the “newly poor.” It was humiliating to have to walk or take a tram where he had formerly used his car in fulfilling his professional engagements, hard not to be able to entertain his friends, and perhaps hardest of all to be obliged to refuse subscriptions to the numerous charities in the town where his name had always stood conspicuously upon the liberal list. His temper, never his strongest point, suffered under the test, and he would come home from Grovebury in the evenings tired out, moody and fretful, and inclined to find fault with everything and everybody.
It took all his wife’s sunny sweetness of disposition to keep the home atmosphere cheerful and peaceful, for Egbert also had a temper, and was bitterly disappointed at not being sent to Cambridge, and at having to settle down in the family office instead. Father and son did not get on remarkably well together. Mr. Saxon, like many parents, pooh-poohed his boy’s business efforts, and would sometimes﻿—to Egbert’s huge indignation﻿—point out his mistakes before the clerks. He would declare, in a high and mighty way, that his own son should not receive special preference at the office, and so overdid his attitude of impartiality that he contrived to give him a worse time than any of his other articled pupils.
Athelstane, who had begun his medical course at the University of Birkshaw, also had his troubles. He had hoped to study at Guy’s Hospital in preparation for the London M.D., and to an ambitious young fellow it was hard to be satisfied with a provincial degree. The thirty-mile motor ride to and from Birkshaw soon lost its charm, and the difficulties of home study in the evenings were great in a bungalow with thin partition walls and a family not always disposed to quiet. As a rule, he kept his feelings to himself, but he went about with a depressed look, and got into a habit of lifting his eyebrows which was leaving permanent lines on a hitherto smooth and unwrinkled forehead.
===Chinese===
萨克森一家连人带一部分家当，挤进了沃尔德畔温奇的那座小宅子。其时园中花还开着，枝头苹果熟得正好，那段日子仿佛是暑假的延续；可随着新鲜劲儿淡去，风雨天气接踵而至，家道变了的境况便一天天显露出来。大多数人起初都敢鼓起勇气同命运较量一番——退居到“小平房”里，这事起头还颇有几分罗曼蒂克的意味——只是这件事里平淡无奇的散文部分还在后头，要面对的种种不便也着实不少。萨克森先生为生意上的事焦心；他是个自尊而敏感的人，如今不得不舍弃罗瑟伍德、舍弃它所代表的社会地位，向世人自认是“新贫人家”中的一员，这在他看来实在是天大的跌份。从前出门赴约办事，他向来坐自家的汽车，如今却只能靠两条腿或搭电车，这很伤体面；无法款待朋友，日子难过；而最难受的，恐怕还是不得不一一回绝镇上众多慈善团体的募捐——要知道，以往那份慷慨捐输的名单上，他的名字回回都列得醒醒目目。他的脾气原本就算不得他的长处，经此一磨更见亏损，每晚从格罗夫伯里回到家里，总是疲惫不堪、郁郁寡欢、烦躁易怒，看什么都不顺眼，对谁都要挑三拣四。
全靠妻子天性开朗温厚，家里的气氛才维持得住愉快与安宁，因为埃格伯特也有脾气，一想到没能去剑桥、只得安下心来进自家的办公室做事，便懊丧得不行。父子俩相处得并不怎么融洽。萨克森先生同许多做父亲的一样，对儿子办事的能耐嗤之以鼻，有时还当着职员的面指点他的错处——这令埃格伯特愤慨万分。他总摆出一副居高临下的口吻，宣称自己的儿子在办公室里绝不该受什么特殊照应，结果把这份“铁面无私”做得过了头，反倒设法让他过得比别的签约学徒都不如意。
在伯克肖大学开始攻读医学课程的阿特尔斯坦，也有他的烦恼。他原指望进盖伊医院（Guy's Hospital）求学，为考取伦敦的医学博士学位做准备；对一个心气高的年轻人来说，只拿个外省学位，实在难以甘心。坐汽车往返伯克肖的三十英里路程，新鲜劲儿很快就没了；晚上在家用功也不容易——小平房隔墙单薄，一家人又未必时时肯安静。他照例把心事闷在肚里，只是走动时总带着一脸抑郁，还养成了挑眉的习惯，在那片原本光洁无皱的额头上，生生刻下了几道再也抚不平的纹路。

===Original===
Pretty Quenrede, who had just left school, was going through the awkward phase of discovering her individuality. At the College, with a full program of lessons and games, she had followed the general lead of the form. Now, cast upon her own resources, she was quite vague as to any special bent or taste. The wartime occupations which had tempted her imagination were no longer available, and “Careers for Women” did not attract her, even if family funds had run to the necessary training. So, for the present, she stayed at home, going once a week to the School of Art at Grovebury, and practicing singing in a rather desultory fashion. Though she pretended to be glad she was an emancipated young lady, as a matter of fact she missed school immensely, and was finding life decidedly slow and tame.
With their elders palpably dissatisfied, Ingred and Hereward would have been hardly human if they had not raised some personal grievances of their own to grumble at, and matters would often have been dismal enough at the bungalow but for Mrs. Saxon’s happy capacity for looking on the bright side of things. The whole household centered round “Mother.” She was a woman in a thousand. Naturally it had hurt her to relinquish Rotherwood, and it grieved her﻿—for the girls’ sake﻿—that most of her old acquaintances in Grovebury had not troubled to pay calls at Wynchcote. The small rooms, the one maid from the Orphanage, the necessity of doing much of the housework herself, the difficulties of shopping on a limited purse, and her husband’s fretfulness and faultfinding, might have soured a less unselfish disposition: she had married, however, “for better or for worse,” and took the altered circumstances with cheery optimism. She was a great lover of nature and of scenery, and the nearness of the moors, with their ever-changing effects of storm and sunshine, and the opportunities they gave for the study of birds and insects, proved compensation for some of the things which life otherwise lacked.
Every morning, after the fuss of getting off the family to their several avocations, she would run down the garden, and stand for a few minutes by the wall that overlooked the moor, watching great shafts of sunlight fall from a gray sky on to brown wastes of heather and bracken, listening to the call of the curlews or to the trilling autumn warble of the robin, perched on the red-berried hawthorn bush. Kind Mother Nature could always soothe her spirits, and send her back with fresh courage for the day’s work. And, in the evening, when husband and children came home to fire and lamplight, she had generally some nature notes to tell them, or some amusing little incident to make them laugh and forget their various woes and worries.
===Chinese===
刚刚离开学校的漂亮姑娘奎恩丽德，正处在发现自我的尴尬年纪。从前在学校里，功课和运动把日程排得满满当当，她一向随大流跟着班级走；如今要全凭自己拿主意了，她却说不清自己究竟有什么特别的志趣。战争年月里那些曾令她浮想联翩的工作已不复存在，“女性职业”一类的出路又吸引不了她——何况家里也拿不出钱来供她受必要的培训。于是眼下她只好待在家里，每星期去一趟格罗夫伯里的艺术学校，唱歌嘛，也是有一搭没一搭地练着。她嘴上装作庆幸自己是个解放了的年轻小姐，实则想念学校想得不行，只觉得日子实在乏味、太不过瘾。
眼见长辈们个个愁眉不展，英格丽德和赫里沃德若不也各自叫嚷几桩委屈，那简直不近人情；多亏萨克森太太天生一副往好处看的好性子，否则小平房里的光景只怕常常要够惨的。全家上下都围着“母亲”转，她是个千里挑一的女人。舍弃罗瑟伍德自然叫她痛心；格罗夫伯里的旧相识大半懒得来温奇科特登门，也叫她难过——那是替女儿们难过。屋子窄小，只雇得起一个孤儿院出来的女仆，许多家务得亲自动手，攥着紧巴巴的钱袋置办东西处处作难，再加上丈夫的烦躁挑剔——换个私心重些的人，脾气早被磨馊了；不过她当初嫁人，原是“无论顺境逆境”都认了的，便带着乐天的达观领受了这场变故。她素来热爱自然、爱看风景，荒原近在咫尺，风云雨晴变幻不定，又尽可供她观察虫鸟，倒也弥补了生活里旁的种种缺憾。
每天早晨，等一家人乱哄哄地出门、各干各的去了之后，她总要快步跑下花园，在俯瞰荒原的那堵墙边站上几分钟：看一道道巨幅的阳光从灰色天幕斜落在褐色的石楠与蕨莽铺成的旷野上，听麻鹬的啼唤，或是听栖在红果累累的山楂丛上的知更鸟啭出秋天的歌喉。仁慈的大自然母亲总能抚平她的心绪，让她带着一股新勇气回屋操持一天的活计。到了晚上，丈夫儿女回到炉火与灯光里来，她总有些自然的见闻讲给他们听，或是说上一段有趣的小插曲，逗得大家一笑，忘了各自的烦恼忧愁。

===Original===
“I’m so glad, Muvvie dear, you’re not a melancholy lugubrious person!” said Ingred once. “It would be *so* trying if you sat at the tea-table and sighed.”
“Humor is the salt of life,” smiled Mrs. Saxon. “We may just as well get all the fun out of the little daily happenings. Even ‘the orphan’ has her bright side!”
As “the orphan” was a temporary member of the Wynchcote establishment she merits a word of description. She came from an institution in the neighborhood, and, being the only servant procurable at the time, was tolerated in spite of a terrible propensity for smashing plates, and for carolling at the very pitch of a nasal voice. She was a rough, good-tempered girl, devoted to Minx, the cat, and really kind if anybody had a headache or toothache, but quite without any sense of discrimination: she would show a traveling hawker into the drawing-room, and leave the clergyman standing on the doorstep, took the best serviettes to wipe the china, scoured the silver with Monkey Brand Soap, and systematically bespattered the kitchen tablecloth with ink. Her love of music was a terrible trial to the medical student of the family on Saturday morning, when he was endeavoring to read at home.
===Chinese===
“老妈咪，亲爱的，真高兴你不是个愁眉苦脸、悲悲切切的人！”英格丽德有一回说，“你要是坐在茶桌边一声声叹气，那可*太*熬人了！”
“幽默是生活的盐。”萨克森太太莞尔一笑，“日常这些鸡毛蒜皮的小事，我们不妨都从中榨出点乐子来。就连‘孤儿’也有她的长处呢！”
“孤儿”既是温奇科特这户人家里的临时一员，也值得花上几笔描她一描。她是从附近一家机构里出来的，因为当时是唯一雇得到的女仆，才被留用下来——尽管她那砸盘子的癖好吓人，唱起歌来又专爱扯着一副鼻音嗓子拔到最高。她是个粗手粗脚、好脾气的姑娘，一心疼爱着那只猫明克斯（Minx）；谁要是头疼牙疼，她也真心体贴，只是全无半点轻重分别：她会把走街串巷的小贩让进客厅，却任凭牧师站在门阶上干等；拿上好的餐巾去擦瓷器，用“猴牌”肥皂（Monkey Brand）擦洗银器，还惯常拿墨水把厨房桌布溅得斑斑点点。每逢星期六上午，家里那位医科生想在家安心读书，她对音乐的这份热爱便成了他的可怕酷刑。

===Original===
“Carlyle says somewhere: ‘Give, oh, give me a man who sings at his work!’ ” growled Athelstane one day, bursting forth from his den to complain of the nuisance, “but I bet the old buffer didn’t write that sentiment with a maidservant howling popular songs in the next room. According to all accounts he loathed noise and couldn’t even stand the crowing of a cock. I should call that bit of eloquence just bunkum. If the orphan doesn’t stop this voice-production business I shall have to go and slay her. How *can* a fellow study in the midst of such a racket? Where’s the Mater? Down in Grovebury? I suppose that accounts for it. While the cat’s away, etc.”
“Hardly complimentary to compare your maternal relative to a cat!” chuckled Ingred. “Stop the orphan if you can, but you might as well try to stop the brook! She’s quiet for five minutes then bursts out into song again like a chirruping cricket or a croaking corncrake. I want to spiflicate her myself sometimes.”
> “ ‘Late last night I slew my wife,
 

 Stretched her on the parquet flooring;
 

 I was loath to take her life,
 

 But I *had* to stop her snoring!’ ”
quoted Hereward from *Ruthless Rhymes*.
===Chinese===
“卡莱尔（Carlyle）在哪儿说过：‘给我，啊给我一个干活时唱歌的人吧！’”有一天，阿特尔斯坦从他那间小窝里冲出来抱怨这桩祸害，“不过我敢打赌，这老古董写下这句感想时，隔壁屋里准没有一个女仆在嚎流行歌曲。据种种传闻，他最恨吵闹，连公鸡打鸣都受不了。要我说，那段高论纯粹是空话。‘孤儿’再不停止这套练嗓事业，我就只得去把她宰了。这种闹腾里叫人*怎么*用功？老妈呢？下格罗夫伯里去了？这就说得通了。真是猫儿不在，耗子……”
“把令堂大人比作猫，可算不得恭维！”英格丽德咯咯笑道，“有本事你叫‘孤儿’停下来试试——不过那跟拦住小溪不叫它流一样白费劲！她安生不了五分钟，就又像唧唧的蟋蟀、呱呱的秧鸡似的唱开了。有时候连我都恨不得亲手收拾她一顿。”
> “昨夜三更我杀妻，
> 尸横拼花地板上；
> 本也不忍要她命，
> 无奈*必须*止鼾声！”
这是赫里沃德从《无情谐诗》（*Ruthless Rhymes*）里念来的。

===Original===
“Look here!” said Quenrede, emerging from the kitchen with a half-packed lunch basket. “We three are taking sandwiches, and going for a good old tramp over the moors. Why not drop your work for once and come with us? You look as if you needed a holiday.”
“I’ve a beast of a headache,” admitted Athelstane.
“You want fresh air, not study,” decreed Quenrede with sisterly firmness, “and I shall just make some extra sandwiches and put another apple in the basket. With mother out, the orphan will carol all the morning, unless you gag her, so you may as well accept the inevitable.”
“Cut and run, in fact!” added Hereward.
“The voice of the siren tempts me to go﻿—to escape the voice of the siren who stays!” wavered Athelstane.
===Chinese===
“喂，听我说！”奎恩丽德拎着装了一半的午餐篮从厨房里出来说道，“我们仨打算带上三明治，去荒原上痛痛快快地远足一趟。你何不破例撂下功课，跟我们一起去？瞧你那样子，正欠一个假日呢。”
“我头疼得厉害。”阿特尔斯坦认了。
“你缺的是新鲜空气，不是啃书本。”奎恩丽德拿出姐妹的坚决劲儿下了断语，“我正好再多做几份三明治，往篮子里再添一个苹果。老妈不在家，‘孤儿’要唱一整个上午，除非你堵上她的嘴，所以你还是认命吧。”
“说白了，就是赶紧开溜！”赫里沃德补上一句。
“那塞壬的歌声诱我前去——正好躲开留守塞壬的歌声！”阿特尔斯坦摇摆不定地说。

===Original===
“Oh, come along, old sport!” urged Ingred. “What are a few old bones to Red Ridge Barrow? You can swat tonight to make up, if you want to.”
“It’s three to one!” said Athelstane, giving way gracefully; “and there mayn’t be any more fine Saturdays for walks.”
The four young people started forth with the delightful sense of having the day before them. It was fairly early, and a hazy November sun had not yet drawn the moisture from the heather. On the moor the few trees were bare, but the golden autumn leaves still clothed the woods in the sheltered valley that stretched below. Masses of gossamer covered with dewdrops lay among the bracken, like fairies’ washing hung out to dry. There was a hint of hoarfrost under the bushes. The air had that delicious invigorating quality when every breath sets the body dancing. It was too late in the year for flowers, though here and there a little gorse lingered, or a few buttercups and hawkweeds. After about an hour of red haziness the sun pierced the bank of mist and shone out gloriously, almost as in summer; the birds, ready to snatch a moment’s joy, were flitting about tweeting and calling, a water-wagtail took a bath in a shallow pool of a stream, and a great flock of bramblings, rare visitors in those parts, paused in their migration to hold a chattering conference round an old elder tree.
===Chinese===
“哎呀，来吧，老伙计！”英格丽德怂恿道，“你这几根老骨头，红岭古冢还能嫌多不成？想补功课，晚上开夜车就是了。”
“三对一，寡不敌众！”阿特尔斯坦大大方方地让了步，“再说，往后的星期六，怕是未必再有这样好的天儿了。”
四个年轻人出发了，一整天尽在眼前，那份滋味说不出的美妙。时候还早，十一月里朦胧的太阳还没吸干石楠上的潮气。荒原上寥寥几棵树已落尽了叶子，可山下那道背风谷地里的树林，仍旧披着金色的秋叶。一团团挂满露珠的游丝铺在蕨莽之间，像仙女儿晾出来晾晒的衣裳。灌木底下隐约凝着一层白霜。空气里有种妙不可言的清冽振奋，每吸一口，浑身的血都跟着欢跳。一年中开花的时节已经晚了，只是零零星星还残着几簇荆豆，或几朵毛茛、山柳菊。红雾迷茫了约莫一个钟头，太阳终于钻透雾堤，灿灿烂烂地照耀出来，几乎同夏天一样；鸟儿们抓紧这片刻的欢愉，叽叽喳喳地飞掠啼啭；一只鹡鸰在溪流的一汪浅水里洗了个澡；还有一大群燕雀——那一带少见的客人——也暂停了迁徙，围着一棵老接骨木叽叽喳喳地开起了会。

===Original===
The Saxons were determined today to go farther afield than their walks had hitherto taken them. The local guidebook mentioned some prehistoric menhirs and a chambered barrow on the top of Red Ridge, a distant hill, so they had fixed that as their Mecca.
It was a considerable tramp, but the bracing air helped them on, and they sat down at last to eat their lunch by the side of the path that led to the summit. The boys had wished to mount to the top without calling a halt, but the girls had struck, and insisted on a rest before the final climb.
“Pity Mother isn’t here!” said Ingred, voicing the general feeling of the family, which always missed its central pivot.
===Chinese===
这一天，萨克森家的年轻人打定主意，要走比以往散步都远的地方。本地旅行指南里提到，远处一座山——红岭的岭顶——有史前立石和一座带墓室的古冢，他们便把那里定作了此行的麦加。
这一路走得不近，好在凉爽提神的空气给他们添了劲，他们终于坐下来，在通往山顶的小路旁用午餐。两个男孩本想一口气登顶、中途不停，姑娘们却罢起工来，非要坚持歇一歇，再作最后一段攀登。
“可惜老妈不在这儿！”英格丽德说出了全家共同的心声——这个家向来少不了它的轴心。

===Original===
“Yes, but it would have been too great a trapse for her, poor darling!” qualified Quenrede. “I don’t see how we could get her all this way unless we hired a pony.”
“Or borrowed an aeroplane. One seems about as possible as the other,” grunted Ingred.
“She shall have a photo of the stones at any rate,” said Hereward, fingering his camera. “Hurry up and finish, you girls, or the light will be gone!”
“Well, we can’t bolt our sandwiches at the rate you do! I wonder you don’t choke!”
===Chinese===
“话是这么说，可让她走这一趟也太折腾了，可怜的宝贝！”奎恩丽德补上一句，“我看除非雇一匹小马，不然没法把她一路带到这里来。”
“要么就借一架飞机。反正两样看着都一样办不成。”英格丽德咕哝道。
“不管怎样，石头的照片她总归会有一张的。”赫里沃德摆弄着相机说，“姑娘们，快点儿吃完，不然光线就要没啦！”
“哼，我们可没法照你们那个速度把三明治囫囵吞下去！真奇怪你们怎么不噎着！”

===Original===
The old gray stones stood in a circle on the top of the hill, from whence they had possibly seen four thousand summers and winters pass by. Whether their original purpose was temple, astronomical observatory, or both is one of the riddles of antiquarian research, for neolithic man left no record of his doings beyond the weapons buried with him in his barrow. Legend, however, like a busy gossip, had stepped in and supplied points upon which history was silent. Traditions of the neighborhood explained the menhirs as twelve giants turned into stone by the magic powers of good King Arthur, who, in defiance of the claims of the isle of Avalon, was supposed to be buried in a hitherto unexplored chamber of the large green mound that stood near. Sometimes, so the story ran, the giants whispered to one another, and anyone who came there alone at daybreak on May morning might glean much useful information regarding the personal appearance of his or her future lover. As it was obviously difficult to reach so out-of-the-way a spot at such a very early hour, the oracles were seldom consulted at the one and only moment when they were supposed to whisper. There were reputed, however, to be other and easier means of gleaning knowledge from them. Ingred, who had been priming herself with local lore, confided details of the occult ceremonial to Quenrede.
“It sounds rather thrillsome!” admitted that damsel doubtfully. “I’d really like to try it, only the boys would tease me to death. You know what they are!”
“They’re going over there to photograph the cromlech. You’d have time before they come back.”
===Chinese===
古老的灰色石头在山顶站成一圈，从那里，它们或许已眼看四千个寒来暑往流逝而去。它们当初究竟是神庙，是观星台，还是兼而有之，至今仍是古物研究中一桩解不开的谜，因为新石器时代的人除了随葬在古冢里的兵器，不曾留下任何记载自己行迹的记录。不过传说这闲不住的长舌妇早已插了进来，把历史闭口不言的种种关节一一补足。乡间的传闻说，这些立石原是十二个巨人，被贤明的亚瑟王（King Arthur）的法力点化成了石头；而尽管阿瓦隆岛（Avalon）一口咬定自己才是王的长眠之地，据信亚瑟王其实就葬在近旁那座绿色大丘里一间从未探明的墓室中。据说，这些巨人有时会彼此窃窃低语，谁若能在五朔节的清晨天方破晓时独自来到这里，便能探得不少有用的消息，知道自己未来的爱侣是何等模样。然而要在那样早的时辰赶到这般偏僻的去处，明摆着难上加难，于是这些神谕极少能在它们据信开口低语的唯一时辰里被人讨教。不过据说另有别的、也更省事的法子可以从它们那里探得底细。英格丽德早把本地掌故装了一肚子，便把这套神秘仪式的细节悄悄授给了奎恩丽德。
“听着倒怪刺激的！”那位小姐将信将疑地承认，“我还真想试一试，只是让那两个男孩知道了，非笑话死我不可。你知道他们那副德行！”
“他们到那头拍石棚去了。趁他们回来之前，你的工夫尽够。”

===Original===
“Shall I?”
“Go on!”
“Tell me again what to do.”
“You let your hair down, and walk bareheaded in and out and in and out round all the circle of stones. Then you put an offering of flowers on that biggest stone﻿—the Giant King, he’s called﻿—and throw a pebble into the little pool below. You count the bubbles that come up﻿—one for *A*, two for *B*, etc.﻿—and they’ll give you the initial of your future lover. With *very* great luck, you might see his shadow in the pool, but that does not often happen.”
“I don’t believe in it, of course, but I’ll try for fun! The Giant King won’t get much in the way of a bouquet today!”
===Chinese===
“我去试试？”
“去呀！”
“再跟我说一遍该怎么个做法。”
“你把头发放下来，光着头，沿着那一圈石头里外穿绕、里外穿绕地走。然后在那块最大的石头上——人家管它叫‘巨人王’——供上一束花，再朝下面的小水潭里丢一颗石子。数浮上来的泡泡——一个泡算*A*，两个泡算*B*，依此类推——泡泡就会告诉你未来爱人的名字首字母。要是运气*特别*好，你还兴许能看见他映在潭里的影子，不过那样的事可不多见。”
“我自然不信这个，不过图个乐子，试试就试试！今天‘巨人王’可等不来什么像样的花束啦！”

===Original===
Quenrede, protesting her scepticism, but all the same palpably enjoying the magic experiment, picked an indifferent nosegay of the few buttercups, hawkweeds, and late pieces of scabious which were the only flowers available. Then she removed her hairpins, and, letting down a shower of flaxen hair, commenced her winding pilgrimage among the old gray stones. There is a vein of superstition in the most modern of minds, and she was probably following a custom that had come down the ages from the days when our primitive ancestresses clothed themselves in skins and twisted their prehistoric locks with pins of mammoth ivory. In and out and in and out, with Ingred, like an attendant priestess, behind her, she performed the necessary itinerary, and laid her floral offering upon what may have been the remains of a neolithic altar. The pool below was dark and boggy and brown with peat. She took a good-sized pebble, and flung it into the middle with a terrific splash. Ingred, giggling nervously, counted the bubbles.
“*A*, *B*, *C*, *D*, *E*, *F*, *G*, *H*, *I*﻿—It’s ‘*I*,’ Queenie! No, there’s another! It’s ‘*J*’! It’s going to be ‘*J*,’ old sport! Aren’t you thrilled? Oh, I say! Whoever on earth is that?”
Following the direction of her sister’s eyes, Quenrede looked through a veil of windblown hair, to see, standing among the stones, a stranger of the opposite sex, garbed in tweed knickers and leather gaiters. One glance was enough. The next second she turned, and beat a hurried and ignominious retreat to the sheltered side of the green mound. Ingred, panting in the rear, followed her to cover.
Quenrede, very pink in the face, sat down on a clump of heather and immediately began to put up her hair.
===Chinese===
奎恩丽德口口声声自称不信，那份乐在其中却明明白白写在身上；她用仅有的几样花——毛茛、山柳菊，还有几枝迟开的山萝卜——马马虎虎凑成一束小花。随后她拔下发夹，放下一头亚麻色的如瀑长发，开始在古老的灰石之间绕行她的朝圣之路。再新派的头脑里也藏着一条迷信的细脉，她此刻遵循的，多半便是一条从太古一路传下来的旧俗——想当年，我们远古的老祖母们身披兽皮，用猛犸象牙簪子绾她们的史前发鬓。里外穿绕，里外穿绕，她身后跟着英格丽德，活像一位随侍的女司祭；她走完必经的路程，把那束花供上了也许正是新石器时代祭坛残迹的石头。下面的水潭又黑又泞，被泥炭染成褐色。她挑了颗够分量的石子，朝潭心用力一掷，溅起老大一片水花。英格丽德紧张得咯咯直笑，数着泡泡。
“*A*、*B*、*C*、*D*、*E*、*F*、*G*、*H*、*I*——是‘*I*’，奎妮！不对，又冒出一个！是‘*J*’！准是‘*J*’了，老伙计！你激不激动？哎呀，快瞧！那到底是何方神圣？”
顺着妹妹的目光望去，奎恩丽德透过被风吹乱的发帘，看见石头中间立着个异性陌生人，穿一条斜纹呢马裤，打着皮绑腿。只这一眼便够了。下一秒她转过身，急急地、狼狈不堪地撤到绿丘背风的一面。英格丽德气喘吁吁地跟在后面，一同躲了起来。
奎恩丽德满脸绯红，在一丛石楠上坐下，立刻动手把头发挽起来。

===Original===
“I never felt such an idiot in my life!” she confided with energy to her sympathetic audience of one. “Ingred! That man knew what I was doing! I saw the horrid amusement in his face. He was laughing at me for all he was worth. I *know* he was!”
At eighteen it is an overwhelming matter to be laughed at. Quenrede’s newly-developed dignity was decidedly wounded.
“After all, it was a very schoolgirlish thing to do,” she remarked, sticking in hairpins as well as she could without a mirror. “Do you think he’s still there? I shall stop here till he marches off.”
“I’ll go and prospect,” said Ingred.
===Chinese===
“我这辈子从没觉得自己这么傻过！”她激愤地向她那位唯一的同情听众倾诉，“英格丽德！那男人知道我在干什么！我看见他脸上那副可恶的笑意。他分明在拼命笑话我。我*就知道*他在笑！”
十八岁上，被人笑话可是件塌天大事。奎恩丽德新近长成的那份尊严，分明是受了重创。
“说到底，这本是女学生才干得出来的事。”她一边对着没有镜子的难处尽力插着发夹，一边说，“你看他还在那儿吗？他不开拔，我就一直待在这儿。”
“我去探探。”英格丽德说。

===Original===
She came back with the bad news that not only was the stranger still there, but he was actually in close and apparently familiar conversation with Athelstane and Hereward, who were calling loudly for their sisters, and to confirm her words came distant jodellings of:
“Ingred!”
“Queenie!”
“Where are you girls?”
There was nothing for it but to come forth from their retreat. It was impossible to stay hidden forever. Quenrede issued as nonchalantly as she could, with her hair tucked under her tam-o’-shanter, and her gloves on. She bowed instead of shaking hands when Athelstane introduced Mr. Broughten, a fellow-student of his college; it seemed a more grownup and superior attitude to adopt. She thought his eyes twinkled, but she preserved such an air of standoff dignity that he promptly suppressed any undue inclinations towards mirth, and stood looking the epitome of grave politeness.
===Chinese===
她带回来的消息不妙：那陌生人不但还在，而且正同阿特尔斯坦和赫里沃德凑作一团，看样子聊得还挺熟络；两位哥哥正扯着嗓子找妹妹们。印证她这话的，是远处传来的一声声拖长调子的呼喊：
“英格丽德！”
“奎妮！”
“你们两个姑娘在哪儿呀？”
没奈何，只好从藏身处出来，总不能永远躲着不见人。奎恩丽德竭力装出满不在乎的样子走了出来，头发掖进那顶苏格兰圆呢帽里，手套也戴好了。阿特尔斯坦引见布劳顿先生——他学院里的一位同学——时，她没有伸手，而是躬身行了个礼；在她看来，这样姿态更老成，也更胜一筹。她觉得他眼里闪过一丝笑意，可她端出那副拒人千里的庄重架子，他也就赶紧收了任何失礼的笑念，站在那里，俨然一副郑重其事的礼貌典范。

===Original===
“Broughten knows all about the old barrow,” Athelstane explained. “He’s got a candle with him﻿—we were duds not to bring one ourselves﻿—and he’s going to act showman. Come along!”
The entrance into the mound was through a low doorway with lintel and posts of unhewn stone. Inside was a kind of central hall with three rudely-constructed chambers leading out of it. A pile of rough stones in front seemed to point to further chambers.
“That part’s never been explored yet,” said Mr. Broughten. “Some of us want to tackle it some day, if we can get permission, but it’s a big job. You don’t want to bring the barrow down on your head, and be buried in the ruins! I never think the roof looks too secure,” he added easily, poking at the stones above with his stick.
===Chinese===
“布劳顿对这古冢了如指掌。”阿特尔斯坦解释道，“他还随身带了一支蜡烛——我们自己竟没想到带，真是一对傻瓜——他还打算客串一回讲解员。来吧！”
进冢的门道低矮，门楣与门柱都是未经雕凿的粗石。里面是一间类似正厅的屋子，三个粗粗砌就的墓室从厅里分出去。前面一堆乱石，似乎暗示着更深处另有墓室。
“那一部分还从来没探过呢。”布劳顿先生说，“我们中间有几个人想着哪天来干一场，要是能求得许可的话——不过这是个大工程。谁也不想叫古冢塌下来砸在头上、活埋在瓦砾堆里吧！我总觉得这洞顶不见得牢靠。”他又轻描淡写地补了一句，拿手杖捅了捅头顶的石块。

===Original===
The girls, aghast at the notion of a possible subsidence, made a hasty exit to the open air, and hovered near the entrance in much agitation of mind till the rest of the party made a safe reappearance. Their conductor, with a side glance at the bunch of flowers﻿—which Quenrede ignored﻿—made some reference to the Giant King stone and his whispering companions: he was evidently well versed in all old traditions, though he refrained from mentioning local practices. He walked part of the way home with the Saxons before he branched off to the place where he had left his bicycle.
“You look *nice*﻿—you do, *really*, with your hair down,” said Ingred to Quenrede that night, as the latter sat wielding her hairbrush at bedtime. “And you needn’t be afraid anybody would mistake you for a flapper. Why, Harry Scampton actually asked Hereward the other day if you were married! By the by,” she added wickedly, “do you know I’ve ascertained that Mr. Broughten’s Christian name begins with ‘*J*.’ Whether ‘John’ or ‘James’ I can’t say!”
“I don’t care if it’s Jehosaphat!” snorted Queenie. “I’ve told you already he doesn’t interest me in the least!”
===Chinese===
两位姑娘一想到头顶说不定会塌，吓得魂飞魄散，慌忙退到露天里，心惊肉跳地在入口近旁打转，直到其余几位平安地重新露面。她们的向导眼角瞟了瞟那束花——奎恩丽德只装没瞧见——又提起巨人王石和它那些低语的伙伴：他对种种古老的传闻显然烂熟于心，只是绝口不提本地的那些做法。他陪萨克森家的几个走了一程，才拐向他寄放自行车的地方。
“你披着头发*好看*——真的，*真的*好看。”那天夜里，奎恩丽德坐在那儿抡着发刷梳睡前的头发，英格丽德对她说，“你也大可不必担心谁会把你错当成没长大的毛头丫头。嘿，前几天哈里·斯坎普顿（Harry Scampton）还当真问赫里沃德，你结了婚没有呢！对了，”她促狭地添了一句，“你可知道，我已经查明布劳顿先生的教名开头是‘*J*’。至于是‘约翰’（John）还是‘詹姆斯’（James），我可说不好！”
“就算他叫约沙法（Jehosaphat），我也懒得管！”奎妮哼了一声，“我早就告诉过你，他一点儿也引不起我的兴趣！”

## XX Under the Lanterns / 灯笼之下

===Original===
It is an ill wind that blows nobody good, and though Nora, Fil, Ingred, and Verity might chafe at being debarred from tennis for a whole week, their adventure in the garden had given them an idea. How it exactly originated could not be decided, for each fiercely claimed the full credit for it. Its evolution, however, was somewhat as follows:
1. Stage 1. How lovely the garden looked in the evening.
2. Stage 2. Why should we not *all* enjoy it some time?
3. Stage 3. Miss Burd evidently does.
4. Stage 4. And looked very fascinating in her white dressing-gown.
5. Stage 5. It was exactly like a fancy dress.
===Chinese===
俗话说，天下的坏事未必人人都遭殃。诺拉、菲尔、英格丽德和薇丽蒂虽然为被禁打整整一星期网球而憋闷，可她们在花园里的那番奇遇，倒给了她们一个点子。这点子究竟起于谁，实在没法断定，因为四个人都咬定功劳全归自己。不过它的演变过程，大致如下：
1. 第一阶段：傍晚的花园多么可爱。
2. 第二阶段：为什么不能找个时候让*大家*都来享受一番呢？
3. 第三阶段：伯德小姐分明就在享受。
4. 第四阶段：而且她穿着白色晨衣的模样，煞是迷人。
5. 第五阶段：那简直就是一件现成的化装服。

===Original===
6. Stage 6. Why should not we all wear fancy dress?
7. Stage 7. *Let us ask Miss Burd to let the hostel have a fancy-dress dance in the school garden.*
Great minds generally think in company, and often hit upon the same invention at the same moment, so perhaps all four girls had an equal share in the brainwave. They communicated it cautiously to companions, and as it “caught on” they sounded Mrs. Best, and finding her favorably disposed to the scheme, begged her to intercede for them with Miss Burd. The headmistress was wonderfully gracious about the matter, gave full permission for the dance, promised to be present herself, and allowed the invitation to be extended to any mistresses and seniors who would care to join the party. It was quite a long time since the hostel had had any particularly exciting doings, so that the girls flung themselves into their preparation with much enthusiasm. Those who were lucky enough already to possess fancy costumes, or who were able to borrow them, of course scored, and the rest set to work to manufacture anything that came to hand. It was to be in the nature of an impromptu affair, but a few days’ notice was given, and the girls were able to devote a Saturday to the all-absorbing problem. Ingred, home for the weekend, enlisted the help of Mother and Quenrede, and turned the bungalow almost upside down in her quest for suitable accessories. She thought of a number of characters she would have liked to impersonate, but was always balked by the lack of some vital article of dress.
“It’s no use!” she lamented. “I can’t be ‘Joan of Arc’ without a suit of armor, or ‘Queen Elizabeth’ when I haven’t a flowered velvet robe! I’m so tired of all the old things! It’s too stale to twist some roses in my hair for ‘Summer,’ and I’ve been a gipsy so often that everybody knows my red handkerchief and gilt beads. I’d as soon be a Red Indian squaw!”
“And why shouldn’t you be?” asked Quenrede. “It’s a remarkably pretty costume.”
===Chinese===
6. 第六阶段：为什么我们不能人人穿上化装服呢？
7. 第七阶段：*去求伯德小姐答应，让寄宿舍在学校花园里开一场化装舞会吧。*
聪明的脑袋往往爱往一处想，又常在同一刻撞出同一个点子，所以这灵光一闪，多半四位姑娘人各有一份功劳。她们小心翼翼地把它透给同伴，见它渐渐“叫响”，便去探贝斯特太太的口风，发现她颇为赞成这个计划，就恳求她代为向伯德小姐说情。女校长对这件事宽和得出奇：不仅完全准许开这场舞会，答应亲自出席，还允诺把邀请扩及愿意来参加的任何女教师和高年级学生。寄宿舍已经好长时间没办过什么特别热闹的事儿了，姑娘们于是个个兴冲冲地投入准备。运气好、手头本就有化装服或者借得到的人自然占了先，其余的便动手就地取材，有什么做什么。这场舞会本意是即兴小聚，不过还是提前几天打了招呼，姑娘们得以把整整一个星期六，都花在这件叫人全神贯注的大事上。英格丽德周末回家，拉来母亲和奎恩丽德帮忙，为了搜罗合适的行头，差点把“小平房”翻了个底朝天。她想过一连串想扮的人物，却总因为缺了某件要紧的衣饰而作罢。
“没辙了！”她哀叹道，“没有一身盔甲，我扮不了‘圣女贞德（Joan of Arc）’；没有一件绣花天鹅绒长袍，也扮不成‘伊丽莎白女王（Queen Elizabeth）’！这些老一套我都腻透了！往头发里绞几朵玫瑰扮‘夏天’，早就俗不可耐；吉普赛人我又扮了不知多少回，谁都认得我那块红头巾和那串镀金珠子。我还不如干脆扮个印第安女人呢！”
“那有什么不可以的？”奎恩丽德问，“那可是套顶漂亮的行头。”

===Original===
“Oh, I dare say, if I could beg, borrow, or steal it!”
“You’ve no need to do either, my dear. I’ve had a brainwave, and we’ll fix it up for you at home. Yes, I mean it! Allow me to introduce myself: ‘Miss Quenrede Saxon, Court Costumier. The very latest theatrical productions.’ I’ll make you look so that your own mother will hardly know you!”
“I’d like to puzzle them!” rejoiced Ingred. “Miss Burd said she should have a parade, and hinted something about a prize. They always give points to whoever has the best disguise. Masks are barred, but we may paint our faces. I think I shall be rather choice as a squaw!”
“You ought to have me with you as your ‘brave’!” chuckled Hereward.
“It’s a ‘Ladies Only’ dance, so you can’t be invited, my boy! There won’t be a solitary masculine individual present﻿—even the gardener will have gone home.”
“You bet folks will peep in!”
“No, they won’t. The premises are strictly private.”
===Chinese===
“哦，要是能求来、借来、偷来，倒也未尝不可！”
“亲爱的，哪一样都用不着。我灵机一动有了主意，咱们在家里就能给你置办齐整。真的，我说到做到！请允许我自我介绍：‘奎恩丽德·萨克森小姐，宫廷服装师，专营最新式戏剧行头。’保准把你打扮得连亲妈都认不出你来！”
“我就爱让他们猜不透！”英格丽德喜滋滋地说，“伯德小姐说要搞一场化装巡游，还暗示了什么奖品。一向是谁伪装得最妙，就给谁记高分。面具不许戴，可脸可以画。我扮成印第安女人，保管够出挑！”
“真该让我扮你的‘勇士’陪着你！”赫里沃德哧哧笑道。
“这是场‘女士专场’舞会，你可不在受邀之列，小子！到时候连一个男的都见不着——连园丁都回家了。”
“等着瞧吧，保准有人扒着门缝偷看！”
“不会的。那地方可是私家地盘，闲人免进。”

===Original===
Quenrede was in some respects a clever and ingenious little person. She was not much good at ordinary dressmaking, where fashion must be followed, but she displayed great originality in her construction of Ingred’s fancy costume. There were two clean sacks in the house, and she commandeered them. She cut one into a skirt and the other into a jumper, stitched up the sides, and frayed out the bottoms to represent fringes. Then she took her watercolor paints, mixed them with Chinese white to form a strong body color, and painted Indian patterns on both garments. The headdress she considered a triumph. She went to a neighboring poultry farm, and boldly begged the tail feathers which had been plucked the day before from some game fowls. These she glued round a cardboard crown, and the effect was magnificent. A dress rehearsal was held, and the family rejoiced over Ingred’s most decidedly Wild West appearance.
“You have a pair of real moccasins that Uncle Ernest sent you for bedroom slippers. I’ll cut some strips of cloth into fringe for leggings, and you can wear Athelstane’s leather belt, and carry an axe for a tomahawk,” said Quenrede, surveying her work with critical satisfaction. “Don’t forget to paint your face!”
“I shan’t show anyone my costume beforehand,” chuckled Ingred. “I really don’t believe anyone will know me! What luck if I won a prize for the best disguise!”
“Bet you anything you like you don’t!” murmured Hereward.
“Why shouldn’t I?”
“Because there may be others even better!”
===Chinese===
奎恩丽德在某些方面真是个机灵巧思的小家伙。寻常的裁缝活计得跟着时尚亦步亦趋，她并不在行，可给英格丽德缝制这套化装服，她却显出了了不起的独创力。家里有两只干净的麻袋，被她征用了：一只裁成裙子，一只裁成套衫，侧边缝拢，底边抽散成流苏穗子。接着她取来水彩颜料，掺上锌白调出浓重的覆盖色，在两件衣裳上画满印第安花纹。那顶头饰，她自认为是得意之作：她跑到邻近的家禽农场，鼓起勇气讨来头一天刚从几只斗鸡身上拔下的尾羽，把它们粘在硬纸板做的冠上，效果蔚为壮观。彩排了一场，全家人看着英格丽德那副不折不扣的西部蛮荒打扮，皆大欢喜。
“你不是有一双欧内斯特（Ernest）叔叔送的真鹿皮软鞋当卧室拖鞋吗？我再裁几条布做成流苏绑腿，你系上阿特尔斯坦的皮带，再拿一把斧头充作印第安战斧。”奎恩丽德一边以行家的挑剔眼光满意地打量自己的作品，一边说，“可别忘了往脸上抹颜色！”
“我的行头谁也不提前给看。”英格丽德笑嘻嘻地说，“我真不信有人认得出我！要是能得个最佳化装奖，那多走运！”
“随你赌什么，你准赢不了！”赫里沃德咕哝道。
“我怎么就赢不了？”
“因为保不齐还有化得更绝的呢！”

===Original===
“Well, of course, that’s for Miss Burd to judge! But I think I’ve a sporting chance, at any rate!”
The dance was to be held on Monday evening after supper, when it was just beginning to grow dusk. The mistresses had taken the matter up quite enthusiastically, and had stretched some wires across the garden, and hung up Chinese lanterns. The hostel piano had been pulled close to the window, so that the strains of music could float out into the garden. At least fifteen seniors had accepted the invitation, and it was rumored that Miss Burd had invited a few private friends. Supper was held earlier than usual, so as to allow time for the all-important operation of dressing, and the moment it was finished every inmate of the hostel fled to her bedroom. Dormitory 2 was naturally a scene of much confusion. The girls tried to put on their own costumes and help each other at the same time. Fil, as a Dresden China Shepherdess, needed much assistance in the settling of her panniers, and the arrangement of her curls, which by special permission from Mrs. Best had been twisted up in curl papers from four o’clock until the last available moment, and came out, much to Fil’s satisfaction, in quite creditable ringlets. The effect was so altogether charming that her roommates called a general halt for admiration.
“You look like a mixture of Dolly Varden and Sweet Lavender, with a dash of Maid Marian thrown in,” decided Verity.
“I hope my hair’ll keep in curl! There’s rather a damp feeling in the air,” fluttered Fil anxiously.
“You could fly indoors, and give it a twist with the tongs, if it gets very limp,” suggested Nora.
===Chinese===
“唔，那当然得由伯德小姐来评判！不过我想，至少我还是有几分胜算的！”
舞会定在星期一晚饭之后、天刚擦黑的时分。女教师们对这件事热心得很，在花园里拉起几道铁丝，挂上了中国灯笼。寄宿舍的钢琴挪到了窗边，好让琴声悠悠飘进花园。至少有十五名高年级学生接受了邀请，还传闻伯德小姐请了几位私人朋友。晚饭比平常开得早，好腾出工夫料理梳妆这桩头等大事；饭一吃完，宿舍里的每一个人便一窝蜂逃回自己的寝室。二号寝室自然乱作一团。姑娘们既要往自己身上套行头，又要腾出手互相帮忙。菲尔扮德累斯顿（Dresden）瓷牧羊女，安置裙撑、整理发卷，处处需要人搭手——经贝斯特太太特别恩准，她的头发从四点钟起就用卷发纸卷着，一直卷到不能再拖的最后一刻才拆，拆出来相当体面的一圈圈小卷，让菲尔心满意足。那效果实在楚楚动人，室友们不由得齐齐停下手里的活计，好生赞叹了一番。
“你看上去像多莉·瓦尔登（Dolly Varden）掺了‘甜薰衣草’（Sweet Lavender），再添上一撮玛丽安小姐（Maid Marian）。”薇丽蒂下了断语。
“但愿我的头发能卷得住！空气里潮乎乎的。”菲尔急得心里直扑腾。
“要是塌得厉害，你可以飞奔回屋里，用烫发钳再卷一卷。”诺拉出主意道。

===Original===
Nora herself was going as a personification of “The Kitchen.” Her skirt was draped with dusters and dishcloths, she wore a small dish-cover as a hat, clothes-pegs were suspended round her neck as a necklace, and she brandished a rolling-pin in her hand.
“I’m bound to be something comic,” she assured the others. “I’d never keep my face straight for a romantic character. I could no more live up to Lady Jane Grey than I could fly! She’s above me altogether!”
Verity, who had borrowed a Dutch costume slightly too small for her, was trying to squeeze her proportions into the tight velvet bodice, and looked dubiously at the sabots.
“I’ll never be able to dance in those!” she decided. “I’ll put them on to start with, and then kick them off and slip on my sandals instead. They’re the most extraordinary clumpy things in the world, I feel like a cat walking in walnut shells!”
Ingred’s toilet progressed very favorably till it came to the stage of coloring her face. She was not quite sure as to the best means of obtaining a Red Indian complexion. First she tried rubbing it with soil from the garden, but that was a painful process which almost scraped the skin from her cheeks. So she washed her face and used cocoa. She mixed it in a cup and dabbed it over, but it would not go on smoothly, and the result was so patchy and hideous that once more she brought out her sponge and wiped it off. At that point Verity came to the rescue, smeared the poor cheeks (already sore through such ill-treatment) with vanishing cream, then powdered on some dry cocoa, which certainly gave a dusky and non-European aspect to her features, especially when combined with the feather headdress. Her dark hair, plaited in two long tails, completed the illusion. The girls held a complacent review of their toilets, then walked downstairs with caution, for Nora’s dish-cover was difficult to balance as a hat, and Verity’s heels kept slipping out of the sabots. Fil’s ringlets, alas! were already beginning to untwist, and Ingred’s jumper, put on in too big a hurry, showed symptoms of splitting down the seam. There was no time for repairs of any sort, however. They were five minutes late, and the rest of the company were assembled on the lawn. The boarders from the hostel, together with mistresses and seniors who had come by invitation, made a total of more than fifty persons, all in fancy dress.
===Chinese===
诺拉自己则要扮“厨房”的化身：裙子上挂满抹布和洗碗布，头顶一只小菜罩充作帽子，脖子上悬一圈衣夹当项链，手里还挥着一根擀面杖。
“我天生只配扮滑稽角色，”她向众人保证，“扮浪漫人物我可绷不住脸。要我够得上简·格雷郡主（Lady Jane Grey），除非我能飞上天！她那种人物，压根儿高出我一等！”
薇丽蒂借来的一身荷兰装略嫌小了些，她正拼命把自己塞进那件紧绷绷的天鹅绒胸衣里，又满腹狐疑地打量着那双木鞋。
“穿这玩意儿根本没法跳舞！”她断定，“先穿着充个门面，跳起来就把它们踢掉，换上我的凉鞋。它们是天下最笨重的东西，我觉得自己活像一只踩着核桃壳走路的猫！”
英格丽德的梳妆一路顺风顺水，到了该给脸上色这一步才犯了难。怎样才能弄出印第安人的肤色，她心里没底。起初她试着用花园里的泥土搓脸，可那滋味实在够呛，险些把面皮都搓下来。于是她洗净脸，改用可可粉：在杯子里调开往脸上抹，可怎么也涂不匀，结果斑斑驳驳、惨不忍睹，只好又拿出海绵擦个干净。幸亏薇丽蒂赶来救场，先给那两片可怜的脸蛋（早被这般折腾搓得生疼）抹上雪花膏，再扑上干可可粉，这才让她的容貌蒙上一层深色，彻底脱去了欧洲气派，配上那顶羽冠尤其如此。她的黑发编成两条长辫，更叫这幻象天衣无缝。姑娘们把自己从头到脚检阅一遍，个个称心，然后小心翼翼地下楼去——诺拉头上那只菜罩难得摆稳，薇丽蒂的脚后跟又不住从木鞋里滑出来。唉，菲尔的小卷发已经开始松散，英格丽德那件套衫也因穿得太急，侧缝有了开裂的征兆。可无论什么都来不及修补了。她们迟到了五分钟，其余的人已经聚齐在草坪上。寄宿舍的住宿生，加上应邀而来的女教师和高年级学生，总数五十有余，人人一身化装。

===Original===
These gay costumes were a pretty sight against the background of trees and bushes and flowerbeds. The sun had set, leaving a yellow glow in the sky, and the Chinese lanterns were beginning to glow in the gathering twilight. It was certainly a varied crowd; all centuries had met together. A Japanese damsel walked arm-in-arm with a Lancashire witch; an Italian peasant hob-a-nobbed with “The Queen of Sheba,” a Spanish lady was talking to “Old Mother Hubbard,” while such characters as “A Medicine Bottle,” or “An Aeroplane” rubbed shoulders with an “Egyptian Princess” or “Dick Whittington’s Cat.”
Miss Burd, garbed appropriately as Chaucer’s Prioress, received the company at the top of the sundial steps, looking, in the opinion of the Foursome League, quite sufficiently like the ghost of yesterday to have justified squeals had they met her alone. When the ceremony of introduction was over, the guests dispersed about the lawn, Miss Perry struck up a waltz on the piano, and the fun began. Dancing on the grass, in the growing darkness, with the Chinese lanterns sending out a soft but uncertain radiance overhead, was a new experience to most of the school. It was difficult not to step on to the flowerbeds, or to brush against the bushes. Trailing garments were decidedly in the way, and came to grief. There was a delirious sort of Eastern feeling about it﻿—a kind of combination of *The Thousand and One Nights* and the *Rubáiyat of Omar Khayyam*. The Abbey tower for once seemed out of place, and ought to have changed miraculously into a pagoda or a minaret.
It was after the girls had been dancing for some little time that Ingred first noticed a couple whom she did not remember to have seen before. They followed persistently in her steps, and even gently bumped into her once or twice, thus compelling her attention. She looked at them, considerably mystified. One was attired in Early Victorian Costume, with a crinoline, a little tippet, and a poke bonnet, from which peeped some bewitching ringlets; the other, in a gorgeous Turkish costume, was enveloped in a shimmering gauze veil.
“Who are those?” Ingred asked her partner.
But Verity could not tell.
In the twilight it was, of course, easy to make mistakes, but Ingred began to have a strong suspicion that neither of the mysterious partners belonged to the school. They were certainly not members of the Fifth or Sixth. Perhaps some of the Juniors had forced themselves in? No, they were too tall for Juniors.
“Perhaps they are ghosts!” shivered Verity.
===Chinese===
这满场缤纷的衣裳，衬着树木、灌丛和花坛，煞是好看。夕阳已沉，天际留下一抹金黄，中国灯笼在渐浓的暮色里次第亮起。这群人确实五花八门，各个世纪济济一堂：日本少女挽着兰开夏（Lancashire）女巫的手臂同行，意大利农家女同“示巴女王（The Queen of Sheba）”亲热攀谈，西班牙贵妇正与“哈伯德老妈妈（Old Mother Hubbard）”寒暄；而“一只药瓶”、“一架飞机”之流的角色，则与“埃及公主”或“迪克·惠廷顿（Dick Whittington）的猫”摩肩接踵。
伯德小姐也应景扮成乔叟（Chaucer）笔下的女修道院院长，在日晷台阶顶上迎候来宾——依四人同盟看来，她那模样活脱脱像昨日现身的那尊幽灵，若是单独撞见，惊叫出声都在情理之中。引见之礼既毕，宾客们散落到草坪各处，佩里（Perry）小姐在钢琴上奏起一支圆舞曲，欢乐就此开场。在渐浓的夜色里踏着青草起舞，头顶上中国灯笼漾出一团柔和而摇曳不定的光晕，对全校大多数人来说都是头一遭的体验。要不一脚踩进花坛、不擦着灌木丛，实在不容易。曳地的裙裾碍事得很，纷纷遭了殃。整场透着一种令人晕眩的东方情调——仿佛《一千零一夜》与《鲁拜集》揉成了一处。修道院的塔楼这一回显得格格不入，真该显个灵，摇身变成宝塔或宣礼塔才对。
姑娘们跳了好一阵之后，英格丽德才头一回留意到一对眼生的身影。他们执拗地紧随她的脚步，甚至还轻轻撞了她一两回，逼得她不能不留神。她望着他们，满心疑惑。一个穿早期维多利亚式服装，撑着克里诺林裙，披一条小肩巾，戴一顶帽檐前伸的旧式软帽，帽檐下探出几绺迷人的小卷发；另一个一身华丽的土耳其装束，裹在一层流光闪烁的薄纱面纱里。
“那两个是谁？”英格丽德问自己的舞伴。
可薇丽蒂也说不上来。
暮色里认错人自然难免，可英格丽德渐渐生出一个强烈的疑心：这对神秘舞伴压根儿就不属于本校。她们肯定不是五年级或六年级的。莫非是低年级学生硬闯进来的？不对，她们个子太高，不像低年级的。
“说不定是幽灵！”薇丽蒂打了个寒噤。

===Original===
“Ghosts don’t bump into people. These are real substantial flesh and blood!”
“It’s so dark, we can hardly see.”
“Well, I vote we keep close to them, and next time we get near a lantern, we’ll turn the tables and bump into them, and try to see who they are.”
It was easier said than done, however; the strangers seemed to have changed their tactics, and instead of pursuing Ingred and Verity now endeavored to avoid them. No “elusive Pimpernels” could have been more difficult to follow. They would come quite close and then suddenly dodge and glide away, only to reappear and repeat the same tantalizing performance. Ingred and Verity began to get on their mettle. It was so evidently done on purpose that they were fully determined to catch the errant pair. After a long game at hide-and-seek they at last managed to dance along side them, and laying violent hands upon them, to drag them into the light of a lantern. As Ingred gazed for a moment in perplexity, the Early Victorian lady gave a most un-Early Victorian wink inside the poke bonnet.
“Hereward! How *dare* you!” gasped his sister.
A firm hand drew her away from the light, and in the shelter of a laurel bush, a voice, choking with laughter, proclaimed:
===Chinese===
“幽灵可不会撞人。这两个是实实在在、有血有肉的活人！”
“天这么黑，咱们几乎什么都看不清。”
“我提议咱们盯紧他们，等下一回凑近灯笼，就反客为主撞他们一下，想法子看看他们到底是谁。”
然而说来容易做来难。那两个陌生人似乎变换了战术，不再尾随英格丽德和薇丽蒂，反倒躲起她们来。只怕连“神出鬼没的‘红花侠’（Pimpernel）”也没这么难盯梢。他们一会儿凑得极近，忽又闪身滑开，转眼又冒出来，重演同一套撩人的把戏。英格丽德和薇丽蒂的倔劲儿被激了起来。这分明是存心逗弄，她们便铁了心要把这对乱窜的家伙逮住。捉了老半天迷藏，她们总算挨着他们跳到一处，下狠手一把揪住，拖到灯笼光下。英格丽德正自怔怔端详，那位维多利亚时代的淑女在旧式软帽底下，极不维多利亚地冲她眨了眨眼。
“赫里沃德！你竟*敢*！”他姐姐倒抽一口气。
一只不容分说的手把她拉离灯光，月桂树丛的遮掩下，一个笑得岔气的声音宣告：

===Original===
“Done you, old girl! Done you brown! What about that bet? I told you you’d never know me!”
“You abominable young wretch,” replied Ingred, laughing in spite of herself. “How *did* you manage it? And who is your friend?”
“Allow me to introduce Vashti, Queen of Persia!”
“Bunkum! It’s a boy! I know it is!”
The explosive sounds issuing from under the shimmering veil of Queen Vashti certainly sounded more masculine than feminine, and that Persian princess confessed presently to the name of Franklin.
“He’s a chum of mine,” explained Hereward, “and he lives close by, so we made it up to come together. His sister lent us the clothes and dressed us. I say, your Prioress never found us out, did she? What about that prize?”
===Chinese===
“骗到你了吧，老姑娘！骗得你彻头彻尾！咱们打的赌怎么说来着？我说过你根本认不出我！”
“你这个可恶的小坏蛋，”英格丽德答道，禁不住笑了，“你*到底*是怎么混进来的？你那位朋友又是谁？”
“请允许我介绍：波斯王后瓦实提（Vashti）！”
“胡扯！是个男孩！我看得出来！”
从瓦实提王后那流光闪烁的面纱底下爆出的笑声，听上去确实更像男声而非女声。这位波斯公主过了一会儿便招认了自己的名字：富兰克林（Franklin）。
“他是我的好哥们儿，”赫里沃德解释道，“就住在附近，所以我们约好了一块儿来。衣服是他姐姐借给我们的，还是她给我们打扮的。我说，你们那位院长一直没瞧出来吧，嗯？那个奖品怎么说？”

===Original===
“There isn’t going to be a prize, and you certainly wouldn’t have deserved it! Look here, you’d better wangle yourselves off before it gets about who you are. *I* should get into a row, not you!”
“Would the Prioress kick up rough?”
“She’d probably think I’d planned the whole business, and encouraged you to come.”
“Even if we apologized?”
“She wouldn’t accept an apology. If you want me to have any tennis next week, you had better clear out.”
===Chinese===
“根本就没有什么奖品，就算有也轮不到你们！听着，趁还没人传开你们是谁，赶紧想法子溜吧。要挨骂的是*我*，可不是你们！”
“那位院长会大发雷霆吗？”
“她多半会以为这整件事都是我策划的，还是我撺掇你们来的。”
“哪怕我们赔个不是？”
“赔不是她也不会接受的。你们要是指望我下星期还能打上网球，就最好赶紧开溜。”

===Original===
“Just a round with you first, and Franklin can take your friend, or vice versa if you prefer it!”
“You impudent boy! Certainly not. I daren’t risk it. Look, Miss Strong is bringing out the lamp, and putting it on the sundial, and I believe Miss Perry is going to take a flashlight photo presently. If you want to disgrace me forever﻿—”
“We’ll go!” sighed a mournful voice. “Though it’s Adam and Eve turned out of Paradise. I say, Franklin, they don’t want us, after all our trouble! We’d better be getting on, I suppose. Our deepest respects to the Prioress. She’s given us a delightful evening, if she only knew it. We’d like to come again some time. Ta-ta!”
===Chinese===
“先跟你跳一圈再走，弗兰克林陪你那位朋友——要是你们更乐意，对调也行！”
“你这个没皮没脸的臭小子！想都别想。我可不敢冒这个险。瞧，斯特朗小姐正把灯搬出来，要放在日晷上，我看佩里小姐马上就要拍闪光照片了。你们要是想让我一辈子丢人现眼——”
“我们这就走！”一个声音哀哀地叹道，“虽说这好比亚当和夏娃被逐出伊甸园。喂，弗兰克林，咱们费了这么大劲，人家还是不要咱们！我看咱们还是动身吧。请向那位女修道院院长转达我们最深的敬意。她要是知道就好了——今晚多亏她，咱们才过得这么愉快。往后什么时候，咱们还想再来。回见啦！”

## III Wynch-on-the-Wold / 三 沃尔德畔温奇

===Original===
Though the College only opened on Tuesday afternoon, the short remainder of the week seemed enormously long to Ingred. Her form mates were the same, but everything else was absolutely changed; she might have been at a new school. She appreciated the convenient arrangements of the handsome building: the lecture-hall, with its stained-glass window and polished floor, the airy classrooms, the studio with its facilities for every kind of art work, the three music-rooms, the laboratory, the gymnasium, and, last but not least, the hostel. Ingred had never before been a boarder, and she had not expected to like the experience, but there is a subtle charm in community life that infects everybody with “the spirit of the hive,” and in spite of herself she began to be interested in the particular set of faces that met round the table for meals. The greater part of the girls were in the middle and lower school, but there were a few members of the Sixth, who sat next to Mrs. Best, the matron, and Nurse Warner, and looked with superior eyes on the crowd of intermediates and juniors. To have secured such congenial roommates was an asset for which she could not be sufficiently thankful. Whatever troubles might await her downstairs, it was a comfort to know that she had three allies ready to flock to her support. She had not known any of them well in the past, but as they seemed prepared to offer their friendship, she also was ready to act the part of chum. By exchanging desks with Linda Slater, she managed to secure a seat next to Verity in school, and entered into an arrangement with her that they should supply the missing gaps in each other’s notes, for Miss Strong often lectured so rapidly that it was impossible to keep up with her.
“I wish I knew shorthand,” grumbled Ingred, comparing scribbles with Verity as the girls tidied their hair for tea. “How anybody’s expected to get down all Miss Strong tells us, I can’t imagine! It’s impossible.”
“I don’t try,” admitted Fil. “At least I do try﻿—I put a bit here and there, but I write so slowly, I’m only halfway through before she’s bounced on to something else, and I’ve missed the beginning of it. I have to stop, too, sometimes, to think how to spell the words.”
The others laughed, for Fil’s spelling was proverbial in the form, and was often of a purely phonetic character. Miss Strong had periodical crusades to improve it, but generally gave them up as a bad job, and recommended constant use of a dictionary instead.
“Though you can’t go about the world with a dictionary perpetually under your arm,” she had remarked on the last occasion. “If you have to write a letter in a hurry, and you begin ‘Dear Maddam’ and end ‘Yours trueley’﻿—well! Please don’t let anybody know you’ve been educated here, that’s all, or it will be a poor advertisement for the College!”
===Chinese===
学院虽然星期二下午才开课，这周剩下的短短几天在英格丽德（Ingred）眼里却漫长得不得了。同班的伙伴还是原来那些人，其余的一切却彻底变了样，恍如换了一所新学校。这栋漂亮楼房的种种便利布置令她赞赏：讲堂里彩绘玻璃窗映着光可鉴人的地板，教室宽敞明亮，工作室里各类美术功课的设施一应俱全，另有三间琴房、实验室、体育馆，还有——同样不可小觑的——寄宿舍。英格丽德从前没做过寄宿生，本来不指望会喜欢这种日子，可集体生活自有一种微妙的魅力，让人人染上“蜂巢精神”，她也不由自主地开始留意起围桌用饭的那几张面孔。寄宿的女生大半来自中年级和低年级，也有几位六年级的，她们挨着舍监贝斯特太太（Mrs. Best）和沃纳护士（Nurse Warner）坐，居高临下地打量着一屋子中小年级的娃娃。能摊上这样投缘的同室室友，实在是她怎么感激都不为过的一桩福气。楼下无论有什么麻烦等着她，一想到自己有三位盟友随时肯聚拢来撑腰，心里便觉安稳。她从前和她们谁都不算深交，可既然人家有意交好，她也乐得拿出密友的架势来。她和琳达·斯莱特（Linda Slater）换了座位，总算在班上挨着薇丽蒂（Verity）坐下了，两人还约定互相补足笔记里的缺漏——因为斯特朗小姐（Miss Strong）讲起课来常常快得叫人根本跟不上。
“真希望我会速记，”姑娘们正梳头准备下楼吃茶点，英格丽德一面跟薇丽蒂对笔记一面抱怨，“谁能把斯特朗小姐讲的统统记下来，我可想象不出！根本不可能。”
“我就不做这个指望，”菲尔（Fil）承认道，“至少也试过——东记一笔西记一笔，可我写得实在太慢，才记到一半，她已经蹦到下一个题目上去了，开头又没听着。有时候我还得停下来，想想单词怎么拼。”
众人都笑了，因为菲尔的拼写全班闻名，而且常常纯凭读音乱写。斯特朗小姐隔三差五就要发起一场“讨伐拼写运动”，可多半以无可救药告终，末了只好劝她时时刻刻带着字典。
“不过你总不能胳膊底下成天夹着一本字典满世界跑，”上回整治到无可奈何时她这么说，“要是你赶时间写信，开头写‘亲爱的马大姆’，落款写‘您的忠十的’——嘿！千万别人家知道你在这儿上过学，就这么一句，不然可要给学院丢尽脸了！”

===Original===
Ingred was not at all delighted to be still in Miss Strong’s form. She only moderately liked this mistress. Undoubtedly Miss Strong was a clever teacher, but sarcasm was one of her favorite weapons of discipline. Some of the girls did not mind it, indeed thought it rather amusing, even when directed against themselves, and enjoyed it hugely when someone else was the victim of the sally. Ingred, however, proud and sensitive, writhed under the attacks of Miss Strong’s sharp tongue, and would often have preferred a punishment to a witticism. As a matter of fact, the mistress rarely gave punishments, and was proud of her ability to control her form without resorting to them. She was short in stature, but made up in spirit for her lack of inches, and would fix her dark eyes on offenders against discipline with the personal magnetism of a circus trainer or a leopard-tamer. Schoolgirls are irreverent beings, and though to her face her pupils showed her all respect, behind her back they spoke of her familiarly as “The Bantam,” in allusion to her small size but plucky disposition, or sometimes, in reference to her sarcastic powers, as “The Sark,” which by general custom became “The Snark.” On the whole Miss Strong’s pithy, racy, humorous style of teaching made her a far greater favorite than mistresses of duller caliber. She had a remarkable faculty for getting work out of the most unwilling brains. Her form always made excellent progress, and she had a reputation for obtaining record successes in examinations. To judge from the first few days of term, she meant to keep up her standard of efficiency. Miss Burd had mapped out a heavy timetable for **Va**, and it was Miss Strong’s business to see that the girls got through it. Of course they grumbled. After the long weeks of the summer holidays it was doubly difficult to apply their minds to lessons, and set to work in the evenings to perform the enormous amount of preparation demanded from them. To some the task was well-nigh impossible, and poor Fil would send in very imperfect exercises, but others, Ingred and Verity among the number, had ambitions, and boosted up the record of the form.
It was after a most strenuous few days that Ingred came to the close of the first week of the new term, and, taking her books and handbag, started off to spend the weekend at home. She left the College with a feeling of intense relief. She had dreaded the return there, and the confession of her altered circumstances. It had not proved quite so disagreeable an ordeal as she had anticipated, for, after the first expressions of surprise, nobody had referred again to Rotherwood; yet Ingred, on the lookout for slights, imagined that she was not treated with as much consideration as formerly. Avis Marlowe and Jess Howard had hardly spoken to her, and, though the omission was probably owing to sheer lack of time or opportunity, she chose to set it down to a desire to show her the cold shoulder.
“Now I have no parties to offer them, they don’t care about me!” she thought bitterly. “They’ll hunt about till they find somebody else who’s likely to act entertainer.”
Fortunately, as Ingred stepped out of the College on that first Friday afternoon, the fresh breeze and the bright September sunshine blew away the cobwebs, and sent her almost dancing down the street. She had a naturally buoyant disposition, and her uppermost thought was: “I’m going home! I’m going home! Hurrah!”
===Chinese===
仍旧编在斯特朗小姐的班里，英格丽德一点儿也高兴不起来。她对这位老师只能说勉强喜欢。无可否认，斯特朗小姐是个教书的好手，可挖苦讥讽是她管教学生时最爱用的武器。有些女生满不在乎，甚至觉得挺有趣，哪怕挖苦到自己头上也不恼；换了别人当靶子，她们更是乐不可支。英格丽德却自尊而敏感，斯特朗小姐那条利嘴每回开火，她都如坐针毡，宁可贵罚也不要一句俏皮话。其实这位老师极少动用惩罚，还颇为自豪，自夸不必借助惩罚也能管住全班。她个子矮小，气势却补足了身高，一双黑眼睛盯住违纪的学生时，颇有马戏团驯兽师乃至驯豹师那股慑人的魔力。女学生向来没大没小，当着面个个恭恭敬敬，背地里却随口管她叫“矮脚鸡”（The Bantam），讥她人小气盛；有时又着眼于她的讽刺功力，管她叫“毒舌怪”（The Sark），叫来叫去，约定俗成便成了“毒舌精”（The Snark）。总的看来，斯特朗小姐授课言简意赅、妙趣横生，比起那些乏味的老师，她得人心得多。她有一手了不起的本事，再不肯用功的脑袋也能被她榨出功课来。她带的班进步总是出色，以考试屡破纪录闻名。从开学头几天来看，她打算把这股高效的势头保持下去。伯德小姐（Miss Burd）给五甲班排了满满一份课程表，而督促姑娘们把它啃下来，正是斯特朗小姐的差事。抱怨自然是免不了的。暑假长长的几个星期一过，再要收心用功，本就加倍艰难，晚上还得坐下来完成堆积如山的预习。有些人简直招架不住，可怜的菲尔交上来的练习总是七零八落；另一些人却有志气——英格丽德和薇丽蒂皆在此列——硬是把全班的成绩顶了上去。
熬过了极其吃力的几天，新学期头一个星期总算到了头，英格丽德收拾起书本和手提包，动身回家过周末。走出学院大门，她如释重负。此前她一直怕回去，怕坦白家境的变故。事实证明这一关远不像她预想的那样难堪——大家惊愕过一阵之后，就再没人提起罗瑟伍德（Rotherwood）；可是英格丽德处处提防着轻慢，总觉得旁人待她不及从前殷勤。艾维斯·马洛（Avis Marlowe）和杰丝·霍华德（Jess Howard）几乎没跟她说过话，虽说这多半只怪没工夫、没机会，她却偏要认定人家是有意给她冷脸。
“如今我请不起她们玩乐了，她们就不把我放在心上了！”她忿忿地想，“她们自会四下寻摸，直到物色到下一个肯出头张罗的人。”
幸好，开学头一个星期五的下午，英格丽德一跨出学院，清新的微风和明丽的九月阳光便吹散了满脑子的愁云，她几乎是一路雀跃着沿街走去。她天性开朗，这会儿心里最要紧的念头只有一个：“回家喽！回家喽！好哇！”

===Original===
The journey was really quite a little business. She had to take a tram to the Waterstoke terminus, then change on to a light electric railway that ran along the roadside for seven miles to Wynch-on-the-Wold. Grovebury, an old town that dated back to medieval times, lay in a deep hollow among a rampart of hills, so that, in whatever direction you left it, you were obliged to climb. The scenery was very beautiful, for trees edged the river, and clothed the slopes till they gave way to the gorse and heather of the wild moorlands. Wynch-on-the-Wold was a hamlet which, since the opening of the electric railway, was just beginning to turn into a suburb of Grovebury. Close to the terminus neat villas had sprung up like mushrooms; there were a few shops and a branch post office, and a brass plate to the effect that Dr. Whittaker had consulting hours twice a week. Tradesmen’s carts drove out constantly, and the electric railway did quite a little business in the conveyance of parcels.
Wynchcote, the house where the Saxons had retired to try their scheme of retrenchment, lay at some little distance beyond the terminus, and might be considered the outpost of the new suburb. It was a small, picturesque modern bungalow; Mr. Saxon had built it as an architectural experiment, intending it for a sort of model country cottage. The tenants who had occupied it during the period of the war had just returned to Scotland, so, as it was vacant, it had seemed a convenient place in which to settle. It was near enough to Grovebury to allow him to attend his office, and far enough away to cut them adrift from old associations. After four and a half years of war work, Mrs. Saxon wanted a complete rest from committees, crèches, canteens, and recreation huts, and would be glad to urge the excuse of distance to those who appealed for her help. Perhaps also she felt that in their straitened circumstances it was wiser to live where they could not enter into social competition with their former acquaintances.
“I just want to be quiet, to attend to my family, and to enjoy the moors and our garden,” she declared. “I believe I’m going to be very happy at Wynchcote.”
Though it was small, the bungalow was admirably planned, and had many advantages.
